BRIGHTENING THE LIVES OF WORKHOUSE INMATES.

The Work of the Brabazon Employment Society.

To give our readers some idea of the excellent work which is being accomplished by the Warrington branch of the Brabazon Employment Society in brightening the lives of inmates of the Workhouse hospital, we print in full the report submitted by the hon. secretary Miss Broadbent, at the annual sale of work held last Thursday week : I have to report an interrupted year of work, as for three months we were unable to visit at the Workhouse owing to sickness. This is very unfortunate as we were anxious to make a special effort this year and do some- thing more to justify our existence. We have already sent several men to Southport for change of air andas our funds were limited, we all felt that consumptive cases should come first, and especially those amongst soldiers. We only send people who are likely to derive real benefit from the change, and= this point of course we follow the doct2r's advice. I must really mention the great kindness of Dr. Hatton of Southport, who helped to make arrangements for us at the Convalescent home, and was very good indeed to the men while they were there. He even gave them pocket money, and in one case an overcoat. There is another branch of our work that we want very muck to develop. When people have done Brabazon work for us and have behaved well while in hospital, we like to give them a little similar work to de at home, and to pay them for doing it in order to help them over the first i few days. When they leave hospital feeling weak after a long illness, with perhaps not a penny in their pockets or a friend in the neighbourhood, it is,of course.difficult for them to make a fresh start in life. We are allowed to bring invalid soldiers in the town within the scope of the Brabazen Society, and I have a small separate grant from the ever-generous Countess of Meath, to buy materials for them. One man very soon learnt how to do bent iron work. We sold a little for him at our last sale, but he is now able to dispose of it himself. Another man we helped for a few weeks and he made a large Smyrna rug. He then became stranger and got regular work and is now doing well. I may mention that I had several most grateful letters from him and two beautiful ostrich feathers from South Africa. Another man made the wooden boxes which you will see. He is too delicate to work out-of-doors, and I have tried in vain to get work for him. He has a pension of a shilling a day, but that is not very much on which to keep himself, his wife and children. I shall be most glad to receive orders for him. Colonel Richardson and the Colonel of the soldier's late regiment most kindly gave me some money for him, to help him through the few weeks till his pension was due. We have received much sympathy from Mrs. Awbery, the new matron, and from Miss Biggin, the superintendent, and many valuable hints about the different patients. rhey are warmly in favour of the work, as indeed every one must be who sees how it cheers and interests the people, often im- proving their health,by giving them something pleasanter to think about than their own troubles and ailments. There is no need to thank my Brabazon workers, they know how grateful I am to them. They are as follows: —Mrs. Adams, Miss Birtles, Miss Bolton, Mrs. Ford, Mrs. Frost, Miss Gandy,Miss Henshall,Miss Fairclough, Miss Johnson, Miss Randall, Miss Roberts,lVliss Wright. Miss Roberts and Miss Johnson still manage the library excellently. They are anxious to have more books and were much disappointed not to receive more responses to the appeal they lately put in the papers. The sale, which was opened by Mrs. Edelsten, a pioneer of the movement in Warrington, realized £23 •

HOW DRAUGHTS EFFECT THE OVEN.

What oven could do its work if for a few moments an icy blast were blowing upon it, the next few a wave of heat, the next a cloud of steam, and so on ? Every woman knows that it would spoil anything put into it. Yet scores of women, and men too, expect the delicate organs of the human body to do unaided, what cost-iron can't do. Digestion, like an oven, needs a steady heat— a heat of about 100 degrees. The liver needs to be at nearly the same heat. Yet wdmen run from a hot kitchen to hang clothes on the line outside; go from before a hot fire in a warm room to make the beds and sweep a cold attic, and then wonder how they have caught a chill and why they have indigestion. To know how these ailments are contracted is not so important as to know that Chas. Forde's bile beans cure them, however they may have been contracted. Mrs. Annie Light, of Court Five, Catharine-street, Aston, says :—"My daughter, Alice Maud, got wet and caught a chill. This reduced her to a weak, helpless state. Her appetite fell off, she was always cold, and seemed altogether 'run down.' We gave her a course of bile beans, and they seemed to tone her up wonderfully. In a short time all traces of her illness left her, and she is now quite well. Indeed, she is in better health now than she has been for a long time." From Roscrea, Ireland, comes a similar tribute to bile beans. Mr. Patrick Maher says:—" While working in a bog I got my feet wet, got a thorough chill, and was laid up for some time. My whole system seemed disordered, and I began to suffer the pains of rheumatism. For four months 'was in bed, and for several weeks I could not bear the bed-clothes to touch my feet or legs, they were so painful. I took doctors' medicines until I *as sick of them. When I tried bile beans I soon felt a difference. By persevering with them I was eventually quite cured, and I have kept well and hearty since." March is the month which tries the system more than any other. Warm days, followed by biting east winds, find out the weak places. Bile beans not only strengthen those weak places but clear the system from disease which has entered thereby, as the above cases show Obtainable from medicine vendors everywhere.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

FRIENDLY MATCH.

LATCHFORD ATHLETIC V. RICHNIOND'S.—PIayed at Latchford in fine weather. From the outset Latchford pressed, and scored five goals in quick succession through H. Shakeshaft (2), Bradshaw (2), and Taylor (1). Richmond's scored from a penalty, and half time arrived with the score :—Latchford. 5 ; Richmond's, 1. On resuming, Richmond's attacked strongly, but the fine defence of T. Dawson and N. Clarke, and the goal- keeper prevented further scoring. Result : Latchford Athletic, 5; Richmond's, 1.

PASSIVE RESISTANCE SALE AT RUNCORN. A sale of passive resisters' goods took place at Runcorn on Monday. Distress warrants had been issued against eleven persons, including the Rev.T. S. Morris, Presbyterian minister; the Rev. H. Targett, Primitive Methodist minister; Mr. R. H. Posnett, ex-chairman of Runcorn District Council; and Mr. J. R Frith,manager of Runcorn Gas Company. The total amount required, including costs, was £lO 25., and for this amount the goods were all bought in.

FAILURES IN ENGLAND AND WALES.—According to "Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the total nu o6er of failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, March 11th, was 189, viz. :—New Bank- ruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazette, 100; deeds of arrangement registered, 89. The res- pective numbers in the corresponding week of last year were : Bankruptcies, 92 ; deeds of arrangement, 81— total 173; being a net increase in bankruptcies of 64, and a net increase in deeds of arrangement-in 1904, to date, of 122.

I FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.;

The modes were never in all the annals oi fashion as accommodating as they now are for there is such infinite variety in the freshest toilettes and newest hats that every woman must find something which will suit her figure, taste, and purse. There are, indeed, no hard and fast rules to restrict all efforts at origin- ality but each and all may dress as they like while, of course, avoiding any display of any tendency towards exaggeration. Our skirts may vary in length and style to suit the requirements of the wearer and thus the slender woman may appear successfully gowned in a skirt of the early Victorian era, not too long and standing well out from the feet, its roundness of outline emphasised by circular trimmings in the form of tucks or bands which run round the lower part from the knee downwards. This is not, however, a mode suited to the figure of a stout woman, whose outline it too clearly defines, and she should select instead the full flowing skirt which falls in gathers from the waist and flows softly round the feet in graceful folds adding height and distinction to the wearer. A simple, but very neat walking costume is iepicted in my sketch. The material used is a brown smooth-surfaced cloth and the cape s trimmed with sable although there are 3everal other less expensive furs which might be substituted to lessen the expense. How- an old fur cape or necklet might be used, the best parts being cut into bands forming the edging to the cape. It requires some experience and great care to join the fur neat- • and nowadays one can buy very good .mitations of good furs at such reasonable prices that the trouble of using up an old skin .s barely repaid. The skirt springs well out from the feet and is cut to clear the ground by a few inches all round, and the blouse bodice aid sleeves are of the latest mode and most becoming to the slender figure of the ur=rer. A SMART BLOUSBJ

•velvet- and cloth is swathed round • with a loose gauze veil which is tied at the back, and the ends brought round to the front and tied in a soft knot under the chin. With the veil worn down this would make a very smart motoring dress. There are some very smart soft leather belts worn which make a nice finishing touch to a toilette of this kind and fit the waist closely in a truly admirable manner. •

_ The smart blouse given in my illustration is -made of navy blue taffetas trimmed with bands of black velvet bordered with very narrow gold braid finished by gold buttons at the pointed ends of the strappings and down the centre of the front panel. The design is a very simple one and yet exceedingly modish, and the blouse was worn with a skirt of soft blue cloth of the same shade, so that the toilette formed a harmonious scheme of colouring. Fashion most decidedly insists that when feasible the bodice and skirt shall match as nearly as possible, and that the vivid contrasts of a couple of seasons ago shall be considered demode. A MOOISH TOILETTE.

In this way a white blouse worn with a black skirt can have some narrow bebe ribbon interthreaded through the embroidery or lace with which it is trimmed. In many ways this uniformity of colouring can be maintained and I once more offer what I consider a most valuable suggestion for the benefit of those with a very limited dress allowance who seek to follow the fashions as closely as their means permit. Namely, to select what they con- sider the most becoming colour to themselves and the best suited to their circumstances in life and have both blouses, skirts, and hats of the same colour though the shades may vary. Sometimes a couple of colours can be worked together and blue and green or black and violet are perhaps the most successful. It is wonderful what good results can be achieved if the tones are carefully selected. The modish toilette whose charms are displayed in my last sketch would look nice expressed in a light tan coloured cloth with the front of white suede or undressed leather fastened with gold or ornamentlal buttons. A good cloth of the style which closely resembles suede could he substituted if preferred, while the cape is trimmed with twists and tassels of light brown and gold. The toque is of cream cloth bordered with fur, a lace veil smoothed over the oloth fall. ing in a cache-peigne at the back.

TO WIN A WIFE.

BY THOMAS SAUNDERS.]

Geoffrey Falconer and Maude Stuart were sitting out a dance at Lady Allday's first ball of the season.

They were not enscOnced in the orthodox conservatory where the broad, leaved palm cast shadows in which they were bid ' • or where the fragrance of oriental blcs-oms caused love to bubble up into their hearts unb' dden.

No; they had wandered along the corridors of the huge mansion which Lady Allday called home, and un- conscioudy found themselves in a secluded corner where no one else would dream of coming. It was a corner, too, which Lady Allday would have strictly forbidden them to enter had she known of their whereabouts. for she had screened it off, :and now re- posed in it,all the different make-ups she used when herself tab ing part in amateur theatricals. "Maude," said Geoffrey, "how is it you always begin to stop me when I begin to talk of our future prospects. You know I love you, for I have told you many a time; and people look upon our engagement as a settled thing. Yet we both know that there is no engagement at all be wean us. How is it, Maude ?"

Such a sceech betrayed the fact that these two young people were on very intimate terms ; and, indeed, as Geoffrey had said, people looked upon them as actually engaged. "Wall, Geoff," Maude at length said, "I will repeat what I hive told you many a time. You know !perfectly well that I am a woman of ideals: One of "my ideals is that a man to claim truly his designation as a man, must do something to prove his claim. You know Geoff, that I will not marry a man who has never done anything to prove his worth. What a horrible thing it would be, if, at any future time, I was placed in a dangerous position, out of which my husband had not the courag6 to extricate me." "But, Maude," said Geoff, "I have never had the opportunity of showing von my courage. It is useless' for me to say that I would die for you, and all the rest of it. if I have not the chance of demonstroting it." "Yes, I know that; and it is not very often that a girl is placed in a position to be rescued. All the same I slould appreciate your courage if I saw it exercised 'on belialf of another.'"

"Bntif nothing should happen for a long time, in which.' could display resource or bravery, would you still keep-me waiting for my answer" ?-said Geoff. "Yes, 1 would. I have set up a standard from which I will not deviate one hair's-breadth. If you would win me you must prove yourself worthy of me." Geoff sighed,forhe knew that courage had never been a predominant feature with him: in fact, he wondered sometirneswhether his share of ithad not been allocated to his brother Eric, who, only the day before had rescued an old woman from the -fury of a savage bull ; while he had shivered all over when his sister Marjory asked him to remove a dead mouse from her path. "But hark," said Maude, " is not that a pause in the music? Yes, it is. Let us away from this musty corner, to the brightness of the ball room." They hurried off, and, as they whirled round the room in the next dance, Geoff wondered if she had not fixed upsthis scarecrow only to frighten off the timid bird Geoffrey Falconer. The encounter 'with Maude had damped his spirits, and, as he looked across the room at Captain Joyce minus a finger and an eye, he envied him his loss. Then he thought of his hostess ; even she bad rescued a little girl from drowning—and his heart sank within him. The more he thought the more he saw his own weak- ness, until, he found himself wishing that some fool would drop a lighted match into the flimsy draperies which hung about the entrance of the smoke room, and thus set fire to the house, that he might try to rescue Maude.

Just at that moment Captain Joyce, who had been escorting the Honourable Ellen West across the room, caught his foot in the embroidery of her dress, and she would have fallen had not the Captain caught her in his arms. Even this incident shot a pang of pain through the frame of Geoffrey Falconer, for, he feared that had he been in Captain Joyce's position, the fair Ellen would have fallen to the floor.

After a time Maude and he were together again, but their conversation was very sparse. %Menthe carriages wereannouneed, however, he shook off his lethargy and led her downstairs.

He handed her into the carriage in such alight-hearted manner, that she wondered if he had already forgotten the little lecture she had givenhim in the shaded corner upstairs. That night Geoffrey did not go to bed. When he had got through three of his strongest cigars, he put on his coat and strode out into the eool night air. He made his way to his pal's, Stephen Yates ; and al- though it was an unearthly hour, he gave the bell a vigorous pull. There was no answer at first, but, after a while, Stephen came creeping dowrstairs. Geoffrey then whisperedthrough the keyhole that he wished to talk with him.

Stephen Yates thought Geoff a bit strange in his head, so humoured him by letting him in. They went into the sitting room, and, raking together the few dying embers in the grate, sat and talked. Stephen was a bachelor, and sympathised with Geoff in his love trouble. Years before, he had been refused on similarly trivial grounds : and, as he told Geoff all about it, there grew between them a feeling of mutual sympathy.. . .

When, in he early hours of the morning, Geoffrey Falconer left the house of Stephen Yates he was light- hearted and joyous, for the conference had given him hope, _ _ _ _

Only a few weeks went by and Lady Allday astonished society by sending out invitations to another ball. It was to be a greater function than any previously, and anticipation ran high. As the guests arrived they were dazzled by the magni- ficence. All was one blaze of light and glare. Her Ladyship had weeded out all the names which did not suggest either personal high prestige, or influential connections.

Geoffrey Falconer, of course, was there, for he was not only of blue blood, but was a special favourite of Lady Allday. Rumour said she was anxious to be able to definitely announce the engagement of Maude Stuart and Geoffrey —but then rumour will say anything. llt was noted that Maude Stuart and he were on even better terms than before, and everyone wondered why their engagement had not yet been announced. They danced together often, and when the time came for Maude to leave, Geoff was very profuse with his attentions. Even Lady Stuart, Mande's mother, was pleased with Geoff's thoughtfulness, and, as he closed the carriage door to waive them off, she suggested that he should ride home with them. This he readily consented to do; and, delaying the carriage only a moment while he bad a last word with Lady Allday, he was soon seated opposite them, and roMing along towards the stately mansion of the Stuarts.

All went well until they arrived in the vicinity of Louton-lane. This was a dark spot, and every coach- man around would rather drive ten miles further than come along this short but dangerous road. Of course it was only dangerous because of its rocky nature, for the days of highwaymen had long been past. When they arrived at this spot, the driver went very slowly, and Lady Stuart was about to hurry him up when she saw the faze of a man peering in at the win- dow, and evidently riding on the step. She screamed, and Maude clung closely to her. The coachman, alarmed, would have whipped his horses on, but they were seized on either side by two pairs of , strong hands. _ _

Maiide looked across at Geoff appealingly, and he,in turn, looked hack at her terrified.

At last, with a look as 'of one driven to his wits' end, he flung open the carriage door and stepped out. In- , stantly he was seized by two men, but he threw them , off as if they were children. t Then, running to the horses heads, he dealt each man a blow which sent him sprawling into the ditch. But it could hardly be expected that Geoff could cope with all the gang of men who seemed to swarm around the carriage.

The two men who had seized him first, now came to the aid of their comrades, and Geoff was in danger of being overwhelmed. The coachman, meanwhile, was occupied entirely in keeping his horses under control, while the footman vainly endeavoured to allay the fears of Lady Stuart awl her daughter. One of the men had seized Geoff, and together they rolled on the ground. to the accompaniment of the screams of Lady Stuart and Maude. Now Geoff was uppermost, and now his antagonist. Mande could see by the light from the carriage lamps, the two men gripping each other's throats, and she nearly fainted. With a frantic effort the coachman freed his horses, and nis-ay the carriage went, leaving the scalers in the roadway. _

The last Maude saw of Geoffrey was, as he layon the ground gripping his assailant's throat. Then she fell back in the carriage and swooned away. Arrived at home Lady Stuart despatched the carriage back to the scene of the attack, with as many men as she could 'mister, to rescue Geoff. They returned, however, and reported to her, that not a trace could be seen of any of the men, not even of Geoffrey Falcorer.

Next iJay when Geoff called'at the Stuart's to inquire after the welfare of Maude and her mother, his recep- tion was of a very gushing.nature. Even the sticking plaster across his cheek was pitted by Lady Stuart as if it had been the most lovable thing in the world. "Poor Geoffrey," she said. "What a murderous attack it was. They seemed not to trouble about us inside the carriage, all their • nslaught was upon you." "Yes," said Maude "look at his throat; why, it is marked all over." -

"Never mind." said Geoff, "it will be all right soon." And it was all right soon; soonerthan they expected. Before many weeks had passed theengagement of Maude Stuart and Geoffrey Falconer was announced. Then came the marriage: after t.hat,the honeymoon. One day they were looking upon the waters of Lake Lucerne, for the honeymoon. was not yet over. Ger ff was sitting in a low camp ?hair, and Maude wrs stand- ing by his side, stroking his black hair gently. "My brave boy," she said, "I knew some day your courage would come and you would win me. WOMEN'S CHAT.

The London season promises to be noted again this Year for its huge public fetes for charitable purposes. The Duchess of Sutherland is organizing a splendid entertainment in aid of the Potteries Cripples' Guild, and next month a banquet, under the presidency of Sir Charles Wyndham is to take place at the Hotel Cecil, in aid of the Ophthalmic Hospital. At the end of May a large and elaborate fête organized by Princess Christian ia aid of the "St. Helena Home of Rest for Friendless Girls "is arranged for, and in the early days of June, Lady Ancaster's annual fête in 'aid of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, will take place. Then there is the concert in aid of St. Batholomew's HoSpital, and the ball at the Albert Hall in aid of King's College Hosp. tal, both of which are arranged for June, and later still comes the largest and most elaborate of the charitable summer fetes which is being organized by Lady Cadogan, and Mrs. Claude Hay, in aid of the Victoria Hospital for Children, and which is to be opened by the Queen. Charitable entertainments are indeed more "fashionable" than ever before, and few Society women do not number some or other function of this description in their London season's list of ' engagements. Getting up in a Hurry.—A medical man has started the co.nfortable doctrine that getting up in a hurry is dangerous to health and even to life. Experience does not coincide with this theory, since most of the longest- livers follow early hours, and it is only the compara- tively few who are able to act upon the principle that a late and leisurely rising is necessary for our physical well-being. Most people have duties to perform which compel them to get up at a certain time whether they want to or not, and it is no use telling them that early rising means physical and mental degeneration. The lazy savage gets up when he feels inclined, and never becomes insane, but it does not follow, as one well- known specialist on brain disease holds, 'that compulsory early rising is a prolific cause of insanity. The evil of exaggeration.—The exaggeration of the countless hygienic dangers that surround us, has had. much to do with the apathy of the public with regard to the prevention of germ diseases. This apathy is the subject of complaint on the part of some of the associa- tions, whose object is the prevention of publicinfection. Nothing has been more denounced than the trailing skirt, but, to most minds, it matters little whether we bring in the mud and disease germs on our skirt or on our boots, and the doormat itself has been condemned as nothing better than a plague spot for the breeding of germs. When dangers surround us day and night, and it is impossible to avoid them, one becomes indifferent, and it is to be faired that medical exaggeration of sup- posed perils has led to the apathy of which they com- plain. Exaggeration always brings its own retribution. I One of the most striking illustrations of this was the failure of a journal which was recently and first of all brought out exclusively for women. Its proprietors are I not usually wanting in success, but they over adver- tised and exaggerated the merits of their new venture, and the result was disappointment and corresponding resentment or indifference on the part of the public. Dressmakers' Disputes.—lf the Society of Dress- makers and Milliners which is to be formed in London, fulfils its aims, it will help to relieve the magistrates of some of the cases of disputes as to pisfits which are a constant source of trouble to them. It is only women they say, who go into Court with these cases, as men apparently prefer to put up with ill-fitting clothes rather than take the trouble of going to law about them. But the magistrates are at the disadvantage of know- -1 ine' little or nothing about the details or tbchnicalities I of woman's dress, and the Society proposes that cases 'of dispute between dressmakers and clients, should be Ireferred to them. The suggestion seems to he a very , sensible one, though possibly not all women litigants I would abide by such decisions, just the same as the I workmen who could not understand why such a system I could be called arbitration, when the case, as it Ihappened, was given against them. Best and Wears Longest.—We hear every now and I then of a non-inflammable flannelette, but the process it I undergoes rather destroys its comfort, and the best t security is to buy only the high clags flannelette such as 1 is made by Horrockses of longcloth and sheetings fame. The name is stamped on the selvedge and it wears much , longer than the cheap and foreign made materials. I Flannelette combines the cheapness of cotton with I much of the comfort of wool, and it has no particular I danger from fire, if only the best home made qualities ' are worn. It is very healthy for both day and night I wear, as it admits of free ventilation, it is warm, soft, ; and light, while in colouring, the new flannelettes ex- ceed anything yet put upon the market. Coloured Umbrellas.—There is a new idea for umbrellas of artistic colours, but if they are to rival ; the gorgeousness of the en. tout cos and sunshade, it is ; to be hored that the spotted pattern will not be adopted. 1 There is always something aggressive in spots, which is ! not seen in stripes. Sun blinds for instance are always striped, and a spotted sunshade is never half so cool- looking asa striped one. Possibly it is because the eye : becomes wearied in looking from spot to spot, but , whatever the cause of the irritation iit is certain that spots are not pleasing to the great majority of people. It is suggested that coloured umbrellas would give brightness to our dull streets in winter time, but colour is only pleasing when in compliment to its surroundiros, and this would hardly be the case with a gaudy red, green, or yellow umbrella on a wet, foggy, and muddy . day. One might just as well suggest putting the police- men into scarlet liveries, but it is to be feared that our climate does not favour such ideals foradding a note of gladness to our streets. . The Craze for Spots.—An epidemic of very 1 age spots has once more broken out amongst dress mat erial of almost every kind and sort, and a similar display appears on millinery trimmings and underskirts. It is fatal to trust any but the most experienced dressmaker with these materials, upon some of which the spots are irregular in size, and appear to be scattered haphazard over the background. Such a toilette is bound to appear more or less loud, but withouta skilful arrange- ment of design, the effect is also grotesque. Spots and stripes always add to the difficulties of the dressmaker, and for this reason such fabrics should be carefully avoided by the home worker. . The Benefit of a Vegetable Course.—Although no other country in the world supplies such an abundance ,of vegetables in their markets, as our own, there is no 1 other country where they are less appreciated. Two 1 vegetables is the extent to which most 'aousewives, go in : preparing dinner, and then always as an accompani- ment to meats. It would however prove much less , costly to serve a third or fourth vegetable as a separate ! course, as our French neighbours do, for it would tend to the consumption of less meat, in the long run, and moreover we should find a vegetable course of the greatest benefit to our health. With regard to spinach, this particular vegetable is invaluable. The man with a disordered liver, and the woman with a bad complexion should eat plenty of spinach. A celebrated chemist says that this article of food contains more iron to the square inch than the most renowned " ferrugenous remedies." The iron in spinach is easily assimilated, and the vegetable is easily digested. If our poorer classes could be brought to realisethe muscle-making of certain kinds of vegetables and the health-giving properties of all, there would unquestionably be less sickness and want among them. The Care of Furs.—Soon we hope to be packing away our heaviest furs, and a few reminders as to the best way in which to accomplish this task may he useful. A tin trunk is the best receptacle foe furs, and a quantity of tissue paper and lump carbon are also necessary. Furs moreover, should neverbe packed:away dirty. They should be thoroughly beaten, brushed and cleaned first. For ermine, oxide of zinc rubbed in with the hand is the best cleanser, while for dark furs, fig dust, or hot. bran, are preferable. All furs should be wrapped separ- ately, and chinchilla should be enveloped in blue or black paper, as it loses its colour if exposed to the light. - -

An Interesting Career.

Warrington has lost a well-known and respected inhabitant by the death of Mr. Robert Anderton, cencee, of the Cross Keys hotel, Buttermarket-street, who passed away very suddenly on Saturday night. For some months Mr. Anderton who was in his 58th year, had been suffering from an internal complaint, but there was nothing to indicate that the end was so near. He was, in fact, able to assist in the conduct of the business on Saturday evening, Becoming very unwell, he retired to bed shortly before eleven o'clock and, despite the attention of Dr. Peaeocke, he died at 11.20. He leaves a widow, two sons, and a daughter. Mr. Anderton had had an interesting career. Educated at the War- rington Blue Coat School, he early acquired a taste for music, and under the care of Mr. Oakden, the musical director, he attained proficiency as a trombone player. On com- pleting -his schoolOraining he entered the band of the Ist Royal Dragoon Guards, but, coming under the observation of Mr. Winterbottom, the musical director at the military school, .Kneller Hall, he was soon transferred to the 2nd Life Guards. In the band of this regiment he. served for many years and gained a long service medal. He had the honour of officiating at many State functions at Buck- ingham Palace and Windsor Castle, and he had also deputized for Mr. Winterbottom at the Royal Academy of Music. Twenty-two years ago he returned to Warrington, and became the licence° of the Cross Keys. He took an active part in the management of the Licensed Victuallers' Association and for many years fulfilled the duties of a committee-man. He had a good local reputation as a bowler, and on two occasions figured in local teams which carried off the Lancashire and Cheshire Championship. He was held in the highest esteem by a large circle of friends who deeply regret his death and; sympathise with the family in their sudden bereavement.- He had _long been identified with St. Paul's Church.

COMPULSORY MILITARY SERVICE.

DISCUSSION AT STOCKTON HEATH The, already familiar arguments in favour of corn. lsory military service were again advanced at a meet- -1134 held in the Church Institute, Stockton Heath, on Tuesday evening under the auspices of the Warrington branch of the National Service League. Judging from the sparse attendance, the proposals of the league have not aroused very great interest in the village. The Rev. H. V. Pigot presided, and expressed himself in favour of the compulsory trainirr, advocated by the league. Of course if the scheme were adopted, industrial conditions would, to a certain extent, have to be revolutionised. There would have to be no risk of men losing their employment by undergoing the military or naval training required. In his opinion it would be a privilege and benefit, as well as a duty, to every citizen to share the work which had hitherto devolved solely upon the Volunteers. Captain G. R. Crosfield fully explained the proposals of the league and laid much stress upon the possibilities of an invasion by a foreign power in the event of Eng- I land again being involved in a campaign like the Boer War. The league admitted that the compulsory training would interfere vvithbusiness to a certain extent but they asked employers to look upon it as a form of insurance. We must insure against invasion and the premium on the lines laid down by the league, would be very slight. The league even went further and said there would be no premium at all, because the men would come back from the camp stronger, better dis- ciplined, and better workmen in every wa,y. Employers would therefore be fully recompensed for any disadvant- ages they might have to incur. It was intended to 'copy the Swiss system by which the men were sent to camp in three batches. He moved a resolution support- ing the league proposals. Mr. F. Pearson seconded and pointed out that one argument which influenced him strongly was the im- provement in the physique of slum strongly would accrue from the training. Mr. H. J. Westbrook supported the principle of the scheme, but did not think there was sufficient guarantee as to its practical working. If they nitosie military service compulsory they interfered with "Freedom of contract between employer and emnloyee. If a man was willing to un tergo training he must have a guarantee that his place would be kept open for him. Then again it would hardly be fair to those who filled the men's places during their absence that they should be turned adrift when the training was over. He entirely agreed with Captain Crosfleld that something would have to be done, but he was unconvinced as to the practicability of the league pro- posals. He was certain the people of England would not stand conscription with its militarism. He thought that military drill should be given in all elementary schools, that further military knowledge should be imparted in the secondarysehools, and that in the higher schools the study of maps, surveying, &c., should form part of the instruction. Mr. T. May and Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins supported the resolution. Mr. Jenkins did not think the difficulty in regard to employment would be insuperable. The relations of employer and employed might, ho con- sidered, be so adjusted that the law would compel the employer to fall in with the scheme. Mr. J. W. Benchier heartily supported the resolution on the ground that nothing would tend to help the country more than the improvement in the physique of the people which would result from the training. He did not, however, think we need fear the bugbear of invasion quite as much as some people thought. In his opinion the jealousies of the continental nations, rather 'than our insular position were our protection. He pointed out how, with the aid of telegraphy and the railways, a large force could easily be massed if an enemy attempted an invasion. In regard to the employment difficulty, Captain Crosfleld was an employer of labour and if he was satisfied that the scheme could be worked, he (Mr. Bouchier) would be prepared to accept his judgment,although it appeared to him that that phase of the question was a great diffi- culty. It was also well to consider how, in the event of the navy being defeated, they would be able to pre- vision the country. In order to prepare for a state of siege, granaries would be required. If they were not provided, the navy would have to protect not only the colonies, but the food stuffs coming from America. The Rev, C. A. NI. Evans asked if the opinions of em- ployers of labour had been obtained by the league. He supported the scheme, • for he contended that if they strengthened the second line of defence, they would be strengthening the navy as well. On the score of dis- cipline, and also believing the old Roman saying " If you desire peace, prepare for war," he supported the resolution. _ _

• Captain Crosfield pbinted out that the league had not gone thoroughly into the details of the employ- ment question as they wanted, first of all, to drive home the necessity of a change from the present voluntary system. Their proposals were almost identical with those in force in Ssitzerland, and in that country it was an unheard of thing for a man to lose his place through undergoing the training. When he first took the auestion up, he did so entirely from the physical, educational, and moral points of view. After going into statistics &c., and reading German pamphlets on the invasion of this country, he saw that invasion was not nearly so impossible as he once thought. Therefore he was now keener on the scheme from the military than from the civil point of view. He did not think the employers of labour had yet been aporoached, but be would mention the matter to the secretary. Apparently the secretary had looked upon the employers as being rather conservative in the matter, and had aimed at getting a numerous backing especially among the working classes. The scheme was so absolutely right and just that it was bound to win in the long run. The resolution was carried unanimously.

MEETING AT FRODSTIAM.

An enthusiastic meeting of the Warrington Branch was held at Frodshiuu in the Drill Hall on Thursday. Admiral Sir N. Bowden-Smith, K.C.B. came specially from London to address the meeting on the subject of "National Defence." The chair was ably taken by the Rev. H. B. Blogg. Dr. Westmacott, M.D. the well known Manchester surgeon, moved the following resolution "That this meeting of the inhabitants of Frodsbam are in favour of National Service for Home Defence or future emern'- encey, and are of opinion that the smatter is urgent and requires the immediate attention of his Majesty's Government, and that our member be requested to convy this resolution to the Prime Minister." Dr. Westmacott, in an excellent little speech, pointed out the great value of National training from the physical point of view, and spoke of the physical deterioration which came prominently before his notice in Manches- ter and the surrounding districts. Admiral .Sir F. Bowden-Smith then supported the rasolution in a speech which was listened to with the greatest attention. He pointed out that the invasion of this country, though not probable, was decidedly possible, and it was our duty to adept some modified form of universal training, so as to remove a possibility which was a standing menace to peace. He showed that the want of reserve for our Fleet was a weakness which the proposals of the League,if adopted, would at once remedy. The resolution was put to the meeting and carried unanimously. Colonel Lascelles proposed, and Mr. C. Carson seconded a vote of thanks to the speakers; and Captain Crosfleld proposed, and Mr. Ockleston seconded a vote of thanks to the Chairman.—lVlany of thepeople present joined the National Service League.

A Conscientious Objector.

At the Borough Police Court; on Monday, Edwin Jones, of Paul-street, applied for a certificate exempt- ing his child from vaccination.— He said he had a conscientious objection to vaccination, which he believed would lie injurious to the health of the child. A sister of his had suffered from abcesses in conse- quence of vaccination, although he could not produce a medical certificate to that effect.—Mr. S. Steel : All we have to do is to satisfy ourselves that the appli- cant has a conscientious objection.—Mr. Birchall : If he has a conscientious objection we are bound to grant the certificate.—The Mayor was understood to remark that he did not believe a man could be con- scientious when he wanted to run the risk of spread- ing smallpox in the town.—The application was granted, Alderman Smethurst intimating that he would not sign the order.

THE COUNTY COUNCILS.

Lancashire and Cheshire Chairmen

Re-Elected.

At the first meeting of the Lancashire County Coun- cil held on Thursday, at Preston, Sir John Hibbert for the sixteenth time was elected chairman upon the mo- tion of the Lord Mayor of Manchester,seconded by Mr. lme, M.P. Sir John reviewed the work of the Coun- cil. Eight years ago there were 370,000 people in the county area without hospital accommoda- tion. Now there were only 47,500 without accommodation. He lamented that the expenditure- had increased from Old. to 9d. since they were. first established, but the additional threepence had beeix satisfactorily expended. When he heard the outcry about the increased expenditure he found that while their county expenditure had increased 50 per cent- since they were established, imperial expenditure had. increased 70 or 80 per cent., and some local authorities. in the same time 150 per cent. The County Council. could pay its way and had no debt.—Sir W. B. Houlda- - worth, M.P., was elected vice-chairman.

The' first 'meeting of the newly-elected County Coun— cil for Cheshire was held on Thursday at Chester Castle. Colonel George Dixon was unanimously appointed. chairman.—Colonel Dixon, in taking the chair, thanked. the Council for the eleventh time for bestowing the- honour upon him.—The aldermen retired andi all were re-elected with the exception of Mr. Joseph Beckett, in whose place Mr. T. Raffles Wiley, of Liscard, was appointed.— Alderman Beeley was re-appointed vice-chairman of the, Council.—Mr. John Thompson moved the re-election of various corumittee.—The chairman Dr. Hewitt, and, Dr. Hodgson were appointed a deputation to join with. the Worcestershire deputation in making a strong xe— presentation, to the Local Uoverninent Board on the subject of national education being made a charge on. the national exchequer, rather than on local ex- chequers.

At subsequent proceedings in committee it was, announced that this was Alderman John Thompson's. last appearance as a member of the Council, he having intimated his intention to resign. The. retirement of Mr. Joseph Beckett through illness was also alluded to. Mr. Beckett was chairman of the Main Roads Com- mittee for many years, while Mr. Thompson was chair— man of the Upton Asylum Committee. .

NEWTON-114-11AKERFIELD LECTURES COMMITTEE.

Radium and its Marvels.

The last lecture of the winter series was given in the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Wednesday evening by Dr. Hampson, M.A., Oxon &c., the subject being. "Radium ; its Marvels, Scientific and Practical." Mr._ W. Collingwood presided, and the hall was crowded. The Chairman in introducing the lecturer said the attendance this session had been far above the average of any other,and it was obvious if they went on as they had been doing, they would have to enlarge the hall. Two years ego he had great pleasure in listening to Dr. Hampson on "Liquid air," but that night he was to lecture upon- a very important, subject, "Radium." (Hear, hear.) . _

Dr. , Hampson said it was very cheering and gratify- ing to see such a large number *f people collected! together to listen to a subject which, after all, was,. purely scientific. There had beena great deal of public. interest taken in this subject of Radium for a long time- and they had had many opportunities of hearing and. reading of what was stated and written about Radium. Radium was something extremely small in more ways, than one; the total quantity of it which was ownedand. possessed in the world, if collected together, would not weigh half-a-pound, nor anything like it. It had been; the business of science, more and more in recent years,. to deal with the excessively small, and it was a woman,. who discovered this wonderful radium, the wife of Professor Curie. After many months of arduons labour she and her husband succeeded in separating and extracting this new substance. It took a very great quantity of the raw material in the preparation—about 80 tons—in order to extract less than half a thimble- ful of the radium required. Under these circumstances- Considering the great quantity of raw material and the. great quantity of labour required it was only natural that the price of the finished product was very great... The price per ounce on the market was £BO,OOO to,. £90,000. Professor Curie states that if they hada pound' of it in a room such as that they would all be. killed, at any rate, if they left the room alive they would be ,blind. The lights were- then lowered and the lecturer handed round a, couple of glass tubes containing minute particles of ' radium in order that the audience rdight see its beauti- ful effects, and there were frequent expressions of won-- delrment. Illustrations, showing scientifically the reason, for the radiation and the scintillations, were given by- means of the lantern anal long and purely scientific ex-. planation was given. Dr. Hampson in conclusiom said that the keenest interest that could be taken in the subject - was fully justified by the very great influence-it had ex- erted and would continue to exert on scientific- progress, the ,possibilities'it had already begin to realize in surgery., and the results of immeasurable importance in the, industrial world to which its study may open the way. After the lecture the working committee were enter- tained to supper by Mr. Ormand the secretary, and several felicitious speeches were made, it being agreed that the course had been the most successful that had. been held.

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

WORLD'S GREATEST EXHIBITION

The St. Louis Exhibition is to be the great event of 1904, next to the Russo-Japanese war. It will be open from May i to Decem- ber 1. For the past year or so America in general and St. Louis in particular have been in a fever of excitement over this coming world's show. Every country in the world has been invited to participate by sending its best, and that to the fullest extent. It is to show life and movement, really to let the ex- hibition form a roll of honour of man, and the works of man in his industries and craftt. The intention is to present the conspicuous progress made in the employment of the energies of Nature, and in useful invention and discovery. In fact, St. Louis, for. the time being, will be an epitome of the whole world. The exhibition will stand in. Forest Park, a well timbered tract of land of about 1,300 acres inside the boundaries of St. Louis. The exhibition will occupy 1,180 acres of this

Great Britain will De represented at the St. Lout Exhibition by reproductions of Kensington Palace Orangery and the cottage in which Burns was born.

park—a space a mile wide and one and three- quarters of a mile long. This is three or four times the size of Hyde Park. Over sixty buildings, ranging HO cost from 5,000 to E,90,000 each, have now been erected. Eng- land will be represented by the Queen's Jubilee gifts and by a reproduction of the Orangery at Kensington Palace, which was built for Queen Anne in 1704. A replica of the cottage where Robert Burns was born will also be erected. The French Pavilion will be a reproduction of the Grand Trianon of Ver- sailles, the handsome villa built by Louis XIV. for Mme. de Maintenon. It was one of the favourite residences of the first Napoleon. Germany's building will be a faithful minia- ture of the Royal Palace of Charlottenburg. The United States Government building has cost Zoo 000. China's building will be a replica of af, moil; temple, centuries old. Japan's exhibit will include almost every feature of social and industrial development.

GRAND DUCHESS OLGA ALEXANDROVNA.

One of the features of the present war in the Far East is the patriotic and self-sacrific- ing way in which it has been taken by both countries, especially amongst the upper classes. On the Japanese side, the Crown Prince and his brother have already taken the field, and on the Russian the Tsar's two bro-

A ROYAL NURSE:

Tsarina and he spend a large part of theft spare time in devotion and intercessional prayer on behalf of their cause and the men who are fighting. Now comes the news that the Grand Duchess Olga Alexandrovna has decided to volunteer as a nurse, and is going out to superintend the work of the Red Cross Society in Manchuria. This will unquestion- ably result in a large number of Russian ladies in the most exclusive set following her brave example; and a most popular actress has already thrown up the stage temporarily for the uniform of the Red Cross. These are the kind of things which do more than any- thing else to cheer on an army in the field fighting under difficulties and suffering priva- tions from hunger and cold, as it makes them feel that they are not forgotten by the high- est in the land. At the moment the Grand Duchess Olga Alexandrovna is the heroine of the hour in St. Petersburg. Bound upon more deadly work, General Kuropatkin left the capital at the end of last week, and great things are expected from this Russian Kit chener.

THE SAD DEATH OF MR. SEWALLIS SHIRLEY, EX-M.P. Considerabe sensation was caused in London last week by the announcement in some of the evening papers that an ex-mPmber of Parliament had died under circumstances which were suggestive of foul play: but the report turned out afterwards to have been unfounded s o far as the question of anything el s e but a natural death. The ex- M.P. referred to was Mr. Sewallis Evelyn Shirley, who used to re- present County Monaghan in th e Conserva- tive interest. The facts wen fully stated at the in- quest, when it was shown that he had come up to town and put un at the Hotel Windsor. He IRLEY. had gone out to call upon a lady in Manchester Street: and there he was taken very ill and gradually grew worse. A doctor was sent for and, as there was no proper accommodation for Mr. Shiiley, allowed him to be taken in a cab to Westminster Hospital. However, he died on the way, and, as the post-mortem showed, there was no question of violence, merely a sudden fatal illness. Mr. Sewallis Shirley was a very well-known man in town, as he had many interests which brought him up to London and many friends, whom he often visited at the House of Com- mons. After having represented County Monaghan from 1868 until 1880, he was defeated: and 'upon the redistribution in 1885 he contested South Monaghan, where he was rejected by nearly four thousand votes, after which he decided not to stand again. Mr. Shirley was a large landed proprietor and J. P. both for Warwickshire and County Monaghan. His seats were Ettington Park, Stratford-on-Avon and Lough Fea, Carrick- macross ; and he was also High Sheriff of County Monaghan in 1884. He was descended directly from one of the oldest families in the country, being of Saxon lineage, as a Sewallis de Ettington certainly existed in Staffordshire at the time of Edward the Confessor. Mr. Shirley was born in 1844 and educated a Eton and Christ Church.

DR. MACHRAY.

From Winnipeg comes the news of the death of Dr. Machray, the Primate of All Canada and head of the Church of England througbout the whole Dominion J In Canada

he was very popular and looked upon wit& the utmost respect by all denominations, and his death will be generally re- grettedon every side. His con- nection with the Dominion is of very old stand- ing and dates back nearly 40 years to the time when Dr. Mach- ray was appoin- ted Bishop of Rupert's Land in 1865. He held .that posi- tion until 1893, when he was made Archbish- op of RuPert's Land and Prim- THE LATE DR: MACHRAY4 ate of All Cana- da. In that same year he was created a Prelate of the Order of St. Michael and St. George. But, realoui as he always was in doing good works in a religious way, he did not neglect other more temporal affairs, and one of his first and most notable acts in the Dominion was to inaugurate d system of education which has proved of the utmost value and benefit throughout the whole Colony. He held, at the time of his death, the position of Chan- cellor of the University of Manitoba. One incident connected with his death was the fact that the fearful cold and snowdrifts in this, the coldest winter known in Canada for many years, prevented the majority of his Bishops from being present at his funeral.

A GREAT PAINTER.

By the death of that famous artist, MrJ Erskine Nicol, another representative of a kind of art which has always been popular, has gone. It is a kind of art which, in spite of the "art-education" of the multi- tude, and of " movements" and societies, I ntern ational and native, will remain Popular to the end,

Mr. Erskine Nicol was a

good painter in

flis Way, felici-

tous in his sub-

jects, _ and con-

scientious in his

work, cheerful

in his outlook on the world, pure _in mind,

and honest in his view of life.

He had his re-

ward in popu- THE LATE MR. ERSKINE° 'ldly and

latity,

solidly

NICOL, R.S.A., A.R.A.

e a r-n e d

firm!y held, and although it isnearly twenty years since he withdrew from the active ro,l of the Royal Academy, and placed him- self on the retired list, he is still affection- at.ly remembered by a wide public, and the engravings of many of his pictures are still 6esteemed and treasured in the land. It was no easy task on which he set out in his boyhood. Almost from his birth, which took place in Leith in 1825, Nicol had shown extraordinary aptitude with his pencil. As a child, he declared his inten- tion of becoming an artist, and—as has so often distinguished the youthful struggles of painters—he had to overcome the objec- tions of a poor and prudent parent. He secured, however, an early apprenticeship to a house-painter, as David Roberts had done before him, and in his spare time he attended the Trustees' Academy in Edinburgh. Owing, we are told, to an appearance of years he did not possess, he obtained at the age of twelve admittance to the schools, although the mini- mum at which he could rightly enter was fourteen. He made rapid progress under Sir William Allan and Thomas Duncan, and in due time obtained the appointment of draw- ing-master at the Leith High School, and at twenty he went to Ireland, where he spent four eventful years.

THE KING'S NEW MOTOR.

No one has fallen more completely under the fascination of the motor than the Ring himself, and he is one of the keenest and most fearless automobilists in the country. Moreover, he, as King, is outside the new Act and has not to carry a number, which is a great advantage. Hitherto, he has only patronised the Daimler Company, who manu- facture their cars in Coventry, and he has some of the finest specimens of that firm's work, including a model shooting-wagonette. However, the last time he wast on the Con- tinent His Majesty, like so many other wealthy motorists, fell a victim to the great German-made car, the Mercedes, which have been all the rage for the last two years and shone so brilliantly in the Gordon-Bennett Cup race in Ireland last year. This car, it is

, ir 4 _

===== 0002601_19040319_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS MAJESTY'S NEW MOTOR.

generally admitted, has few, if any, equalt at present on the market, if the question of cost is of no account with the purchaser, and they are noted not only for their re- liability and simplicity, but also for their quietness in running and great speed. In build they are a comparatively low, long car on very graceful lines, and until lately used to be distinguishable by their peculiar bon- nets. His Majesty's new car is said to be capable of doing sixty-five miles an hour on a clear road, and it is built on light lines, containing only four seacs in all, including the chauffeur, and has no protectors of any sort. The car arrived at Buckingham Palace only a few days ago in view of the com- mencement of the real motoring season, and ere long it will be pretty well known by sight, as are His Majesty's other cars.

THE QUEEN'S NEW PORTRAIT.

Of Mr. Edward Hughes, the celebrated portrait-painter, whose painting of the Queen in her Coronation robes formed such a pleasing gift to the King on the forty-first anniversary of his wedding, Sir John Millais has said: "Very many people can paint a man, but very few can paint a lady; and Hughes can." Those who know Mr. Hughes's work will appre- ciate this ver- dict. He has shown over and over again that the,great painter was right in his criticism. All that is lovely in famous feminin- ity of the pre- sent generation has been charm- ingly portrayed MR. EDWARD HUGHESII by Mr. Hughes's brush. The Queen has sat for him five times, and it is well known that the portrait he painted of her in 1896, when she was Princess of Wales, and which was afterwards exhibited at Agnew's Gallery, and by the King's special desire at the Guildhall, is preferred by the Royal Family to the efforts of any other artist. Among the famous sitters to Mr. Hughes are the Duchesses of Montrose, Devonshire, Leinster, and Dudley, Princess Isabel D'Orleans, Lady Lubbock, with her little son; Lady de Trafford, the Marchioness of Worcester, Lady Helen Vincent, and the Princess of Wales, and the two Royal Prin- cesses Victoria and Maud:

Warrington v. Swinton.

A GRAND GAME AND A POINTLESS DRAW.

Warrington took their strongest fifteen to Swinton on Saturday, confident of holding their own at least. The encounter was one which aroused a lot of interest in both districts and some 2,000 ardent Warrington entkusiasts journeyed to the colliery village by rail and road. Manchester and Swiaton folk also swarmed to the enclosure to witness what was looked upon as the match of the day in Lancashire. Fine weather prevailed, and fully 9,000 spectators surrounded the enclosure when play commenced. The ground was in good order considering the absence of grass, and a fast game was looked for. Mr. J. IL Smith had charge of the whistle and the teams lined out _in the following order: Swinton.—Chorley, back; Gartrell, Dixon, Traynor, and Hampson, three-quarter backs; Molvneux and Wall- work. half backs; Evans, Barnett, Preston, Smith, Tickle, Simister, Cheetham, and Yates, forwards. Warrington.—Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son, and Harris, three-quarter backs; Bate and Davies, half backs; Boardman, Swift, Cook, Thomas, Fairhurst, Naylor, Jolley, and Harmer, forwards. Touch judges, Messrs. T. Anderton, L igh, and C. Simm, Wigan. Dickenson sot the ball in motion for Warrington,who had the slope to contend with, but the return was poor, and Swinton were soon on the defence. Bate charged Molyneux's kick down in the first minute, but the danger was averted. Warrington maintained their footing in the home quarter, and a beautiful round of passing by the backs seemed certain to result in a score when Isherwood mulled the transfer to Fish. A moment later the ball was kicked past Gartrell, and Fish out- pacing his opponent dribbled away in fine style, only to over-run the bouncing ball at the critical moment. Swinton rushed the bill away to centre, but Warrington were soon back again, only to fail through faulty pass- ing again, Dickenson being the offender. While the attack was proceeding hotly, Traynor stood offside, and a penalty to Fish ought to have resulted in a goal, but it didn't, much to the chagrin of the visitors. Still Warrington pegged away, Harmer knocking forward from Da,vies's cross-kick right on the line. Quickly the game travelled across to the far corner, where Fish made a desperate effort, but had to succumb to num- bers. A mull by Dickenson finally relieved the tension for Swinton, whose front rank swept down upon Hallam and tackled him at centre. Davies was prominent with a fine break-away, only to be pulled down when a pass would have given Harris a walk-over. Then the home backs got a look in, and Hampson came down speedily from Tra,ynor's pass. Hallam tackled the sprinter in glorious fashion however. Swinton, from Molyneux's kick managed to get a position near the Warrington line, Bate kicking clear before the danger was acute. Half-time, no seore. _ _

Warrington had had so much the better of the game in the first half that they were expected to do great things on change of ends. Swinton, however, went off with a rattle, and were soon in the Warrington quarter. Preston took a penalty shot at goal, but the ball dropped a yard short. Fish cleverly ran the ball out, but the Blues returned with such effect that the ball was rushed to the Warrington line, Harris saving pluckily, inches only from the magic rut. Molyneux had a fruitless drop at goal, and then a strong effort by Dickenson changed the venue. Warrington at once put on pres- sure, and the home defence was tested to the utmost. First Fish and then Isherwood WAS stopped at the corner. Then Davies dodged to within a yard of the line, Dickenson tried to jump over, and Bate was downed in the very last stride. The Blues were completely over-played, but they defendedrmly. Warrington tried every way forabout 20 minutes, but the try would not some. Hallam even had a drop at goal, but was a long way out. Harris's win,' was rather neglected, which was a mistake, as the sturdy right-winger might have succeeded where Fish had failed if he had been properly attended to. Swinton managed to get to the ceatre before the whistle sounded for full time, leaving the game a pointless draw.

THE REPLAYED TIES.

WARRINGTON SWAMP SWINTON.

Great interest was shown in the re-played cup-tie between Warrington and Swinton on the Wilderspool ground on Wednesday. Most of the big works in the town closed at 4 p.m., and with contingents from Wigan, Swinton, Widnes, and St. Helens, there were quite 7,000 people on the ground at 4.30, the gate receipts amounting to £l6O. Warrington relied on exactly the same team as at Swinton, but the Blues made no less than half-a-dozen changes. Fine weather again pre- vailed and the ground was in capital order. Mr. J. H. Smith refereed and the teams were :

Swinton :—W. Dixon, full back; V. R. Hampson, D. Trayner (captain), A. Chorley, and J. T. Lewis, three- quarters; A. Williamson, and W. Wallwork, half-backs; J. Preston, W. Cheetham, H. Barnett, E. Yates, T. Pom- fret, J. Coletrop, W. Simister, and J. Smith, forwards. Reserve, R. Tickle.

Warrington (as in Saturday's match). The home side won the toss and Preston started for the Blues against a good breeze. Warrington were at once put on the defensive, the visitors getting the ball smartlyfrom the pack and making frequent use of the touch-line. A lengthy punt by Fish eventually took play to the opposite end, and excitement grew as War- rington attacked hotly. Fish made a fine effort from half way, but was brought down near the line. Before War- rington could be driven back a penalty was givenagainst the Blues, and Fish placed a goal amid ringing cheers. Swinton at once forced the game to the Warrington half, but the attack was easily:dealt with,and the Blues made only a short stay here. Warrington soon reached the Swinton quarter, but slow passing by Bate led to the backs making little headway. The forwards rushed the game to near the line, and quick transfers between Bate, Davies, and Dickenson resulted in the lengthy centre dashing over the line with a couple of opponents clinging to him. The place was easy and Fish kicked another goal. Up to half-time Swinton had a fair share of the game, but never looked like scoring. The work of the home backs was below par and the interval arrived with the score 2 goals and a try to nil in War- rington's favour. Diskenson re-started for Warrington, who went off at a rare pace. Two minutes play sufficed for Davies, Dickenson, Isherwood and Fish to get going, and the latter completed a fine movement by running through the Swinton defence and scoring at the corner. Dicken- son took the kick but failed, against the wind, to send the ball over. The Warrington men were now perform- ing in better style; the work of the backs being much cleaner. Isherwood was soon over again, after Bate had bluffed through, but he was recalled for a faulty transfer. Swinton were given no chance, however, and Davies getting away on the right com- pletely baffled the defence with a short cross- kick, Isherwood rushing over and grounding, for Fish to kick another goal. With a lead of 15 points War- rington relaxed their efforts somewhat, and Preston nearly brought about the downfall of the homasters with a fine run. Warrington soon attacked apain, Harris making a powerful effort to get in at the corner, but succumbing to Dixon. The homesters were exceedingly well-placed, and clever passing between the halves and centres resulted in Isherwood doubling through the goal-posts. Fish raising the total to 20 points with another goal. Final :—Warringtoli, 4 goals 4 tries (20 points), Swinton, nil.

LOLL GHERNMENT REFORM.

THE DUTIES OF THE CITIZEN. Friintiaa or a Legtl3.

On Satarday afternoon, in the Minehoster Coal Ittxch Lace, a caaference wis held cot parsons interested lathe reformof local government, with the object of ' forming a league in which local associations represent- ing the ratepaying interest under various names may be linked for united effort in this direction. Councillor Dodds (Warrington) presided, and the towns repre- sented by individual reformers or members of various societies included London, Southampton, Cheltenham, Stoke-on-Trent, No rthwich, Manchester.Salford, Bolton, Oldham, and Rochdale, Stockport and Warrington. Mr. H. Boleti Smith, the secretary, said the great thing needed in every town and district was an awaken- ing of the public spirit in connection with the public duties of the pssple. The greatest evil that had to be contended with in connection with the rapid develop- ment of local government was the apathy, and, he ought also to ?cid, the widespread ignorance of the people. An organisation such as that now suggested could, by the co-operation of different local associations affiliated, employ lecturers and adopt other means to educate the ratepayers as to what were their powers under the law. He mwed:—"That this meeting of persons working for the more efficient administration of municipal government, including representatives from various parts of the kingdom, agrees forthwith to take the necessary steps to establish a national league for the promotion of municipal reform, which shall endeavour to link together for united effort all local non-party organisations which aim at the improvement of local government, and to enlist the co-operation and support of persons who agree with the objects of the league." The resolution having been seconded, Mr. S. G. Hobson (London) said that if the organisa- tion they proposed to form was to represent the general interests of the whole nation that was a matter for the Houses of Parliament, and not for an organisation which, as he understood it, was to be utilized in the in- terests of ratepayers alone. The Chairman sag the propesed objects of their organisation were the encouragement of municipal enterprise where necessary for the public benefit, the furtherance of measures intended to improve the public health, and the education of public opinion in the best, methods of citizenship. Mr. S. Tebbutt (Southamoton) said he could have nothing to do with an association started to encourage municipal trading. He was aware that was debatable ground, and he thought they would do no good by bringing it forward now. Whatever they did, however, they must keep politics out of their organisation. Asso- ciations formed totprotect the interests of ratepayers had in numbers of cases been captured by one or other of the political parties and ran for the particular bene- fit of that party. The Chairman 'agreed that local government should b e separated from politics. Mr. S. G. Hobson admitted that the whole question was full of difficulties. He supposed all present had come there with some idea of the attitude that ought to be adopted towards these questions. One attitude had been represented by Mr. Tebbutt, and it was really an attitude of suspicion towards the general work of municipal bodies. If that was the attitude of the Con- ference, Mr. Hobson said, it would be his duty to with- draw, because he believed very strongly in developing the idea of municipal government. (Hear, hear.) He was not present to take part in what would simply be a combination of ratepayers' associations, because he thought the general policy of such associations up and down the country during the last few years had been pernicious in its influence. There was, therefore, a wide gulf between those who believed in the development of local and municipal self-government, and those who wanted to curtail what was being done by local govern- ing bodies. If the Conference ad met with the idea of co-operating in the general development of local govern- ment, he and the Fabian Society, which he represented, would be glad to aid the movement in every possible way. In the course of further discussion. _ _ _

Mr. S. Norbury Williams said he did nqt think they would be doing right if they made merely the keeping down of rates their chief object. That was one of their bases, but ;what they chiefly aimed at was purity of municipal life. They could not calculate the character of their administration by their rates. If advanced ex- penditure WAS for the good of the citizen he was for that expenditure, . because he wanted the life of the citizen to be as happy and clean as it could be made. He would rather live in a town rated at 10s. in the pound and have a good and pure life than in a town where the 'rates were 3s. or 4s. in the pound,and where the public life was at the lowest ebb. They must have more than the question of rates in view. He agreed with Mr. Hobson that they should seek the progress of municipal government. The resolution was carried. A COMMITTEE APPOINTED.

rae following ware appointed a provisiolal com- mittee, viz :—_ _

Messrs. S. Norbury Williams, Manchester; James Dodds, Warrington; H. Bodell Smith, Mottram ; A. Thompson, Openshaw ; James Howard-, Middleton; John Edwards, Liverpool; W. Pott, Glossop; Allen Clarke, 'Bolton; C. Topham, Blackpool; L. Flood, Prestwich •, J. Gaming Walters, Chorlton ; Mrs. Clark, Women's Local Government Society; and Mrs. Parkhurst, Manchester. 4 It was also resolved that membership of the League should consist of local non-party societies paying not less than lOs a year, or is a month, and of men and women paying an annual subscription of not less than 2s Gd, each affiliated society to have one vote for ?very 2s Gi, it contributed to the League funds.

OBJECPS OF TEIE LEAGUE.

The coAference considered the constitution of the League, a draft of which had been prepared. It was resolved that the name of the League should be "The 'Local Government Reform League." The objects of the League were, after much discussion, agreed upon as follows :—To link together for united efforts all local organisations which aim at the improvement of local government; to enlist the co-operation of adult men. and women who agree with the objects of the League; to bring pressure to bear on local authorities and on Parliament, for the protection of the rights of citizen- ship and for the advancement of municipal reform; to promote parity and justice in local government; to secure the abolition of . the office of alderman; • to raform abuses of rating and assessment; to secure the amendment and consolidation of municipal laws and of Public Health A cts ; to corn- , bat the associations of local government officials when public interest requires it ; to seoure an independent 'audit of the accounts of any local authority under a I clear and complete statutory form; to enforce the rate- payers' right of access to municipal accounts and minutes of councils and committees during office hours ; to I separate local government from party politics; to secure the enforcement of pains and penalties for bribery and corruption during elections; to further all I measures aimed to improve public health; to encour- age municipal enterprise whenever necessary for public benefit ; to educate public opinion ia the best methods of citizenship ; to obtain sex equality as regards voting for and election to all local governing authorities. Some other matter relating to management, meetings, , methods, &c. were referred to the Committee for con- sideration at their first meeting. In the evening, Mr. George Harwood M.P., presided over a public meeting at which addresses were given by Mr. S. Norbury Williams, Mrs. Parkhurst, and Mr. S. G.Hobson (London, the subjects dealt with being "The Office of Elective Auditor," "Women and Local Govern- ment," and "The Efficiency of Local Administration."

THREE DAYS' CURSING.

The LAmas of Tibet held a solemn gathering last week and cursed the British expedition for three days. They then prophesied that the British forces would dwindle away by the act of Providence.

The claim of individual traders for retuon of tolls had failed because it was essential to the maintenance of this claim that default by the Canal Company should be proved. He thought that the arbitration clause in section 83 of the Act of 1885 and clause 10 of the agreement prevented traders in their Individual capacity from proving any default by the Cane' Company; but even if that were not so he should be inclined to hold that, as regarded the individual traders, jurisdiction was ousted by section 20 of the Act of 1885. If their action failed merely because something essential to their case failed to be decided by arbitration, he thought the action might be stayed and not dismissed. In his judgment, the appeal ought to be allowed. Lord Justice Stirling concurred. Lord Justice Cozens-Hardy, while concurring on some points with the other judges, differed from them upon some others. He said it was necessary to consider the action of the Corporation separately from the case of the traders. He could not regard section 88 as a mere agreement between the Canal Company and the Corpora- tion; he thought it was an enactment conferring rights upon a particular class of traders, manufacturers, and others, and that being so, itseemed to him that section 88 mast confer rights to be dealt with or enforced in the manner prescribed by section 22. He sereed that the objection which had been raised as to the Corpora- tion was damning, but it appeared to him that it would be wrong to postpone the adjudication of the claims of the traders until after the award of a reference to which they would be no parties. He could not agree that the jurisdiction of the Court was Ousted.

Manchester City Council ani the Judgment, Alderman Southern's Criticism of the Plaintiffs.

The Manchester City Council held an adjourned meet- ing on Wednesday at the Town Hall. The Lord Mayor (Mr. T. T. Shaun) presided. Mr. Alderman Southern proposed the adoption of the report of the Special Committee re; the Ship Canal. He desired, he said, to congratulate the Council upon the result of the ease heard on Tuesday in the Appeal Court.--tHea.r, hear.) It was a very important matter to the city, which 'ma!, so largely interested financially in the canal, that the judgment had been given in the form it had. The' Company were bound by law to maintain a channel through the whole length of the borough of Warrington which should be at all times "at a width of fifty feet and a depth of eight feet below the level of the lowest spring tides. Anyone acquainted with the estuary of the Mersey and of the rivers that ran into it would recognise at once what an enormous responsibility that was, because they might have a channel on one day at its full capacity and there might arise a north-westerly gale and in a few hours thousands of tons of send might be brought up and deposited in the channel which had been dredged at the expense of the Company. On the other hand, there Might be floods which would cause a similar silting up of the waterway. To say, there- fore, that the Company should "at all titans" maintain these conditions was to set them a very heavy task. The conditions asked them to overcome the forces of nature, which it was not within human power to do. That had been pointed out to the plaintiffs in the action. The Company had said they had done all they could by dredging, but that the channel could never be kept clear by that process alone. If, however, lock gates were placed at the end of the estuary, what Warrington wanted could be accomplished "at all times." To give that security would cost over £BO,OOO, and the Canal Company were prepared to spend that money in order to provide an efficient remedy. That was as far as any reasonable men could go, unless they admitted the judgment of Mr. Justice Byrne, which was a strictly legal judgment, mad accepted the words" at all times." What the plaintiffs ought first to have done was to have sent, not for a lawyer,but for an engineer,to judge as to-whether the Canal Company were doing their duty. Lord Justice Vaughan Williams allowed the appeal. Instead of going to an expert engineer for an opinion on the point, the plaintiffs had treasured up all com- plaints there might have been when a barge could not get up for want of a foot of water, and had asked for damages for all that might have happened during a long period, Lord Justice Vaughan Williams recognised that that was not a just proposition, and therefore the appeal was allowed, with the costs of the expensive litigation, which ran for about eleven days in the lower courts.

Mr. Alderman Vaudrey seconded the motion, which was passed.

MR. CDRDER SPEAKS.

Mr. S. B. Carder, Brook Street, Little Dunmow,Esaex, makes the following statement. "Last November I was laid up with pains in my back and side. If I stooped I could not get straight again, for when I attempted to raise myself it seemed as if my back was comin,g in two. I could not sleep at night and in the morning I was so tired and weary that I was not fit for anything. I had advice, and tried all kinds of remedies, but they did me no good. I began taking Dodd's Kidney Pills and they have done wonders for me or they have completely cured me. I have not had a pain or ache in my back since I finished the treatment; I feel liken new man.
