ST. HELENS.

THE Sr. HELENS LOVE TRAGEDY.

Leopold Bennison, the young St. Helens man who last week attempted to cut the throat of Kate Grady, his sweetheart, and then almost took away his own life, by means of a razor, is fast recovering from his injuries. Miss Grady's wounds are now in a perfectly satisfac- tory state, and she will be discharged from the Provi— dence Hospital soon..Bennison will probably be brought. before the magistrates next Tuesday.

Backl2y-street P.S.A.

Members of this society mustered in strong force a '• the half-yearly distribution of prizes, which took place at Buckley-street Methodist Free Church on. Sunday. Councillor Dodds Presided. Prayer was offered by Mr. H. Woolley, and Mr. Shaw read the- lesson. Mr. F. Warburton efficiently rendered the solos "There is a green. hill" and "0, rest in the Lord." Miss Plinston was the accompanist. The prizes were distributed by Mrs. Dodds and Mrs. Clay. The society again boasts of an increase of members at the beginning of another half-year: The Chairman said he was glad once more to wish them God-speed in the good work they were doing in the town. He was proud or having the honour of being on the same platform as Mrs. Clay, for whom he had the highest regard. He thanked her for taking up pul?lic work in the town. There was room for ladies on the 'Fawn 'Council. It was a disgrace to Warrington that they had not two ladies on the Education Committee. If he held the same position in the future he should' see that ladies were put on. Referring to the church, Councillor Dodds said he was glad to see it so nicely decorated, and it was a credit to them. They had shown the perseverance of a spider, and like the ant they had provided for the future, and best of all they I had fixed themselves upon the Rock of Ages.

THE EXAMINER.

MARCH 19, 1904

T h e Government

THE PLIGHT OF have decided that on THE GOVERNMENT. the whole they have nothing to gain by ap- pointing a Royal Commission to inquire into the condition of trade. Lord LA.NSD )w.)rE remarked with quaint simplicity that such an inquiry was not necessary with statesmen who were committed to the policy of retaliation. It is not necessary with them because the probability is that it would strengthen the cage for Free Trade. Lord GOSCHEN seemed to favour the idea of a Commis- sion. He cherished the hope apparently that it would have the effect of persuading Mr. BALFOUR and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN to give up a wild goose chase and to prevent the disruption of Clair party It is too late, for Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is on his way back from Egypt to complete the disruption. Mr. BALFOUR is wedded to the Sheffield pro- gramme, which, as Lord LANSDDWNE frankly confessed, cannot be explained. iHow Retalia- tion can be worked is a secret not to be wrung from Ministers, who do not know it them- selves. Very little enthusiasm for a Royal Com- mission was visible on the Opposition side in the Lords' debate. The case against Protection has been stated with such overwhelming force in the last six months that a Royal Commission,a;though it would consolidate the evidence, could scarcely tell us anything very new. Meantime the country is steadily averting its face from Protection. Many of the election expert 3 on the Unionist-, side are already counting the Liberal majority. They think it may exceed the forces of Unionists and Nationalists taken together, but they prel3r to forecast a situation of which Mr. JOHN REDMOND will be master. It also cheers them to surmise that Lord ROSEBERY will not work harmoniously with the Radical wing of the Liberal party, and that the Unionist Free Trader will not vote for any amendment of the Education Act.

The debate of the House of Commons on the administration of the Education Act is one of many signs that some compromise will be found inevitable. The principle of public control over schools maintained out of public money cannot be withstood for an indefinite period. It is so fatally inconsistent with the tradition of all public administration to put the ratepayers:in a minority on boards of management of schools, every penny of whose cost comes from public funds. Many Churchmen are already of opinion that by admitting any representatives of the ratepayers into the management of voluntary schools the Government practically gave up their case. It is quite easy in criticising Lord RO3EBERY'S speech at Newc istle to foreshadow the difficulties of a Liberal Ministry, but the critics ignore the fact that the difficulties of their own party are insurmountable. They have indeed reached such a pass that organised administram tion in every department is all out of gear. No support can be grasped with vigour and concen- tration because there is no unity of mind in the reconstructed Cabinet. Mr. BALFOUR hoped to make a favourable impression on the country b military reforms, but it is seen that these are baulked by the obstinacy of those members of the Ministry upon whose blunders the proposed re- forms are's severe censure. This state of things increases the public impatience for a change of Government. After nine years of office Mr. BALrouR. and his colleagues are at thelend of their resources. Lord ROSEBERY simply expressed a widespread belief when he said that fresh minds needed to carry on the Government. That ie most obvious by every day's procedure in both Houses of Parliament.

The elections for the Board of Gu iriians and Rural District Council are only going to involve a couple of contests in this district. We are not sure whether this is a feature on which the town may congratulate itself or not. Certainly the rate- payers are saved some expense, but the state of things which exists does not indicate a great amountof public interest in these bodies. At Black- burn,for an instance to the contrary,every Ward is being contested in the Guardians' etection,there being 52 candidates in the field for 26 seats. The only contested seat on the Warrington Board is in Latchford Ward,where Miss Broadbent opposos the retiring representative, Mr. George Pilling. On the front page will be found an adver6isernent of the lady candidate's address, wherein ay.i think she makes out a very good case for women having a share in the administration of the Pour Laws. In Orford Ward Mrs. Wilkinson has been returned unopposed, so that MIA. Clay is assured of some assistanca and companionship in the arduous labours she has for so long undertaken single- handedly. In Groat Sankey Mr. George Thompson is the formidable opoonentof Mr. Thomas Halton, the retiring chairman of the Raral District Council. Both possess a record of useful public work and it is a pity, we think, that'each gentleman cannot be returned to serve the rural ratenaprs. Tne contests take plac.) on Alonday the 23;:h inst.

Another stage has been reached in the long sdrawn-out battle between the traders and the Cor- poration of Warrington on the one hand, and the Manchester Ship Canal Company on the other, 'by the decision which was given in the Court of -Appeal on Tuesday. The decision is far from satis- factory and the Corporation come out of the legal fray the worst of all. This latest judicial decision lays it down that the Corporation have no ground of action, but that they are bound by the arbitra- tion clause in the agreement with the Ship Canal 'Company which states that all disputes arising .shall be settled by reference to arbitration. The local traders are in a still more anomalous Position. Whilst the judges agreed that they had a right of action, the Court of Appeal holds that the traders are not in a position to exercise that right until the Corporation has proceeded to arbitration and obtained the award of the arbitra- tor. It therefore follows that there are two •ourses open to the plaintiffs in the recent action. :rbey must begin de novo with arbitration proceed- ings or appeal to the House of Lords for a reversal of the decision of the Court of Appeal. The mem- bers of the Corporation have now to choose between these two alternatives. Either of them Will be costly, and will mean a considerable addi- tion to the heavy bill of costs which stands against the Corporation. The probability is that the Corporation will fight to the bitter end and appeal to the Lords.

The Liberals of Stockton Heath are rapidly arming themselves for the fray which is expected at no distant date. and on Thursday they again had the pleasure of listening to a brief but thon,ht- ful address on current politics by Mr. A. J. King, the popular prospective candidate. Knutsford Division has long been regarded as a Tory Gibraltat," but at the present moment the pros- p.ects of the party of progress and reform are par- ticularly cheering. Mr. King is meeting with every encouragement in his efforts to convince the elec- tors of the necessity for a change in their represen- tation, and his exposure of Protectionist fallacies and Governmental ineptitude will, Undoubtedly very materially influence the Voting when the trial of strength comes. It is significant of the trend of opinion, that, in the recent County Council election at Knutsford, the Hon. Alan de Tatton Egerton, M.P., was defeated by a substantial majority. It must have been a rude shock to the member and his supporters to find that after all their stronghold was not impregnable. Liberals have every reason to look forward to a triumph at the general election, and it is gratifying to find that such energy is being thrown into the work of organization at this end of the big constituency.

We do not question the fact that there is a con- siderable amount of distress in the town or that there is a large number of unemployed, but we do marvel at one or two recent happenings. On Saturday the Warrington football team journeyed to Swinton to play a cup-tie, and they were sup- Ported by some 1500 Warrington enthusiasts, mostly workingmen, none of whom could make the railway journey, see the match, and pay in- cidental charges for a less expenditure than 4s. Therefore there was a good £3OO taken out of the town on Saturday On Wednesday the same teams m3t again at Warrington, the first game having resulted in a draw, and between 6,000 and 7,000 people assembled at Widerspool at 4.30 in the afternoon. The gate-money realised £l6l and again the workingman had shown himself pos- sessed of the wherewithal to see a football match. We do not argue from this that the dis- tress is not so severe as it is represented to be, but we may fairly deduce that workingmen in Warrington are not without spare cash. Some of their more unfortunate brethren may be on the verge of starvation, but the fact that so many can afford to spend 4s. to ss. on football in the course of a few days throws a little light on the state of employment locally. There is some satisfaction in reflecting that to-day the Wiganers will probably 'lug more money into the town than was taken out last Saturday.

The attendance st the Meeting held to protest against any interference with the discretionary Power of the magistrates with regard to licensing, tIISEI disappointing. Not more than 15,0 people • "ought it worth their while to display any active ,interest in this important subject. Warrington la One of the most difficult towns in the kingdom to arouse to any degree of enthusiasm on. public ,affairs, especially when "the trade" is affected. he ramifications of this particular industry in warringson a-c so widespread that there seems to be a general disposition to sit still and Say" nuffin." It is quite true that the Govern- ment's Licensing Bill is not yet before the country, but we have heard enough from the Prime Minister to make us fear for the worst, and it is high time the public showed their resentment at any attempt to interfere with the discretionary Power of the magistrates. So far as Warrington is concerned the trade" has no reasonable

ground for complaint against the magistrates in administering the licensing laws; if anything the licensing committee has erred on the side of leniency to the brewers and publicans. There has been no wholesale reduction of licences, and the steadily increasing population has tended to enhance the value of every existing licensed house. On the other hand what has been the attitude of " the trade" towards the Bench P A. solemn promise was given some years ago that the " long pull" would be discontinued, but the Chairman of the Licensing Committee is com- plaining that he has good reason to believe that the practice is still in existence- To reduce the Powers of the magistrates would make them im- Potent in protecting the interests of the public,and We hope such a retrograde step will be firmly -opposed.

We commend to the notice of that large body of Our readers who are interested in the temperance movement, Mr. Henry Whitham's article on page 6, entitled "Found Drunk." Mr. Whitham has always been a keenly observant man, possessing at. the same time the capacity for turning the result of his observation to practical use. Very few men *°art have obtained a closer insight into the lives Of the workers of England and America than he has during his long experience on both idea of the Atlantic, and his views there- fore on the great national :evil of drunkenness merit thoughtful consideration. Christianity per ae Ye bluntly dubs a failure as a cure for the vice of intemperance, but unlike many critics be gives a -Practical suggestion for the lessening of the vice I.,l3deplores. Mr. Whitham boldly tilts at thoae riendly Societies and Trades Union Branches "Inch hold their meetings at the public house,and lu our opinion he has "touched the spotOr " one spot at least. There can be no questioning the harmful possibilities of this Very general practice on the part of the "30oleties named, and Mr. Whitham puts the caso in a nutshell. "If you want to do something Practical," he says "remove the Labour Union and the Friendly Societies' Lodge from the public- and its environments." These associations 'cis°. remove themselves if they like. Will they o soP The Trades Council has long since set a good example locally, but the Friendly Societies .sro .distinctly backward. There is excellent oppor- qlnity here for temperance workers both within and 'without the various Societies.

,oteille more nail has been driven into the coffin GOVerilMent by the result of the East Dorset t Where a Unionist majority of 96 has bean .11rued Into a minority of 820, and the electors .40e said Nol to a candidate bearing the rather

un-Engiish name of Van Raalte. The Conserva- tive's cognomen smacked too much of the Rand for the "Dosset " electors. Free Trade was, how- ever,the rallying cry of the Liberals,and the verdict of this largely agricultural constituency makes an important contribution to the strength of the Free Trade party. They know f all well in this part of the country the effect of Protection, and past experience leads them to fight shy of Mr. Chamberlain's proposals. Since his pronounce- ment ten months ago, there have been seventeen contested elections, including East Dorset,and the net result is a gain of six seats by the Free Trade party. A year ago 13 of the.l7 seats were held by Unionists, and four only by Liberals. To-day ten are held by Liberals and seven by supporters of the Government. If the results of • a general election were in the same proportion the Opposi- tion would gain about 180 seats, and the great majority of 1900 would ba a thing of the past.

The I wall-paper trade is just now affording a capital illustration of what tne public might safbly expect under Protection. A few years ago all the great wall-paper manufacturers formed a strong combination, and the prices of paper were very considerably increased. Naturally, the result was that foreign makers, especially the Germans, began to send us wall-paper at lower prices, and this was resented by the Wall Paper Combine, whose secretary wrote a letter to the Lancashire press demanding Protection as a remedy against this state of things. The combine is being forced by this outside competition to reduce its prices by about one-third, and we may expect to hear from this quarter bitter denunciations of the Free Trade which prevents them from extorting excessive prices from the British householder. None the less, they will meet with scant sympathy after their grasping policy has resulted in opening the home market to their foreign competitors.

Coming Events. "The Church and Social Problems" will be the Rev. H. Dunnico's subject at the service in the Royal Court Theatre to-morrow (Sunday) evening. The Rev. R. Wilkins Rees will preach on "Christ- ianity's credentials found in its helpfulness" at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church to-morrow (Sunday) morning. Under the auspices of the Chamber of Commerce, Mr. C. Lancaster (Liverpool Chamber of Commerce) will lecture at the Town Hall on Monday afternoon on "The Montreal Congress and a tour through the Dominion." _ _

A great meeting in celebration of the Bible Society Centenary will be held in the Parr Hall on Monday evening. The principal speakers will be the Bishop of Liverpool and the Rev. Dr. Watson (lan Maclaren) The annual public meeting in connection with the Free Church Council will be held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Thursday evening. Mr. C. E. Parker will preside, and the speakers will be the Rev. W. J. Townsend, D.D., and the Rev. Alexander Mackennal, B.A. D.D. At the Parish *Church on Friday evening the Rev. F. E. Powell, Vicar of Sewerby, and formerly of St. James', Latchford, will deliver an address to men on "Sunday Observance." LADIES BEST GLACE BOOTS and SHOES, UNDER the CLOCK, BRID GE-STREET.—Ad7t,

The Cost of the Inquiry. The cost of the inquiry which is now proceeding into the alleged trading with the Corporation by members of the Council is estimated at £l,OOO. This sum, which is more than a penny in the 1, will be included in the estimates to be submitted at the next meeting of the Town Council.

St. Patrick's Day. This event was duly celebrated in Warrington. There were many "Wearers of the Green" on Thurs- day and sprigs of shamrock were at a premium, the supply not being equal to the demand. At night a ball and banquet were held in celebration Of the event.

Hospital Sunday. To-morrow is Hospital Sunday, and the collections at the various places of worship will be devoted to the funds of the Warrington Infirmary. It is unneces- sary for us to say anything to commend this institu- tion to the generous support of our readers, and we hope the amount raised will be even larger than in past years. H. M. There will be a magnificent displ4y of pictorial and scientific photographs, colour photo- graphy, lantern slides, trade exhibits &c. There will also be demonstrations of the use of radium and X rays. Full particulars appear in our advertising columns.

The Price of Bread. At their next meeting, the members of the Master Bakers' Association will again consider the price of bread. We are informed that local bakers are not wishful to take undue advantage of the sudden rise in the price of flour, but if the advance should continue, they will ultimately be forced to increase the price of bread.

Death of Mrs. Margaret Crosfield. We regret to announce the death of Mrs. Margaret Crosfield, widow of the late Mr. Morland Crosfield, which took place on Monday at Little Acton, Wrex- ham. She was the mother of Mr. Ernest Crosfield and Mr. Sidney Crosfield.

PUBLIC BENEFIT, UNDER the CLOCK for Bas BOOTS in LANCA.SHIB,E.—Advt. Scientific Lecture at St. John's. The members of St. John's Literary Society enjoyed a rare treat at their weekly meeting on Thursday, 17th, inst., when Mr. R. H. Albery, B. Sc., (Rona. Vict), the physics master at the Warrington Technical Instituth, delivered a highly instructive scientific lecture, illustrated with most elaborate experiments. The lecturer fir at dealt with wireless telegraphy, and after giving a brief outline of the history and develop- ment of the electric telegraph, graphically explained the wireless system of which so much has recently been heard. With a transmitting instrument on the platform Mr. Albery sent mom alphabet signals to another part of the room where they were recorded on a receiver, the audience clearly hearing both the despatch and reception of the letters. Mr, Albery also introduced the X rays and other scientific phenomena, explaining the same in a most lucid manner. The lecture was listened to throughout with much atten- tion, Mr. Albery's powers of explanation being fully appreciated. At the close a hearty vote of thanks was accorded the lecturer for his services.

FREE TRADE v. PROTECTION.

I have followed the correspondence under this sub-heading with considerable interest and, at times, trepidation. The nature and extent of the controversy have compelled the writers to trespass at some length upon your space—and may, be, upon the patience of your readers—and if the issue of the conflict did not mean so much to the workingman one would almost tire of the Kepitition of the same stale arguments. What a chaos of plans, inconsistencies and con- tradictions ! It is really wonderful the turn for figures and phrases and even parables certain correspondents have suddenly acquired. We have even been favoured, in a contemporary, with an autobiography by a learned gentleman,—to whom we are really ,grateful for many happy hours—who in- forms us that he has been a Protectionist from "his youth up." We have certainly heard both % sides of the question, and if we are to believe all that we have read, we shall be as many-sided as the re- spected gentleman from Birmingham who is alleged to have rejuvenated the wonderful cure for all the ills that this Free-Trade country of ours is at present suffering from. One would have thought that the Tories would have prevented such. a catas- trophe, considering the losg rule they have enjoyed, but instead of getting better we find ourselves in a sorrier plight then before, and unless it is seen to we shall soon be altogether obliterated from the map. If the wanton and needless expenditure of the present incomparably bad Government was restricted, there would be no need to seek excuses for incompetence.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
Co3muNism

It is said that every syllable we breathe is written on the air, that the atmosphere is a vast wandering library, and it is within scientific imagination that all our words may be rendered back to us again. If that be true, if there is a probability of all our utterances being presented to us in some _form or other, then some ot—us are going to have a very rough time. Some years ago, when Mr. Joseph Chamber- lain was a Liberal with socialistic tendencies, and defending the programme he wished at that time to force on the Liberal Party, he said : " If the rich want their rights to be respected, as they I ought to be, they are bound in return to respect the rights of their lass fortunate brethren. It is said that these views lead straight to Communism, and that Communism is a very terrible thing. Let us under- stand each other. I, for one, have never thought it possible or expedient to bring everything down to a dead level. I have never supposed you could equalise the capacities and conditions of men. The idler, the drunkard, the criminal, and the fool must bear the brunt of their de- fects. The strong man and the able man will always be first in the race. But what I say is, that, the community as a whole, co-operating for the benefit of all, may do something to add to the sum of human happiness, may do something to make the life of all its citizens, and, above all, .of the poorest of them' somewhat better, somewhat nobler,somewhat happier.' Those remarks of his, and others to the same effect which might be quoted, show that there was a time in the history of Mr. Chamberlain when ' domestic legislation took a very prominent part in his political programme. Unfortunately for himself and for the welfare of the masses of the people h 3 allied himself with those "who toil not, neither do they spin" and his reason- ing predilections have taken a right about turn. As one of the Capitalists he is staggering humanity by his audacious attempts to sacrifice the well-being of the people for the sake of benefiting the select few. There was a time when the "public good" was his first thought. Later events have gone to prove that he has forsaken that, and 113 longer should he trade on the support given to him on account of the promises which he made—but which have not been fulfilled. He has allied himself with those who have acquired their positions in life not by merit but by birth; and, as such, they will see to it that so long as they can catch the votes of the people by the jingling of the Birmingham brass they will be able to "roost " com- fortably and I always have plenty of " run " to sport themselves and enjoy the fun. They will drop him as soon as they dare. _.

SEEN AND /COT SEEN. ,--- If Protection is to benefit the ivorkinaibaan, how is it that the artNtocracy of this country is so loud in pro- claiming it? Whilst social questions are con.testing precedence with political questions and whilst we are still to be governed by Parliament it is essential that the land-owners and capitalists should have a counter move for fear of some very funny alterations in the laws of this country. Railway Rates, Taxation of Land Values, Abolition of Royal- ties, the Housing Question all these are apt to make them: more tightly grip their holdings and fight to the death; and any suggestions as to their revision make them shudder; of course,they are the friends of the people and they know &at Pro tection is the thing. It is so nice to have ideals tha will benefit mankind in general—and themselves in particular. Let us remember that there are two things to be considered in studying the subject—that which is seen and that which is not seen. If the manu- facturer makes a big profit it does not follow that the wages of the workman will be increased proportionately. The conversion of the private company into a limited one, the election of titled personages to a place upon the directorate,— very often those who condescend to receive a large fee but who think it is infra dig to work,—the num- berless smaller capitalists who take shares . and clammer for a dividend they have never worked for, rob the workingman of what he is fully entitled to and what he has earned by the sweat of his brow. It is needless to explain how the increased cost of every- thing would tell upon him. If he complains and says that he cannot live on the wages he is getting, he will, no doubt, be told that he can "'clear out" as there are plenty of Chinese who would be glad of his job at half the wages. And so it will mean that the work- ingman will be either compelled to work for even less than he is getting now and so drag out an existence or else be forced to seek the shelter of the Work- house. That is what is not seen. . A FARCE.

It is futile to rely upon the promises of those who are profiting by the Government. lam sur- prised that our clergy have not spoken out clearly upon the question. Fancy, and in this enlightened century, our missionaries going out into ,the whole world calling upon the unsaved to accept the teach- ings of Christ, carrying with them a Bible in one hand and Protective Tariffs snorting retaliation in the other; preaching against the curse of covetousness; proclaiming honesty as the best policy; exhorting them to love their neighbours as themselves &c., &c., and all the While such rampant hypocrisy is so prevalent at home. Why the whole thing is a farce ! Surely, it is getting time that we either acknowledged the teachings of Christ as the guide not only in spiritual matters, but also in temporal or else admit that the whole thing is wrong and drop it. There ought to be some plain speaking, and there would be if it were not for the fact • that the highest bidder gets the most concessions. But the time is not far distant when we shall be men strong and true, able to fight the battles of life without the aid of such unworthy statesmen, and weak-kneed parsons. Many are be- ginning to see through the inconsistencies of some of those who imagine themselves the followers of Christ. It is ever so easy to preach against the lusts of the flesh--the difficulty arises when we endeavour to practice what we preach,—ourselves. We are com- manded to "judge not lest we be judged." We cannot however refrain from expressing our abhorr- ence of such hypocrisy and we are quite prepared to accept punishment for so doing. What is morally wrong can never be politically right." Finally if the force of democracy is to be felt it must be strong and, above all, concentrated and united. It is useless to think that the battle can be won by "numberless units each of them so preciously independent that no one of them can unite with another for a single day." Let us not sacrifice the public good for the sake of personal spleen. EDMUND OSBORNE.

MR A. J. KING SPEAKS AT STOCKTON HEATH.

ENCOURAGING SIGNS.

Mr. A. J. King, J.P., the prospective Liberal candi- date for the Knutsford division, again visited Stockton Heath on Thursday evening and addressed a large gathering in Providence Schoolroom, Chapel-lane. The occasion was a tea and entertainment promoted by the Liberal Association of the village and, thanks to the energy of the officials who had the arrangements in hand, the affair was a gratifying success. Tea was served to a large company, the following ladies presid- ing at the tables :—Mesdames Mounfield, Sherburn, Lloyd Jones A. H. Dunn, Booth, Rowbotham, England and Whitfield, Misses Mounfield, M. Bouchier, and E. Grounds. At the entertainment, Mr. C. Jackson Holmes presided and was supported by Mr. A. J. King, J.P., the Rev. J. S. Hughes, Messrs. Josiah Longland, Joseph Moundeld, E. England, A. H. Dunn, and W. H. Sher- burn.

The Chairmanalluded to the great wave of enthusiasm whiLla seemed to be passing over that suburb in the direction of Liberalism. (Applause.) That tendency was manifested not only in politics but in the conduct of their own local affairs. Success depended entirely upon organization, whether it was in the political or religious worlds, or in the carrying on of business con- cerns. He was glad to know that in Stockton Heath and Warrington the Liberal organizations were in such a happy and lively state. Speaking for the Warrington Association, he never knew a time when there was such unanimity in the party as there was to-day. Never had their good friend Crosfleld—and he was sorry he was no longer their neighbour—such a good support and strong backing as he had at the present time. The same encouraging state of things extended to Stockton Heath. If they always acted upon the true saying " United we stand ; divided we fall," there could be little doubt as t' the the result of the next election. Although it was always dangerous to prophesy, public opinion was undoubtedly on their side. (Applause.) That feeling was reflected everywhere, as exemplified by the results of the local County Council elections and the defeat of the Tory ; candidate at Knutsford. (Applause.) Then, that day they had the joyful tidings of a great Liberal victory in East Dorsetshire. (Applause.) At War- rington the Liberal party was thoroughly united. Never was there a determination to put forth greater efforts than would belbrought to bear in the next election They had all sorts of tales as to when the election might be expected. According to people ,aho were supposed to be in" the know," a dissolution should have already taken place, but as it lives, the Government had held together in a way. (Laughter.) On the other hand Conservative officials in London made the positive statement that there would be no election this year. They could not, however, disregard the defeat • of the Government the other night. (Hear, hear.) Although it was only a small thing and concerned a side issue, it showed plainly which way the wind was blowing. (Applause.) That and other events indicated,, he thought, that the Government could not hold together very much longer. He took it for granted that the next Government would be a Liberal Government. The questions which would have to be faced were of such importance to the welfare of tne country that they should be dealt with in the way that only a Liberal Government could deal with them. In regard to the fiscal policy, he did not think they would hear as much about it in the future as they had heard in the past. (Hear, hear.) There were other ' matters, such as the importation of Chinese slavery into South Africa which, he thought, would engross the attention of the country much more than the fiscal policy or any other policy that might be introdined by Mr. Chamberlain. Mr. Holmes read some of the con- ditions under which the labourers were to be employed andlsaid they were such that Englishmen would not allow any employees to submit to. The expenditure of the country, continued the Chairman, was increasing enor- mously by leaps and bounds and it would have to be checked. New sources of revenue would have, to be tapped, and they knew perfectly well that snub, sources could be tapped without interfering with tha eomfort of the people at large. In introducing Mr, King, Mr. Holmes said he had no doubt that he would shortly be the member for the division. (Applause.) Mr. A. J. King expressed sinaem regret that Mrs. King was unable to be present. It would IMO teen a I great pleasure to her to have aomaiiiihied him, but,' owing to the state of her health, it was not possible for her to undertak3 the journey. Alluding to his prob- able candidature, Mr. King pointed out that he had not yet been asked to become the candidate, but if no sub- stitute could be found he would be prepared to contest the division. (Applause.) He wished to associate him- self with the remarks of the Chairman with regard to their former neighbour, and his good friend, Mr. Crostleld. (Applause.) He felt that there was something wanting in a Liberal meeting at Stockton Heath when Mr. Crosfield was not present. He was sure they all wished him complete success in the neighbouring constituency of Warring- ton. (Applause.) Mr. Kin,* made a sympathetic allusion to the death of the Duke of Cambridge who, he said, was one of the oldest members of the royal family and had served his country for many years. He was sure they all wished to express their symnathy with the king and other memhers of the royal family in the loss they had sustained. Alluding to his Majesty's efforts to promote peace and goodwill among nations, the speaker said they hoped the King's desire that the Press throughout the world would use its influence and the ability at its command. to encourage a good feeling, would be complied with. He thought they might look forward to King Edward going down to posterity as the king of peace. Speaking of local affairs. Mr. King said it was hoped that one of ,the results of that gathering would be the formation of a Women's Liberal Association in Stockton Heath. That was a most excel- lent scheme and he wished it every success. The Education question was one which specially effected women. Not only were the majority of children in the schools girls, but something like three-fourths of the teachers were women. It was therefore desirable that the mothers of the land should have a voice in the directing of educational affairs. Under the old School Board system the ratepayers had a right to elect women on the education authorities' but lunder the new Act that right had been taken away and women could only appear on the authorities by co-option on the part of the male members. That, he contended, was not a satisfactory position. The ques- tion of Free Trade and Protection was certainly a ques- tion which above all things affected the women and children, because if food became dear and wages became lower, and the purchasing power of a sovereign was re- duced, it was by the woman and children that the strain would first be felt. It behoved every woman to use every influence to see that our great Free Trade policy should not be interfered with. He thought that for the moment Protection was scotched. They wanted, , however, to make it absolutely impossible for the demon of Protection to raise its head again in this country. (Applause.) Mr. King quoted from;a speech by the late Lord Salisbury in which he attributed the increased Protection abroad to the cost of the enormous armaments. It was almost the fulfilment of aprophecy that Protection should have raised its head in this country when we had just spent 250 millions on the waging of a war, and at a time when we had increased our peace expenditure on the military and naval forces by something like 50 per cent. in eight years. He would urge those present not to accept too readily the figures given by the other side, even when taken from the Blue Book. Mr. King dealt with figures advanced by Pro- tectionists with the object of showing that of late years there had been a great decrease of employment in the cotton and woollen trades. He showed that the decrease was almost entirely in the employment of children, and that meant that the old half-timers' system was going out and that the children were being better cared for in regard to education. Protectionists ignored the home trade which was much more important froin the point of view of employment than the export trade. In the coal-mining trade in 1891 there were, 520,000 people employed. In 1901 the number was 648,000. In the building trade 701,000 people were employed in 1891. The number employed in 1901 was 945,000. He thought that Liberals in the Knutsford division might take heart for there were many encouraging signs. Every election , which had been fought on the fiscal question since Mr. Chamberlain. introduced his proposals, had resulted in a Liberal gain. • If they had not won the seat the Conservative majority had been reduced. In their own division there had recently been County Council elec- tions. At Wilmslow a Liberal was returned by a , majority of 200, and in the stronghold of Knutsford the sitting member had been defeated by 349. (Applause.) They must not make too much of that, because Mr. Goodson, the successful candidate, proclaimed that it was not a party contest. At the same time they knew perfectly well that Mr. Egerton had the whole of the Conservative workers at his back, and he and his agent proclaimed throughout the election that it was really a party contest udder another name. At any rate the theory of the-divine right of the great family in the neighbourhood to represent the constituency in all possible ways had received a shock. What Knutsford ' had done' he thought Knutsford division might hope to I do,if the Liberals would stick to their task. (Applause.) Another encouraging fact was that Lord Stanley, of Alderley, who was an enthusiastic Liberal, had become the president of the Liberal Association of the division, ' land intended to throw himself heart and soul into the work of the party. They must not, however, be too sanguine. It would require every effort on the part of every Liberal in the division if the election was to be won, and the Central Association was doing all it could to help forward the cause. The result really depended upon the efforts of the various local committees. The Govern ment, continued Mr. King, seemed to be getting into a more hopeless mess every day. They had entirely lost the confidence of the country; they had no confidence in each, other or in their leader; and they had no settled convictions. The budget proposals were not to be introduced until after Easter. He supposed the budget was one of those things they were in no hurry to bring in. (Laughter.) Heunderstoodthe Chancellor of the Exchequer would have to provide something

like an additional seven millions of taxes, and he could well believe that he did not know where to turn to find them. One thing was certain— that he would not be allowed to have resource to the duty of is. on corn which helped the previous Chancellor of the Exchequer out of his diffi- culty. Mr. King endorsed everything that had been said against the introduction of Chinese labour into South Africa. When we entered upon the war, one of the prospects which he thought reconciled many people to it, was that it would open up an enormous field for Isurplus white labour in South Africa. They knew now ' that no white labourers need apply. If the labour was not to be black,it must ibetyellow.lt was not solely a ques tion of being able to pay 100 and 150 per cent, dividends on the mines. These Jews and great mining capitalists wished to run South Africa with slave labour which would have no voice in the management of the country. He was very much encouraged to find over and over again that people who differed from him on the Govern- ment's policy in regard to the war, now admitted that they had been grievously disappointed and duped, and that if they had known what they knew now, they would never have supported this war policy. (Applause.) A very enjoyable progsamme was submitted. Songs were pleasingly rendered by Madame Williams Rhys and Miss Ethel Dunn. Mr. J. S. Rhys acquitted himself creditably as an elocutionist, and Master Arthur Dunn contributed a violin solo. Miss Cissie Gillies and Mr. A. H. Dunn discharged the duties of accompanists, and Miss Gillies gave two pianoforte solos. The Chairman, Mr. King, the artistes, and all who had assisted were cordially thanked on the proposition of Mr. Longlaud, seconded by the Rev. J. S. Hughes. Mr. Hughes expressed the hope that the result of the County Council elections would encourage them to be more active in their endeavour to win the seat in the Parliamentary contest. (Applause).

THE SHIP CANAL APPEAL

EFFECT OF THE MOMENT. The Position of the Corporation.

The decision of the Court of A.ppeal in the action of Crosfield and others v the Manchester Ship Canal Company (which we publish elsewhere) is difficult to understatid, but so far as we can make it out it means that the Corporation as one of the . plaintiffs is dis- missed from the action on the ground that their only remedy as a Corporation is by reference to an arbitrator under the agreement existing be- tween the Ship Canal Company and the Cor- poration. Mr. Justic3 Byrne in the first Court decided that the Corporation had a right of action apart from the reference to arbitration, but that opinion was based in a great measure on the ground that the defendants did not object to the juris- diction of the Court before the ease was down for trial, and that their neglect amounted to a waiver, and that it was then too late to attempt to oust the jurisdiction of the Court. Mr. Justice Bryne also decided that the traders had an independent right of action and were not bound by the reference to arbitra- tion under a contract t 3 which they were not parties. On appeal, two of the three judges agreed that the Corporation had no ground of action, but were bound by the reference to arbitration. In passing we may say that the Corporation were advised before the action was commenced by Mr. A. Macmorron, K.C., a counsel recognised as a leading authority in municipal govern- ment, that the Corporation had a right af action and consequently: the Corporation joined, on that advice, the other plaintiffs in the action. With regard to the posi- tion of the traders in the action, the Court of Appeal has in effect decided that they have a right of action, independent of the Corporation, but that their right is governed by an arbitration clause in the Ship Canal Company's Act of 1835, which states that all disputes arising under that Act shall be settled by reference to an arbitrator. Two of the judges lathe Court of Appeal are of opinion that before the action can proceed the question as to whether the company has or has not carried out their obligations under the agree- ment with the Corporation must first be settled by an arbitrator. The judges do not dismiss the action with the traders but stay it until the arbitrator to be appointed has made his award. Lord Justice Cozens Hardy was emphatic in his opinion, in which he differed from the majority of the Court, that the traders had an independent right of action and that the appeal as against them ought obe di Isaacaqd 1"11.9 qqt.t9.ll I allowed to proceed. It will therefore be aeon aS matters stand at present that both Mr. Justice Byrne and Lord Justice Cozens Hardy have given judgment in favour of the traders while two other judges—Lord Justice Vaughan Willigms and Stirling have expressed a con- rary opinion. The position of the traders is anomalous because while all the judges have now agreed to their having a right of action, the Court of Appeal have stated that it cannot be exercised until the Corporation have proceeded to arbitration and obtained the arbi- trator's decision.lf the Corporation refuse so go to:arbi- tration the traders have no remedy,and will be unable to to proceed with the present actionor any other action. The fact that the Corporation are struck out as one of the plaintiffs is not a matter of serious importance and does not effect the main issue. It is immaterial whether the Corporation or the traders compel the Ship Canal Company to carry out their duties. The dismissal of the Corporation will only affect the Corporation so far 'as their portion of the costs is concerned. We understand that the plaintiffs in the action have lodged a petition for an appeal to the House of Lords in the• hope of getting the decision of the Court of Appeal over-ruled. The question of costs is a sorioua matter. The Court of Appeal has stated that all costs incurred after the Compagly raised the question of ouster jurisdiction in the firgt court must be borne by the plaintiffs. If the decision is reversed in the House of Lords costs will, of course, follow the event. In face of the decision it is hardly probable that the Corporation will support the Ship Canal Company in their proposed bill to carry out works at Walton. AN ANSWER TO ALDERMAN SOUTHERN. Mr. H. Roberts, J.P., has addressed the following letter to the" Manchester Evening News " in reply to the remarks of Alderman Southern (which we publish on; 'another page) at the Manchester City Council on Wednesday : Sir,—lf the speech of Aldermen Southern at'the last meeting of the Manchester City Council is correctly reported, it is, in at least two particulars, an unjustifi- able and regrettable utterance. For a man of Alder- man Southern's wideexperience to make a statement so highly controversial when, as he must know, this import- ant case is still sub judice (as an appeal to the House of Lords is certain) was most indiscreet, but when he goes even further, and claims that the judgment in the 'Court of Appeal decides that the claim of the War- rington traders for" damages for all that might have happened during 'a long period" was "recognised" by Lord justice Vaughan Williams as "not a just proposi- tion and therefore the appeal was allowed," he, Alder- man Southern, made a statement contrary to fact, as no such point was before the Court of Appeal for decision The Ship Canal Company have won in the Appeal Court upon one point and one point only,and that of a purely technical nature, namely, that the court has no jurisdiction until the question of fact in dispute for so many years, as to whether the Ship Canal Company have or have not fulfilled their dredging obligations has been decided by an arbitrator. Should an arbitrator agree upon this point, with the judgment of Mr. Justice Byrne, in whose court the whole matter was thrashed out in the fullest detail (which fact makes Mr. Justice Byrne's decision of great importance), then the traders, who are not dis- missed from the action by the Court of Appeal, can proceed with their claims. As I have been in the fore- front of all the negotiations between the Manchester Ship Canal Company and the Warrington Corporation and traders for eighteen years, I can unhesitatingly ' claim that I have done everything in my power to maintain the most friendly relations between those whom I represent and the Manchester Ship Canal Com- pany, and I regret therefore all the more that a con- struction should have been put upon our action which is not consistent with this fact.—Yours,

H. 'ROBERTS.

Managing Director, Messrs. Jos. Crosfield and Sons, Limited, Warrington.

March 17, 1904,

Local Transport Company's Affairs. In the Liverpool Registry of the High Court, on Monday, before Mr. Registrar Cleaver, the public ex- amination under the Companies Acts was continued of Mr. Ernest Harrison Forwood, who was a director in the Manchester and Liverpool Transport Company Limited.— Witness was cross-examined as to the taking over of the Bishop's Wharf and Shipping Com- pany, Limited, Warrington, and he said that Mr. Carson, the chairman of that company, saw him (witness) regarding the amalgamation. He believed the reason Mr. Carson desired the amalgamation was that they wanted more capital. As a result of the working of this branch of the com- pany, thousands of pounds were lost every year. The firm was carrying on that branch now, and they were now making ends meet. At the time this company was taken over by the Transport Company he did not know its ramifications, and therefore he left it in the hands of those who had been managing it. It was supervised by him(witness) from London.—Mr. Greer: Your firm has now got all the assets of the Warrington company? Witness, in reply stated that since possession had been taken on behalf of himself as debenture holder, some of the assets of the company had been sold. The wharf and warehouses in Manchester had realised about £23,000 and some barges had also been sold, but he could not say what they had realised. None of the Warrington assets had been realised. The examination was con- laded.

SCHOOLS OVERCROWDED. ADVICE TO THE WARRINGTON EDUCATION

COMMITTEE,

H.M. Inspectors' Views. At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee of the Education authority, Mr. H. M. Richards, H.M. Inspector, expressed his views with reference to the general condition of the public elementary schools in the Borough. Mr. Richards pointed out that he could not conceal from them the fact that a great deal had to be done in Warrington. Things in Warrington for a. long time past had been in a stagnant condition. War- rington had not had an edncation rate and they would have to pay now for that luxury to a considerable extent. Accommodation, he thought, was the most pressing need in Warrington. They were forcing children by legal penalties into unhealthy surroundings. They were herding them together, and this was not only a danger to the health of the children, but a danger to the health of the commtmity. The chances of epidemic and disease were increased. Then there was another serious conse- quence; the schools being crowded, the attend ance was bad and the authority lost Government grants. Owing to limited accommodation the places in schools were generally taken up by the better class children, so that the very poorest children were neglected, with a result that the children of that part of the com- munity it was most necessary to civilise did not go to school at all. The staffing of the schools also became more difficult, because teachers would only come to schools when they were pleasant. Lastly, it imperilled the Government grant' and in the future it would imperil that Government grant much more than in the past. In concluding his review of the educational needs of Warrington, Mr. RICHARDS states that if the over- crowding were removed he should not insist upon alterations being carried out, but if the overcrowding were going to exist, not only these alterations, but much more serious ones would have to be exacted from the elementary school managers. EDUCATION IN WARRINGTON. The Salaries of Head Teachers.

The Warrington Education Committee will hold its monthly meeting on Monday evening. The minutes of theiSchool Management sub-committee contain a large number of increases of teachers salaries. gianted in accordance with the scale adopted by the Committee on May 18th, 1903.

At the Committee Meeting correspondence was sub- mitted from the Warrington Head Teachers' Associa- tion, ueging that the past services of the head teachers should be recognised by the Committee in applying the scale .of salaries recently adopted by the Committee. particularly in the cases of the head mistresses. It was proposed "That the salaries of head teachers who have already served four years as head teachers in schools in the Borough, be increased by £lO annually, until the maximum provided by the scale is reached." This proposal having been voted upon and rejected it was resolved that while the committee feel that length of service cannot be taken into account in fixing the salaries of the head teachers, they are prepared to give the most careful consideration to say special cases that may be brought to their notice. It was decided that the Easter holidays for the elementary and secondary schools should commence on Wednesday evening, March 30th, the elementary schools to re-open on Monday, April 11th, and the secondary school on Tuesday, April 12th.

AFTER THE FOOTBALL MATCH. Struggle In a Railway Carriage.

A Warrington football enthusiast who journeyed to Swinton on Saturday succeeded in making matters rather too lively for his fellow passengers on the return journey. The result was that yesterday morning the magistrates at the Borough Police Court had to hear a charge against James Mather, 15, Oliver-street, of assaulting Thomas Mullin, 107, Winwick-road. According to the statement of Mr. Browne, who appeared for the complainant, the men witnessed the match, spent some time in Manchester, and started for home by the 9.30 train from Central Station. The com- partment was quite full. Soon after leaving the city, defendant began to be abusive to Mullin, telling him that "he was always flashing his money about, and lay- ing big odds on Warrington," and that he wanted throw- ing through the window. Complainant told defend- ant that he had never bet with him in his life, and 114 could do what he liked with his own money. Defendant made several threats to strike Mullin, but his friends pursuaded him to sit down. When the train was nearing Warrington, complainant was taking his mackintosh off the rack when defendant struck him on the left eye. He then knocked him down, kicked him on the forehead several times, and got on the top of him. When the train stopped a railway porter had to release the complainant who in consequence of the asss.ulthad been off work all week.

Defendant's version was that he was talking about the match and complainont kept" pulling his sovereigns out" and wanting to bet him. When near Warrington complainant butted him three or four times with his head, and he (defendant) then struck back.

The magistrates believed complainant's story, which was supported by the evidence of a railway inspector and imposed a fine of ss. and costs.

A RATEPAYER'S GRIEVANCE.

Protest Against the System of Rate Collection.

Thursday was the day set apart at the Warrington Police Court for the hearing of summonses for non- payment of the borough rates. A case in which an interesting point was raised was that of Mr. John Griffiths, School-street, who was summoned in respect of £2O 9s. 10d. He admitted that the rate was due and said he offered to pay it on Wednesday, but the authorities would not accept the money because he refused to pay 2s. 6d. the cost of the sunimons, For several years, he said, he had paid the rates regularly, and he produced vouchers for 1901-2-3 showing that the dates on which the rates were paid in those years were. the 16th and 17th of the months of March and Septem- ber. On Monday he received a summons for the past half year's rates. He had made arrangements to pay the money during the week, and he considered it was out of all reason and contrary to common sense, that business men should receive such a peremptory notice from Mr. Woodcock.

Mr. W. H. Woodcock, the borough collector, said that on February 22nd he served Mr. Griffiths with a second notice stating that if the rate was not paid by Monday February 29th, proceedings would be taken. At the foot of the notice it was stated that no further intima- tion would be sent. He heard nothing from Mr. Griffiths. It might be a fact that he had paid regularly on a certain date in other years. Mr. Woodcock said the rate was due on the demand being made and, after serving a second notice on Mr. Griffiths, he had n 9 alternative but to take proceedings. It would make rate collecting an absolute impossibil- ity if everyone chose to pay when they liked. Mr. Griffiths said he had no intention to thwart the authorities. He produced a cheque for the rates. , The magistrates decided to remit the cost of the sunemons, but asked Mr. Griffiths to be a little more punctual in his payment in future. Mr. Griffiths: I shall want to satisfy myself further on the matter.

STOCKTON HEATH.

PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE., The monthly meeting of this committee was held in the National Schools 'on Thursday evening under the presidency of Mr.G. Pilling. There ware also present: —Messrs. Whitley, Pemberton, Clarke, Pearson, Mclntyre, Fairhurst, Swinton (surveyor), Farrington (inspector of nuisances) and W. E. Hough (deputy clerk).

THE NIGHTSOIL CONTRACT. The Deputy Clerk read a letter from Mr. Lunt, who until recently held the nightsoil contract, complaining of the deduction of £6 2s. 2d. from his last account of £6O 10s. He contended that, according to the inspec- -tor's statement, the pails were left in very good con- dition, and referred in detail to the various items in- eluded in the deduction. The Deputy Clerk said the deductions were made in accordance with the committee'* instructions. The reason the contract was terminated was that the work was not being satisfactorily done. The committee instructed the Clerk to write to Mr. Lunt explaining the deductions, and stating that they .could not see their way to make any further payment.

THE LIGHTING OF THE VILLAGE. In accordance with instructions given at the last -meeting the clerk wrote to the Warrington Corporation requesting them to furnish the lamps at Stockton Heath and Walton with incandescent burners. Mr. W. S. Haddock, the gas engineer, now wrote stating that the alteration would necessitate a large expense and had better be delayed until next season as it would incur double expense if done now. Moreover the Corporation ceased lighting the lamps for the -summer months on May 14th. The Committee decided to defer the matter for the present.

TWENTY STEPS BRIDGE. The Clerk to the Cheshire County Council wrote -stating that he had suggested to the Engineer of the Manchester Ship Canal Company that the footpaths forming the approach to Twenty Steps Bridge, which the company were under obligation to mQintain and re- pair, should be formed of concrete flags. As the adjoining footpaths, which the County Council had to maintain, were paved with blue bricks, the liability of the Council and the company would be clearly defined, and the alteration would also be of advantage to foot passengers. The letter was considered satisfactory. THE SURVEYOR'S REMUNERATION. The committee granted the usual remuneration of £lO to Mr. Swinton for wo* done in the parish during the past year.

ELECTION OF GUARDIANS. -- Contests at Latchford and Great Sankey.

On Monday, March 28th, the triennial election of .Guardians will take place. There will be five contests in the Warrington Union' one in the Borough and four in adjoining townships. The election in the Borough is in Latchford Ward, where the two candidates are Miss Broadbent, of the Hollies, and Mr. George Pilling, who has been Chairman of the Board. In her address, Miss Broadbent states that she has for ten years visited at the Workhouse, chiefly in the Infirmary, and has been a member of the Brabazon Society since its commence ment. She has been for ten years the Brabazon Secre tary, and has been greatly pleased to see what a cheer- ing effect the work has had on the spirits of the invalids, some of whom are condemned to pass the remainder of their days within the hospital walls. She has ample time at her disposal, and, if elected, will devote herself energetically to all the duties coming within the sphere of a guardian of the poor, especially inconnection with the care of the women the girls, the children and the aged sick and infirm of both sexes, who together com- prise the vast majority of those who have to be dealt with under the poor law. She points out that as there are 160 women and a large number of children to be .cared for, there is plenty of room for two or three women on the Board. She advocates the strictest economy, and promises to endeavour to safeguard the ratepayers' interests by the most judicious expenditure of their money. It was at one time anticipated that there would be a contest in Orford Ward. Three nominations had been .sent in, but at the last moment, Mr. James Robinson, the present member, and Mr. James Henry Smethust- retired.The result is that Mrs. Wilkinson, of 222, Orford lane, becomes a member of the Board without a contest. In St. John's Ward the Rev. Father Wilson, who has been a member of the Board for three years, and the Rev. Dr. Mercer were nominated. The Rev. Father Wilson has now retired in favour of the Rev. Dr. Mercer, who is returned unopposed. Another new member of the Board is Mr. Robert Bolton who has 'had a walk over in Fairfield Ward. Outside the Borough, there will be contests at Great Sankey, Haydock and Newton (2). In Great Sankey, where the position of Guardian carries with it a seat .on the Warrington Rural District Council, Mr. Thomas Halton is opposed by Mr. George Thompson. Mr. Fred Thomas Jefferson was also nominated but he withdrew from the contest. In Haydock, Mrs.ETayward, wife of Dr.llayward,is a candidate for the seat vacated by the Rev. J. H. Riddette. She is opposed by Mr. William Flanagan. There are two contests in Newton. The return of Mr. E. L. Bentas tharepresentative of Wargrave Ward is .opposed by Mr. Astley King, and in Viaduct Ward, Mr. John Clarke's return is opposed by Mr. Thomas Lythgoe POLLING ARRANGEMENTS. Polling will commence at 12 o'clock noon on Monday, the 18th inst., and close at 8 p.m. There will be two polling stations in Latchford Ward, one at St. James's school, Old-road, and the other at Christ Church school, Wash-lane. The ballot boxes will be conveyed to Mr. Sutton's office and the result declared there the same 'evening. At Great Sankey the polling station will be at the public schools, Liverpool-road. Mr. Kerfoot will be the presiding officer. The votes will be counted in the schoolroom and the result declared the same evening. At Earlestown and Haydock the polling arrangements will be made by the Clerk to the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Council.

PARISH COUNCIL ELECTION AT BURTONWOOD. Tl, election of the Parish Councillors will take place •on ;Monday, March 28th. Twelve candidates will go to the poll for the seven vacancies. THE REVISING BARRISTER AND "SIR WALTER RALEIGH."

Lecture at the Old Academy.

Mr. James T. Foard, the revising barrister, visited Warrington on Thursday evening with quite a different object in view than that of revising the voters' lists. His mission was to deliver a lecture before the mem- bers of the Warrington Academy on "Sir Walter Raleigh, the discoverer of Virginia' and he treated the subject in a manner which delighted the small gather- ing thatassembled to listen to hun.—Mr. Henry Roberts, J.P., presided, audit) his introductory remarks he could not refrain from alluding to his former acquaintance with Mr. Foard, when assisting in revising the lists on behalf of the Liberal Association. "I looked upon him in those days," said Mr. Roberts, "as a somewhat. awe-inspiring individual, learned in the law, and I have not," he proceeded to say, "lost altogether my sense of his power in that respect." Mr. Foard, in alluding to Raleigh's colonising instincts, observed that earth hunger was a phantom which had deluded many states and countries since the world began, and without stating whether this fugitive form of patriotism was an iaspiring pursuit or a tran- sient lunacy, he pr )posed to place before them a necessarily imperfect portrait of one of the real founders of our empire, who 300 years ago, realised to some extent the dream of a Great Britain beyond the seas. The early life of Raleigh was briefly reviewed and his marvellous ascent into power as a favourite at the court of Elizabeth was partly ascribed to his poetic adulation of Elizabeth and to his skill as a courtier. The incident attri- buting an act of gallantry to him, in throwing his embroidered cloak in a puddle in order that the queen might pass, probably happened, but added Mr. Foard there was no reliable confirmation of it. After referring to the many Royal favours which were granted him and his persistent love of acquisition, the lecturer pointed out that in 1584. Sir Walter obtained a charter of col- onization and unsuccessfully attempted the settlement of Virginia, in one or two following sears. He also ob- tained a large share of the forfeited Irish estates and introduced here the cultivation of the potato and other plants. A brief allusion was made to Raleigh having incurred the quem's displeasure by marrying one of her maids ofihonour,the daughter of Sir Nicholas Throckmor- ton and the lecturer passed on to Raleigh's expedition to Guiana. To discover the fabled El Dorado or region of gold this expedition was planned. Rileighembarked in 1595 and reached the Orinoco, but was obliged to return after having done little more than take a formal possession of the country in the name of Elizabeth. On the accession of James 1., the King's mind was poisoned against Raleigh, whom he deprived of all his offices. Falsely accused of complicity in Lord Cobham's treason in favour of Arabella Stuart, Raleigh was brought to trial and sentenced to death. He was, however, reprieved, and during his twelve years' confinement in the Tower he devoted himself to literature, and wrote a "History of the World" and other works. His subsequent release and his disastrous second enterprise to the Orinoco, in which he attacked the Spaniards, was followed by his execution on his former sentence. Mr. Foard quoted Macauley's description of Sir Walter Raleigh as "soldier, sailor, courtier, orator, statesman and philosopher." His varied experience was likened by Mr. Foard, t "the bounds and rebounds of this tennis ball of fortune." A discussion ensued. Alderman BENNETT proposed 1. t vote of thanks to the lecturer, and referred to his visa to Raleigh's home in Ireland. Dr. NADEN, in seconding, said it.had never been made so clear to him before that Raleigh was a man who deserved so well of his country, and yet one who was so badly rewarded. A man to whom we owed so much ought to have received more consideration. He was glad Mr. Foard had brought Sir Walter Raleigh to their notice, and rehabilitated him in their affections. Mr. WOODCOCK supported the vote of thanks, and said as a writer and one who helped to spread the powers of England abroad Raleigh came before them as a prominent figure. It was worthy of notice at the present time that Raleigh's "Observation on trade and Commerce" was a Free Trade pamphlet and was sup- pressed as being unsuitable to the times. (Laughter.) The vote was carried unanimously and Mr. FOA.RD briefly acknowledged the compliment. A vote of thanks was passed to the Chairman on the proposition of Mr. HALL, seconded by Mr. FAIRHURST. The CHAIRMAN, in responding, said he was surprised to hear Alderman Bennett state that it was2o years since he took an active and serious part in politics.He fancied he had seen utterances of Mr. Bennett much more recently, which appeared to him to be on political topics. (Laughter.) Mr. Bennett's reference to the fickleness of the public must have been inspired by the esult of the East Dorset election. (Loud laughter.)

PASSIVE RESISTANCE: NEW STYLE. A new variety of passive resister has turned up at Stockport. A defendant summoned ou Thursday for non-payment of poor rate, said he was r!uassive resister, but not of the Education Act. He paid two shillings per week towards his mother's support, and he claimed that under the circumstances he ought to pay :no more poor rate.

CURED BY VENO'S LIGHTNING COUGH CURE. -- Mrs. MARTIN HILL, The Hem, Shifnal,Salop, writes: "I have had what the doctor called bronchial asthma, and he said I would never be cured, but thanks be to God and Veno's Lightning Cough Cure I am better now than I have been for six years. I can lie down and have a good night's rest, which seems a God-send to me. I had tried everything that people told me of but nothing did me a bit of good. During the Winter I had to sit in a chair for months at a time because of the shortness of breath. All that has gone now, and I am looking very well. THE POWERS OF LICENSING JUSTICES.

PROTEST AGAINST THREATENED RESTRICTIONS. Mr. F. MonlTs:-i.P. and the Long Pull."

A public me3ting promoted by the Warrington Temperance Federation was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey street, on Wednesday evening, to protest against " the proposal to take away powers long held ,by licensing magistrates." Mr. F. Monks, J.P. pre- • sided over a fair attendance. He was supported on the platform by the Revs. J. F. Matthews, C. Harvey Cook, John Yonge, G. H. Carnson, and J. S. Hughes, Messrs. T. Birchall, J.P. J. W. Hayward, J.P. E. J. Guest, J,P. E. Boreland (Liverpool), C. E. Parker, and W. H. Sherburn (secretary). The local branch of j the British Women's Temperance Association was well represented._ At the outset

The 'Rev. J. F. gkrrilEW3 said that when the Temperance Federation decided to hold that meeting, their first idea was that the meeting should not be a meeting of temperan” people only, but that it should, as far as:possible, be a meeting of the citizens of their dear old town. Having decided oa that point, they thought the very best thins to do would be to ask the Chairman of the local Licensing Bench to take the chair. (Applause.) They knew tint when Mr. Nionks came among his fellow-citizens, he was not only sure of a very warm and cordial welcome, but that what- ever he had to say carried exceeding weight for the simple reason that they all knew him to be a good man. (Applause.)

LETTERS OF APOLOGY. VIE RECTOR'S OPINION.

Mr.StrEanurts than raid several latters of apology for non attendance.

Miss Bessie Dutton wrote: Ich in my own heart most earnestly protest against any bill which may lessen the discretionary power of magistrates in refusing licences, and feel confident that any attempt to pass such a bill will arouse indignation and opposition which it will be impossible to allay. The Rector (Rev. Ciao!' Willis) wrote : I have a special service on Wednesday evening and cannot attend a public meeting that night. If I had been free I would certainly have attended and expressed ray opinion in favour of a continuance of the present system of grant- ing or refusing licences. I have perfect confidence in the fairness and wisdom of the local magistrates. Mr. Ziba Armitage, who WAS away from home, wrote: My sympathies are eatirely with the object of the meeting, and I trust that the Prime Minister will be made to feel that he cannot tamper with the discre- tion of the magistrates in this matter, without incur- ring the displeasure of the law-abiding portion of the community. Mr. H. Roberts wrote: lam afraid I cannot con- veniently attend to-night's meeting as 'should have liked to enter my strong protest against any attempt to restrict the powers at present possessed by the licens- ing justices. If any alteration is needed it is most certainly in the direction of the amplification of such powers rather than in their reduction. I also feel very strongly that Quarter Sessions justices ought to be deprived of any power to reimpose upon any district, licences which for good cause have bean refused by the local magistrates. Mr. FRED STOTT wrote that owing to other engage- ments he could not be present. "My sympathies" he said" are entirely with the objects of the meeting, and as a representative of the working classes, I can assure you that our desire is to have a larger control of the drink traffic through our public representative. We therefore strongly protest against any proposal which will decrease that control, and increase the power of the brewers and their allies." The Rev. R. WiLKENs REE 3 WIS unable to attend owing to a meeting at Lymm. "I am," he wrote, "in very strong sympathy with the object of the meeting and sincerely hope that the protest made will be most emphatic and quite unanimous." The Rev. Aorley Stevenson had written stating that until the Government proposals were more definitely stated he preferred to withhold any expression of opinion. Unfortunately, said Mr. SHF:RBURN, they very soon found that was just a moment too late. Other letters of apology had been received from Councillors L. Ellison and EL Taornton. THE CEIXIIINIAN'S SPEECH.

The CIIIIRMAN said that when Mr. Matthews asked him to take the chair he had no hesitation in doing so, because after more than twenty years' experience on the Licensing Committee, he thought he was in a posi- tion to speak with some authority as to their policy and the result. (Applause.) As far as Warrington was concerned, he did not think there was the slightest need for any interference on the ground of undue hard- ship in carrying out the law as it stood. He ventured to say that there was not apublican in Warrington, who had lived here during the last twenty years, who could with any degree of fairness; say that he had suffered in the slightest degree from any action of the magistrates If a publican had been fined, or if anything had been done to restrict his action, it had been in accordance with the law and in the interest of the public welfare, , and that, he held,was the paramount, duty of the magis- trates to attend to. The restrictions imposed had been in accordance with the law, and for the comfort and advantage of licence holders. He believed he was correct in saying that during the twenty years that he had been a member of the Licensing Committee no new licence had been granted. In 1883 there was one fully- licensed public-house to every 333 inhabitants pf the borough. There were then 135 beerhouses and fully licenced houses whereas to-day there were 123. Taking the population at 67,000 it worked out to an average of 545 per head of the population. That had been done without any injustice to anyone, and with good results as far as the inhabitants were concerned. As far as the action of the Warrington justices was concerned, there was not the slightest need for any interference, and he thought they were quite right in resenting interference on the part of the Government, at the instigation of the brewers. (Hear, hear.) He was glad to say that within the last two or three years the off-licences ' had been placed under the control of the magistrates. Mr. Gladstone, he thought, never committed a bigger blunder in the • whole course of his life, than when he passed the Bill authorizing off-licences. (Hear,hear.) He (Mr. Monks) believed the off-licences were worse than the fully- licensed houses or beerhouses, because they lent them- selves more to secret drinking. He hoped the time would come when the magistrates would be able to deal drastically with off-licence holders if they found them violating the law. (Applause.) Some time ago the Licensing Committee specially requested that the system of the long pull should be stopped,and a sort of promise was given to , the Bench that that would be done. He, however, had it on the very best authority that the long pull was still in practice in Warrington, and he did not hestitate to say that if it continued he thought] it would be a just ground for refusing a licence.—(applause)—because, in his opinion, it was an evasion and an illegal act on the part of the licencee. He was not going to blame the publican any more than he blamed anyone else. The publican was to a certain extent the creature of circumstances. They were not now dealing with the old-fashioned system under which a man brewed beer and there was competition: not in giving so much of it, but in giving the best beer which did not do anything like the harm that the bear now sold might do. Under the tied-house system it was an impossibility for any magistrate or police officer to ascertain the amount of pressure that was brought to bear upon the individual licence-holder to sell as much beer as he possibly could. Therefore he held that if the long pull system were con- tinued, it would be a right and proper thing for the magistrates to strike not only at the licence-holder but at the man who owned the house, because it was for him to see that the house was properly conducted, and that the proper quantity of beer which was paid for was sold, and no more. (Applause.)

A RESOLUTION OF PROTEST.

Mr. T. BIRCEIALL moved: "That this . meeting o citizens of Warrington piptests most strongly against any reduction of the discretionary powers so long exer- cised by the licensing magistrates with respect to the free and unquestionable right of refusal to grant or re- new licences for the sale of intoxicating liquors which, in the interest of public welfare anl morality, the magistrates may deem it expldient to refuse, such re- duction of powers being in direct opposition to the vital laws of progressive government which ever tend towards increasing the powers of local self-control." He thought in regard to the threatened legisla- tion on the powers of licensing justices, the Government had yielded in a most dastardly way to the pressure of the trade. He did not want to say any hard things of the trade, but it was not like other trades,and wanted specially looking after.
