"WHY DON'T YOU DRINK VI-00C311 ?"

Every working man and woman should be asked this question daily. The opinion of the Ref. F. F. Taylor, of Suddenham near Manchester, who writes :—" I like Dr. Tibbles' Vi-Cocoa very much, and have used it every day. I believe it is all you represent it to be. It is far away to be preferred to tea, coffee, and other cocoas," is the opinion of thousands. You must have read the striking testimony that is being publisheddaily respecting the merits of Vi-Cocoa. And yet you have not tried it. We advise you to do so at once.

Dr. Tibbles' Vi-Cocoa can be obtained in 6d. packets and 9d. and Is. G 1 tins from all grocers and stores ; or the Proprietors will send you free a dainty sample tin upon receipt of a post card to 60, Bunhill-row,London, E.C., asking them to do so.

llt had always been found to be a dangerous trade and for centuries they had had more legislation, he thought, lin regard to the management of this trade, than with respect to any other. He did not always blame the publican. The fact was that he was selling an article which,if the Angel Gabriel sold it,would be mischievous (Hear, hear). He was not going to give any opinion as to the wisdom or otherwise of taking intoxicating drink, but this he did know, and there was no getting over the fact—that the greater the sale, the more disastrous the results. (Hear hear.) He had taken a great deal of interest in the work of the War- rington bench in. dealing with various crimes, and during the last magisterial year, ending in November, he tabulated the various cases of lawlessness which came before him. In round figures, he adjudicated on 830 cases. Of these 253 were "drunks," 203 resulted from drinking, and 1.61 were other cases, such as school attendance prosecutions. They would therefore see that the cases resulting from drinking were three to one against the others. It mubt also be borne in mind that were the parents sober and thrifty, many of the school attendance cases would not have to be brought before the Court. It was very requisite that the licensing justices should devote a great deal of time to their duties and so be able to stand between the public and the publicans for the protection of the public. It was a great mistake to think that their powers needed curtailing. They wanted, if anything, increasing. The magistrates protested most energetically that they had dealt mildly with cases, as far as the publicans were concerned. The tendency had not been on the side of drastic restriction, but rather on the side of leniency. He was hoping that the threatened legisla- tion would end in smoke. The more they protested and the better likely it was to do so. He thought the Gov- ernment was beginning to see that the country was roused If some of their Church of England friends would for once stand and be temperance wen and temperance re- formers before political opponents, he thought they would be doing a very good servios. (Applause). He was afraid sometimes that they talked one way and voted the other, but this time they would not do so. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19040319_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
AlEt. E. J. GUEST AND THE LOCAL BENCH.

Mr. E. J. GUEST seconded. He was. he belidved, regarded by his fellow justices as somewhat of a fanatic. He went a great deal further than Mr. Monks and many others. He regarded public-houses as public nuisances—(applause)—and the fewer they had of them the better. (Applause.) There was no question that landowners who had the power were very careful not to have too many public houses on their land. In many cases they restricted the number to one, and in some cases they had none at all. His feeling in the matter was that the public ought to have local option. (Rear, hear.) He had a great respect for his fellow magistrates, but they were not representative men. They were not amendable to public opinion in the sense of being voted for, and therefore he thought the public ought to have the same power to say whether they would have public- houses in their midst, as the land owner had the power to say whether he would have them on his land or not. Mr. Monks had told them that the number of licences:in Warrington had not increased. That might be so; but there had been bar- gaining between the magistrates and the brewers. Licences had been given up in congested areas where they were not needed and where, he supposed,they were not productive, and new licences had been given in dis- tricts where they were not wanted—(hear,hear)—and in districts where the owners of houses and ratepayers pro- tested against them.He had always raised his voice against that,but, he had never been listened to.He had always been a minority. He certainly thought that the magistrates, so long as they remained between the public and the brewer, ought not to have their powers restricted. They certainly ought to have as much power as they had now. He wished they would use that power—(hear. hear)—as they might use it. They had begun to use it in Liverpool, Birmingham, and other places, with very happy results, and he trusted that the magistrates generally would, so long as they had the power, use it better than they had done in the past. He was looking forward to the time when they would not be governed so much by expediency as by justice to everyone. (Ap- plause.) WHAT HAS BEEN DONE IN LIVERPOOL.

Mr. E. BORELAND asked his hearers not to put the hand of the clock back 400 years. For 400 years magis- trates had been endowed with powers in regard to licences—powers that were given expressly for the pro- tection of the public, and not for the protection of the publican. (Hear, hear.) The brewer as a seller of intoxicating drink under a tied house system never came into the minds of the legislature at all. He did not think the people at the beginning of the twentieth cen- tury were prepared to go backward on this important question. (Hear, hear.) He quite admitted that for the last few years they had been so accustomed politically to go backward, that to go forward at all would be a•strange experience. But after all it was time to cry "Halt." (Hear, hear.) We had gone back far enough. The licensing question was one that concerned•those who drank and those who did not. He believed the action of the Liverpool licens- ing bench had had no little part in moving the liquor traffic to interview the Prime Minister. Up to the beginning of last year,the Liverpool mvistrates closed 418 liquor shops and the liquor men were not so muchl concerned about what had taken place but were rathet frightened of what was coming. Mr. Balfour scolded the benches of magistrates for doing their manifest duty and practically asked the Quarter Sessions to upset the decisions of the local benches of magistrates. Mr. Balfour also promised that the Government would introduce legislation to prevent magistrates from carrying on their work. Many benches of magis- trates had held their hands in consequence of the threat but he was glad to know that' the Warrington bench had done something this year, in taking away music licences. (Hear, hear.) Public-houses ought not to have any adventitious aid in the shape of music and dancing and amusements of that 'kind to encourage drinking. (Hear, hear.) The licensing committees of Liverpool, Man- chester and Wigan Countylhe was glad to think,had not held their hands in consequence of Mr. Balfour's in- timidation. Last month, the Liverpool bench extin- guished 71 licences, makinga reduction of 489 in fifteen years.

The CHAIRMAN: You had a good many to start with. Mr. BORELAND : We had not as many as some places but I think we were worse than you were a few years ago. Continuing, Mr. Boreland said some magistrates had taken their cue from Mr. Balfour and had not done their duty. illlr. Balfour was still unrepentent, and had written stating that he was prepared to carry out the promise he male to the trade twelve months ago. The Licensing Bill would not be introduced until the last minute and he had a shrewd suspicion that if Mr. Balfour could drop the thing he would be glad to do it. (Hear. hear.) But the liquor men had him by the "scuff of the neck." and were demanding their pound of flesh, and as they had votes and money and influence at elections, he did not see bow Mr. Balfour was going to get out of the difficulty. He hardly thought that the House of Commons, which had previously declined to grant compensation, would now consent to do so. If the liquor traffic required compensation for licences surrendered, let them provide it themselves from their own funds. (Hear, hear.) The only man he had any with was the publican who ran his own busi- ness, and a publican of this kind could insure himself against the risk of losing his licence with the Licensing Insurance Corporation for the sum of £5 per annum, and for that small premium be could receive £l,OOO in the,event of the licence being withdrawn. He had no sympathy with the rich brewing companies with their tied houses and had still less sympathy for the 300 parsons who owned shares in brewing companies. (Hear, hear.) If.the discretionary povv3r of the magistrates were taken away, the public would be at the mercy of the liquor trade and the big brewing syndicates of the United Kingdom. That was a vary serious matter. (Hear, hear.) Speaking of the work of the Church of England Temperance Society. Mr. Boreland said he had organised 39 meetings on the lines of the present one in the Liver- pool district, and he had received permission to publish the names of 228 supporters. Of these 228 supporters he was pleased to say that 45 ha,l been Church of England clergymen. (Applause.) Many of the meetings were held in Church of Englagd premises, lent free for the occasion, and very often the Rector or Vicar had been in the chair. He believed many of these clergy- men would march side by side with temperance workers. and at the next election would npt vote for candidates who would support the Government in their wild career of fastening the liquor tradelupon this nation, as it had never been fastened before, but rather in favour of men who would expand the liberties ofithelpeople and entrust them with the power of saying whether they would have any liquor houses in their districts, and if so on what conditions. (Applause ~ • The resolution was carried unanimously. THE SECOND RssoLurioN.

The R3V. G. H. CARNSON moved the second resolution which read as follows :—" That this meeting is opposed to any legislation which would tend to wardsthe creation of vested interests in licenses granted by magistrates— such licenses being subject to withdrawal from year to year should the public interest require it. (2) That copies of these resolutions ba sent to the Prime Minister the M.P., for the Borough, and the Town Clerk." Mr. Carnson said he had not taken a very prominent part in temparance work in Warrington but on such occasions as Temperance Sunday when he had taken the opportunity of referring to temperance questions,ladies had told him that they admired his zeal but not his judgment. (Laughter.) He was not a politican, but it appeared to him that efforts were being made to make the drink traffic firmer and more powarful than it had been before. (Hear, hear.) If it were a good trade it would not need it. 'He heard of a publichouse not tea miles away for which a sum of £7,000 was given. He understood that the rating of that house was about £75 A private house not very far away was valued at £1,500 and it was rated at 215. There was something wrong about these values and it puzzled him absolutely. The Chairman had just told him that he ought to get to know. (Laughter.) If they allowed the Government to make the drink traffic a vested interest the public would be in a worse position than before. Looking at the effects of this trade they could not, in the best interests of this country, allow the Govern- ment to accomplish this object. (Hear, hear.) He would like anyone to tell him what good the trade did. He thought it would horrify some of the ladies and gentlemen who fingered the profits of the trade if they went into the lower parts of our cities and towns and saw the effects of alcoholic drink. The ramifications of

the trade were tremendous, and these increased the difficulties of temperance workers. He trusted everyone would be determined to light against any curtailment of he power of the magistrates. (Hear, hear.) THE REV. C. HARVEY COOK AND THE BREWERS' REQUEST.

The Rev. C. HARVEY COOK, in seconding the resolu- tion, said if he could have his way, he would have the cost of all the illfortune brought to our beloved land by the drinking of intoxicating liquors, refunded from the brewers' profits. (Hear, hear.) There were four questions which they ought to put to themselves : (1) What is the liquor trade asking of the Government? (2) Why is it asking? (3) Who are they who areasking? (4) How are they asking? He tried to study this ques- tion in a judicial temper, detaching himself from all prejudices and looking at it in the light of pure fact.. What was it that the liquor trade was asking of the Government ?It was simply this. The trade was:asking to have an immensely valuable property handed over to it gratuitously. The proprietorship in licences was worth millions of money, and he could quite understand why this concession was being asked. If he were a brewer he would be disposed to join in. If he could get a valuable property for nothing, there was so much human nature in him that he would have it, though he were a parson. (Laughter). The request was made for the creation of a vested interest in licences and if the Government granted it the day of the Government of the people for the people by the people was past. (Applause). They wanted the workingman to understand what this matter meant to him. (Hear, hear). One heard so much about the workingman that , one got absolutely sick of hearing about him, as though there were no others who worked, except the man who stripped his sleeves up, and as though no one did any thing worth having except the men of big muscles who earned their living by the sweat of their brow. There were other kinds of work calling for greater physical energy, for greater mental effort and greater exhaustion than that demanded of the working man. (Hear, hear.) When the licensed trade said that their trade was their politics, then if ever the devil showed himself he showed himself there. (Laughter.) It was the cloven hoof. If a man's trade were his politics then he was no longer influenced by the compassion of brotherhood and the care of his kind. (Hear, hear). He quite understood that a man might make his trade his politics, but let him not call himself a lover of his kind; let him not dare to take upon himself the name of a brother of the human race. Such a man no longer cared for the people; all he wanted was to establish himself so that he could not be moved. Why was the trade • making this request? Because it wanted to make its position legislatively secure. It did not want the matter left in the hands of the people. The trade wanted to get entrenched behind Acts of Parliament so that if the people wanted to touch it it would be able to bring in a costly bill for getting its legislation altered, and the power of the people would be simply impotent against it. "The trade" knew what it wanted. He wished temperance people knew better what they wanted. (Hear, hear.) What was more, the trade was bent on getting what it wanted and if enthusiasm, spending money and giving time would bring about what it wanted, the trade would get it. If temperance workers and social reformers did not show up to the peopleS the specious pleas and the specious arguments of the trade, the trade would win and rigthly so. He had no patience withpeople who complained because others won when they might have won and simply lost through sheer ignorance. It was significant that contemporaneously with the rise of the tied house was coming the demand for the creation of a vested interest. -When the publican ran his own house he did not ask for compensation. He did not even ask for a vested interest. It was only since the growth of the tied house that this request had been made. What did it mean? It meant that it was not the poor publican who was asking for a vested interest it was the rich brewer—the man who ought to I stand alone best. (Hear, hear.) Did they suppose for one moment that if this were granted that the publican would be in a better position? If they granted a vested interest the poor publican would become poorer and the rich brewer richer. That was the whole trend of the thing. It was quite pitiable to see men going, cap in hand, saying "Oh these terrible teetotal people. We do wish you would do something with them. These poor fanatics—cannot you put them in some asylum or curtail their powers? Oh, do something for us, good man." (Laughter.) Now that that hae not succeeded, the brewers hadfried a game of bluff. 'They were now demanding something, and when a man demanded of him, he (Mr. Cook) was not going to take it lying down. (Applause). If the workingman of the country would only understand the brewers' request; only under- stand what it is going to lead to, if granted, he would not take it lying down. (Hear, hear.) In the name of Gol, in the name of our common humanity, in the name of our English brotherhood, in the name of the people, , for the love of our country, for our homes, for our children, let them kindly but firmly yet unmistakably I quash the request of the trade. (Applause). PUBLIC SPIRIT REQUIRED.

The Rev. JOHN YONGE moved a vote of thanks to the Chairman. They all, he said, looked upon Mr. Monks as one of the best friends the town possessed. It might be that in some things he and they might not see eye to eye, but they believed in his honesty, impartiality, generosity, and public spirit. He (Mr. Yonge) con- fessed that when he saw such a comparatively small audience, his heart went down towards his boots. He wondered when Warrington ever would answer to the call of a great cause, apart from party feeling or de- nominational ends. What they wanted in Warrington, beyond everything else perhaps, was a thoroughly public and patriotic spirit, a thoroughly Christian and humane spirit which would always lead the people in all their churches, and all through the town, to rally together for the support and advocacy of a Christian cause. He submitted that the cause which had called them there that night was one of the greatest and most urgent causes that they had before them at the present day. He wished it could have been possible to say that Warrington, by a large, enthusiastic and crovvded meeting, had carried all the resolutions sub- mitted. He was quite at onewith ihespeakers in think- ing that if the Government did succeed—and he earnestly prayed they might not—in putting that fetter on the discretion of the magistrates, it would be a very sad thing for the country. He earnestly hoped all present would do their best to educate their friends and neighbours into seeing the impolicy of the proposals which the Prime Minister had suggested to the nation. (Applause.) The Rev. J. F. MATTHEWS seconded, and the motion was heartily agreed to. The CHAIRMAN, in reply, said the licensing question was one to which he had given a great deal of thought. Ho could not say he agreed with all that theZspeakers had said—he thought some of them had rather wandered from the subject—but on the main point they quite agreed. He honed the result of the agitation would be that the Government would let well alone. (Applause,)

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

ELECTIONS OF DISTRICT COUNCILLORS AND GUARDIANS.

The opposing candidates in the Viaduct and Town Hall Wards are busy looking up the electors, and in consequence of the varying interests concerned, the con- tests are likely to be very keen. Contests for the posi- tions of Guardians will take place in the Viaduct and Wargrave Wards. In the former, Mr. T. Lythgoe opposes Mr. John Clarke, who for very many years has been a familiar figure on the Board, and in the latter, Mr. Astley King, Ironmonger, opposes Mr. E. L. Bent. The elections take place on the 28th inst. LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD INQUIRY AT EARLESTOWN.

Mr. F. H. Tulloch, M.1.C.E., acting on behalf of the Local Government Board, held at inquiry at the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Wednesday morning, into the application of the Newton-in-Makerfield Urban District Council for the issue by the Board of a provisional order to amend their local Act. The Council sought powers to increase the width of new streets, to purchase, sell, and let for hire gas stoves, and to remove the limit placed on their borrowing powers in resuect of their water undertaking. In connection with the latter, the Clerk to the Council (Mr. C. Cole) reported that out of the sum of £3LOOO which the Council were authorised to borrow £27,172 had been sanctioned, and £5,546 was still required to complete the scheme. The yield of water from the boreholes, well, and headings is 513,000 gallons per twenty-four hours,and the average consump- tion about 300,000 gallons, or 17 gallons per day per head.

FOUND DRUNK.

A LESSON FOR FRIENDLY SOCIETIES AND TRADES UNIONS.

Looking through my " Examiner," the first of the New Year's issue, I found more than one reference to the sad case of a boy of 14 years being found drunk and lying across the footpath. I must confess that I was somewhat surprised at reading this because I remembered that some months ago the British papers were jubilant over some temperance work which the Imperial Parliament had just allowed to escape it,and which gave what seemed to be a tremervlous power into thd hands of the magistracy. Had some one blundered? Had Parliament made a mistake, or were the magistrates afraid to use the powers placed in their hands? However this might be the fact re- mained that it was still possible for young lads to get drunk and it was not possible to find out that any one bad been punished according to the law. Is it in order to ask if the boy of fourteen is the father of the man? If the answer be in the affirmative, and drunken boys are numerous then may Britain be decad- ent. Previous to my son starting out to Honolulu,he had for his Sunday work the teaching of a class of young men whose ages would average 20 years each. The results were not satisfactory to him and he invited me to have a talk with him about it. My position was a rather difficult one as my experience would not allow me to give him the least encouragement on the religious ' side of the question. The boys wanted to have, and would have" a good time." The Pastor's sermons were voted a bore and Charlie's " talks " were not much better. The constant cry of the elders of the church and Sunday school was, "What shall we do to keep our hold on the young men ?" Of course there is nothing original in this question. It has been asked times without number. The Roman Catholic Church keeps its hold from birth to the grave There is no worrying about the soul's salvation, neither is there any trouble about the Sabbath day. Go to early mass, take the car, and be off to the races, and the church is satisfied. I speak the truth in say- ing this, I see it every Sunday the year round. The great point is not to die without seeing the priest. But I am leaving my text. Charlie and I concluded first, that a vast number of parents were simply unfit to have the flare and bringing up of children,and next, that his time would be better employed if he directed his energies and such aptitude as he had for teaching to the training of the young mind when it made its first appearance in the Sunday schools. There still remains the young man to deal with. How to keep him out of the public house is the question. Several speeclles have been made in Warrington recently on this very important subject, and with not one of them could I agree, nor could I find anything practical. My old friend Mr. H. Thornton, I just pitied. He saw no way out of the trouble. His only panacea seemed to be christianity, but I think that he must allow that that has entirely failed. Drunken men and boys do not, and will not listen to sermons and teetotal lectures. Mr. Thornton may spend his money, but it will be of no use. The most sober people on earth,—and they are found in India,—know nothing of Christian methods. I think very few men have seen more of drunken- ness than I have. I spent three of my young years in the home of a Roman Catholic, and he ,was an inveterate drunkard, and I twice cut him down,_ when he was attempting to hang himself. I stood at his bedside when he was dying, and read from the book that was placed in my hand the service that was to take him to heaven. I have more than once seen the man that was to teach me my trade, take the bread out of the house and sell it for beer. To me the question I write of, has always had an importance above and beyond all others, and yet I am not a teetotaler.

For a number of years every question that has come under my notice for consideration has had to be first determined upon from a labour point of view. How will it affect the workingman? I am satisfied that drunkenness and gambling are the greatest evils he has to contend with. Could the workingmen of England be kept sober for one month, the change they would experience would simply knock the bottom out of the everlasting labour and capital question. Teetotalers should, in my opinion, try to bring about a sort of coalition arrangement with men who are not abstainers, but are men of good repute and fixed habits. They would then hear of method whereby young men would certainly be kept out o two of the greatest snares that the spirit of evil could possibly invent. I refer to the Friendly Society's Lodge and the Trades Union Lodge. These two Lodges represent institutions to which I am much attached, and have been a member of, all my life, and lam not likely to try to do them an injury. I speak for their good and that of the community. In the summer of 1903 I took a week's vacation at an old gold mining camp in the Nevada mountains. In conversation with some of the residents I found that an Oddfellows' Lodge was a great feature in the camp. Of course I had to tell them I was a P.P.G.M., of the English Order. Their curiosity was aroused, and we discussed the difference existing between the two Orders. This ended in me receiving a pleasing invitation to attend the Lodge. It was a gala night, a banquet and speech making. I found quite a num- ber of lady Oddfellows, and they saw after the feed- ing business in great style. After this came the speech making, and I could have wished my English brothers to have been present. I think they would have heard something that would have benefited them. No Lodge has any connection whatever with intoxicat- ing liquors, nor is any member of an Oddfellows' Lodge allowed to have, either directly or indirectly any profit from the sale of intoxicating liquors. Of course I had to give a talk, and I took this feature, and the presence of the ladies for my text. I was sure that this was one way, and a good one, of making the workingman a sober man, even if there wai no theology mixed up with it. I must also state that better even than the above is the fact that Labour meetings are never held in saloons, or _public houses.

The great lesson to be learned from this is, I think, the biggest object lesson the reformer can learn. The Young man, say of 17 and on, does not have the sense Of shame blunted at entering a public-house under the plea or excuse that he is only attending his Lodge, or Trade Union meeting. The young man of 17 or 18 years of age is persuaded to join an Order. Possibly he was never in a public-house before, but he must go in one now if he wishes to join a Lodge. When he comes out of the Lodge room there is the invitation to go into one of the other rooms and "have a glass." There is no need that I should say any more, the result must be apparent to all. If you want to do something practical, remove the labour Union, and the Friendly Society's Lodge from the public house and its environments!

I would advise my reader to get the first issue of Mr. Stead s "Daily Paper" and turn to page 3 where he will 'find the following item touching just one small portion of London, that of Paddington. The whole male population of Paddington at the last Census was 61,065. But the number of adult males who on Sunday, December 27, 1903, visited the public-houses of Paddington was 83,411. From this it would appear that very few of the males of Paddington are " oncers." The bulk of them go to the public-house in the afternoon, and then return again in the evening. The number of male adults attending all the places open for religious worship when the" Daily News " Census was taken was only 8,099. The figures are only one degree less distressing when we turn to the statistics as to women and children. There are 82,350 females in Paddington, of whom 16,275 went to church or chapel on Census Sunday. But 28,118 adult females visited the public-houses on December 27. The figures as to children are even more amazing. De- spite recent legislation. 10,646 children under 15 visited Paddington public-houses, while only 6,957 went to church. The figures are disheartening, but they are at any rate conclusive.. So far as Padd- ington is concerned, London, on Sunday worships Bacchus, and not ,Christ. VDoes this worshiping of Bacchus pay the working- man? My experience, and it has been a long and varied one, answers emphatically No ! HENRY WHIT.EIAM, San Francisco. Feb. 1904,

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Farewell to an Old Wesleyan Chapel

In anticipation of the rebuilding of the Rixton Wesleyan Chapel a farewell service was held on Sunday evening in the old sanctuary. There was a large congre- gation, and old tunes and hymns were rendered in a hearty manner. Appropriate remarks were made by the Rev. W. B. Butters, Mr. Birchall, of Barton, Mr Blythe, of Warrington, Mr. S. Wright of Cadishead, and Mr. Blane, of Carrington. Mr. Tonge, of Rixton, gave a short history of the chapel, which has been in existence for sixty years. He mentioned several old member; notably, Mrs. Atherton and Mr.J. Matley, who died at the advanced age of 102. The collection realised £5 and Mr. W. Garner, of Cadishead, commenced opera- tions this week. During the rebuilding the Temperance Hall, Hollinfare,will be used as a place of worship.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
W ycllife Church P.S.A.

The usual meeting was held on Sunday, when there as a good attendancs, and a most encouraging meet- ing. Nineteen new members joined and 3 re-entered, making the membership for the second Sunday in the new year 436. The Hon. Secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) gave them a very hearty welcome, and a word of advice. Mr. W. Beswick presided, and spoke upon the keeping of God's laws and His Sabbaths. God's commands, he said, must be obeyed, then they would give to them life.—Mr. Thomson, of Liverpool, was the speaker, and gave an excellent address upon "Remember and forget." These words, he said, were two seeming contradictions. There were many things they would do well to forget which they too often remembered, and also in the opposite direction. The Psalmist advised them to remember all God's benefits to tbem. He urged the men present to go in for helping their fellow men, who were their brethren. The speaker gave some pointed illustrations, which had a telling effect upon the aud- ence. Miss E. Mounfield sang "It is time to rest' and "The Promised Land" with good effect. Mr. Smiley also took part in the meeting.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Services for the People.

The service at the Royal Court Theatre on Sunday evening was taken by the Rev. Alexander Harvey of Widnes, who preached from the well-known text from John 3. 16," God so loved the world that He gave His only begotten Son, that whosoever believeth in Him should not perish, but have everlasting life." The preacher took various aspects of the love of God, and said that many people only found time and money to benefit humanity as a whole, and were apt to estimate God's love according to their own, whereas God loved not only the world but the individual, in giving His well-beloved Son to die, and it was this personal acceptation of Christ's sacrifice for sin that was the only efficacious belief in Christ. People might believe all about Christ, yet not believe on Him to the salvation of their souls. Coming to the last point in the text,the preacher stated that it was not necessary to die to ob- tain eternal life, and that as boon as a man believed in Christ for salvation he possessed eternal life, the nega- tive of this statement being that if a man believed not he had not life. If they could trust the finished work of Christ they could also claim God's promise of "everlasting life." The Rev. H. Dunnico is expected to take the service next Sunday.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
The Rev. J. S. Hughes and Yellow Labour.

At Hill Cliffe Baptist Chapel on Sunday evening the Rev. J. Samuel Hughes preached on the subject" Slavery under l3ritish rule." Speaking from Isaiah x.l-2.,the rev. gentleman said recently there had arisen a question which had called forth feelings of profound astonish- ment and indignation, the introduction of which was an insult to the moral sense of the Christian community, viz., the determination of the Government to import 300,000 Chinese labourers to work in the South African mines, under conditions which were nothing I.ss than a state of bondage and slavery. War, against which some of them declaimed until it was too late, had brought forth its bitter fruits and its costliness; after problems, and terrible consequences and burdens seemed to have turned the heart, blinded theeye and blunted the moral sense and maddened the brain of the Government. We were led to sow the wind we were now reaping the whirl wind. This was a question on which the church had a right to speak, and on which she ought to speak with no uncertain sound. .As Baptists they had a royal heritage in this rnatter,having ever been in the forefront of the battle for the freedom of the subject, civil and religious. The self-sacrificing labours of those heroes of emancipation in Jamaica—Rnibb and Burchell—were inscribed largely in the history of the struggle against slavery, and on them rested the respon- sibility of guarding all such liberties with a jealous eye By whatever name this traffic in men was known— whether indentured labour or anything else—to them it was nothing less than an ill-disguised attempt to re- establish slavery under the British flag, and as Christians they lifted their voices in solemn protest against sueh a shameful snbyersion of the liberty of the subject by a nation bearing the Christian name. The proposal was, to say the least of it, dishonouring to our country's fame. The British people would indeed be unworthy stewards of the heritage placed in their hands, and traitorsto their noblest traditions, if they permitted their fame to be so dishonoured, and their flag to be disfigured, with the record of so shameful an ordinance upon our statute book. The proposal is fraught with great danger to morality ; and it was contrary to the spirit and teaching of Christianity.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHARGE AGRINST MR J. HARRISON.

Confessions of Defalcations and an Appeal

for his Family.

Charges of embezzlement preferred by his employers against Joseph George Harrison, late Macclesfield re- presentative of Messrs. Greenell, Whitley, and Co., brewers, Warrington, were heard at Macclesfield on Wednesday. Mr. Lance Bentley, barrister , (in- structed by Mr. Draper of; Messrs. Robert Davies and Company), appeared to prosecute, and the prisoner was defended by 'Mr. J. G. Barclay. The total defalcations alleged against the prisoner amounted to £439. An investigation was made of the prisoner's books in January, and as a result the prisoner absconded, and on March Ist was arrested in Jersey. Prisoner's salary cad commission amounted to about £350 a year. On January rth the 'prisoner wrote to Mr. Downes, managing director: "It is with extreme regret and remorse that I have to inform you rey cash is very much short and has been for some time. . . When the exact amount is ascertained I will refund every penny as far as possible. All the money has gone into on cilannel, viz., 'the trade.'" Later he appealed to the directors not to proceed to extremes, and added: " I hope and trust the worst will not be- fall me, if only for the sake of my wife and little children." Mr. Voisey, accountant, Warrington, auditor to the compan 7, said he had examined the books, and ascer- tained that the amount of prisoner's defalcations to be as stated. He was cross-examined by Mr. Barclay with the object of showing that the prisoner's books had not ' been properly supervised, but this he strongly denied. Prisoner was committed for trial to Knutsford Quarter Sessions.

SUSPICIOUS CONDUCT OF A TRAMP.-At Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Thomas Feeney, a tramp, was charged with being on the enclosed' premises of the Misses Tinsley, The Elms, Penketh, at 12.15 the same morning.—Police- constable Houghton stated that he heard dogs barking, and on proceeding to The Elms, he saw the , prisoner coming from the back of the house. When questioned, prisoner said he was going to Widnes. He I was rornanded until Friday next for inquiries to be I made.

The Examiner, Saturday, March 19, 1904.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
A WESLEYAN MINISTER'S CYCLING TOUR IN GERMANY.

The Rev. George Harbottle, superintendent of the Dunstable Wesleyan Circuit, formerly minister of Bewsey-road Church, received a hearty welcome from his many Warrington friends on re-visiting the town this week. On Sunday. he preached to large congre- gations in Bewsey-road Church in connection with the Guild Festival, and on Monday he delighted a larg gathering by a racy and instructive lecture, entitled Through Germany by cycle and fourth class." It was intended to have the lecture in the meetinc, room but by the time the proceedings had been opened the room became so crowded as to necessitate an adjourn- ment to the church. Mrs. Garnett, of Penketh, was unable to preside, but a capable substitute was found in her son, Mr. Philip Garnett. In the course of his introductory remarks, Mr. Garnett spoke of the pleasures of touring on the bicycle. He was the possessor of a motorcycle, he said, but his experience of it did not warrant him in recommending others t purchase one. Only on Saturday last, he and others might have been seen lifting it into a railway guard's van. (Laughter) :—The Rev. George Harbottle, at the outset of his lecture, said one of the advantages of a cycling tour was that like John Gilpin's, ride you started at your ownldoor and got off there. In alluding to his railway journey from Hamburg to Berlin, he said the fourth class conveyance in which he travelled was practically a big horse box with seats round the sides, giving accommodation for 29 persons and the luggage was packed in the centre. The fare was very cheap, the charge for the distance of over 100 miles being ss. 6d. and 6d. for the bicycle. In an interesting manner, Mr. Harbottle gave his impressions of Berlin. The principal thoroughfare was the tinter den Linden (under the lime trees) and was two-thirds of a mile in length and 160 feet wide, the lime trees in the centre giving a picturesqueness to the whole. Statuary met the eye frequently and at Lustgarten where the principal buildings were situated, there were some magnificent monuments. The German Emperor was staying at the Schloss or Royal Palace and the lecturer said he was struck with the quietness with which the German people received their Emperor. There were none of the.loud hurrahs and waving of hats which distinguished an English crowd when receiving royalty. The Germans drank much more beer than the English but it was of a weak quality, and during the whole of his tour he never saw a drunken man. As an " Englander " he met vvitu universal kindness from the Germans, and only on one occasion was a rude remark made to him and that was with reference to the Transvaal. During his stay he spent a Sunday in Berlin and found- large congregations at the morning services and small ones at night. It seemed rather peculiar to him as an E-iglishman to find the German Emperor attending divine service in the morning and the Opera House in the evening. Re- mounting his bicycle at Berlin, Mr. Harbottle took his hearers in* imagination on, his tour through Potsdam, Wittenberg and Leipzig and along the Rhine to Coblentz. In alluding to Wittenburg, the lecturer referred to Luther's association with it,and of his visit to the spot where Luther publicly burned the , Papal Bull. The German Evangelical churches were ' rich in statuary, and candles were largely used. In North Germany, Roman Catholicism predominated, but in middle and South Germany, the people were mostly Protestants. In referring to his cycling ex- periences, he said, he made himself a member of a German cycling club, and never experienced the slightest difficulty from the time be left England until he returned. He could not speak Ger- man very fluently but he always succeeded in making himself understood. He found lovely roads, surrounded by beautiful scenery and he picked up sufficient information on his tour to make an inter- esting book for cyclists. The cost of his cycling tour, he added, was smaller than if he had spent his holi- days at the seaside in England, and the days spent in Germany were amongst the happiest in his life.—The meeting terminated Nvith an acknowledgment of the Rev. G. Harbottle's and the Chairman's services, on the proposition of the Rev. R. Wilkins Rees.

A REMARKABLE BOOK.

MR. OSWALD SToLL's remarkable work, published by Messrs. Simpkin and Marshall, in which he essays most successfully to prove that man can, if he chooses, 'pro- long his life on this planet indefinitely is naturally commanding a large sale. The fact that it attempts to dispose of all pre-existing beliefs as to our mortality naturally compels attention and although till now no special notice has been taken of it by churchmen it is bound to obtain pulpit-reference before long, beciuse of its claims that the true teachings of Sacred Writ are all in favour of the theory. Unlike most clever philo- sophical works, the book is published ata popular price, and should the world appraise it as an addition to our knowledge, based on fundamental scientific principles, . .

Mr. Stoll -will have to be handed down to iosterity as one of the most illuminant thinkers of the age.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

Authors of letters intended for publication must mall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper azimut in,.if possible,not later than Thursday.

The Ragged School Soup Kitchen. Sir,—Will you kindly allow us througn tne medium of your paper, to inform the public that we have been dis- tributing soup and bread at the Ragged school daring the past week. We have been well aware for some timel of the terrible distress in the town, especially in th? Bewsey district, hence our repeated appeals for help. - Mr. Skelland, who has been indisposed of late and Mr. Owen, the treasurer of the Mission, determined to start the soup kitchen, and on Saturday last went round the town with the Drum and Fife band, collecting Coppers from that class whohave little to spare,but who in times of want and privation like the present, set a noble example to others more comfortably circum- tanced. We are glad to state that sufficient was collected to make a start, and, aided by the generous donations re- ceived from the tradesmtm named below, we have been able to distribute daily over 80 gallons of good soup, and over 100 lbs. of bread. Some heartrending sights have been witnessed; children ravenously devouring their dole of dry bread, too hungry to wait until they received their soup; the soup itself swallowed almost scalding hot, the hungry little ones, like Oliver Twist, plaintively asking for more. Some curious utensils were brought,—battered tins, jugs minus handles &c.' Some of these we had actually to bind round with string, before we could pour any soup in them. We have again to thank Mr. Hallwood for a quantity of loaves, and other articles; Messrs. G. Bowcock, Cross (Corporation-street), Singleton and others for meat. Mr. Harrison for one guinea; Messrs. Carter, Barlow, and others for various necessary ingredients. We again strongly appeal for help to enable us to keep the soup kitchen open during this period of acute dis- tress. On behalf of the committee, JOHN W. HOWARTH. The "Observer _" and the Sermonettes. Sir,—The writer in the"Observer"is still on the ram- page "swearing at large," and dancing round my four statements, but he has nover tried to answer any one of them. The other day he was airing his ignorance about the export of coal. He does not know that steamships cannot sail without coal, and that we o nby far the largest fleet of steamers in the world. He seems to think that our steamers can sail from this country to the ends of the earth, and carry coal enough in their bunkers to last them both the outward and the return journey. If he ever cuts his wisdom teeth he will learn that no steamer can do that and carry a big cargo. If our commerce is flourishing, and our steamers are fully employed,we are bound to be exporting large quantities of coal. But I cannot find time to expose all his false and foolish statements as I am away from home, lec- turing for a month at 'York, Hull, Richmond, Thirsk and Melton for the Yorkshire Liberal Fecleration and 1 have to get through my correspondence as best I can. My. opponent cuts a peculiarly ridiculous figure when he tries to tackle any of my propositions. I gave four instances in which Mr. Chamberlain's statements and the Board of Trade returns flatly contradicted each other. I asked which of the parties was lieing and lam still waiting for an answer. When he takes sides and tells us whether he believes Mr. Chamberlain or the Board of Trade it will be soon enough to answer him. Your readers must not be bluffed by " freak "years like 1872 and 1900. In the former year we had a great peace. In the latter we had a great war, and we were exporting horses, and war materials, and coals at a phenomenal rate. My opponent is too shifty for my liking. If I bundle him out of one mud hole, he takes refuge inanother,and life is too short for that silly game. Why does he not cut the cackle and answer my four questions like a man ? Yours truly, York.

JOHN TAYLOR.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
The Cause of Labour.

Sir,—The magnificent triumph secured by Labour in the return of Mr. J. Ball, nominee of the Independent Labour Party, at the head of the poll for elective auditorship, following so closely on the labour victory in Orford Ward,has undoubtedly caused no small surprise in local political circles. Casual labour gains will cause a fluttering in the orthodox dove-cotes until labour successes become a natural sequence in political warfare, and until labour asserts its right to take its proper place in the conduct and management of local and national affairs. Those in the thick of the fight for justice and pro- gress can perceive a rapid awakening of organized labour—aye even of the disorganized proletarian—an awakening to the necessity of having men of their own class possessing fearless and independent calibre in voicing their sentiments and grievances on all govern- ing bodies. Various reasons are ascribed for the unusual interest the workers are displaying in matters appertain- ing to their own social condition and its alleviation. In dealing with this subject it must be borne in mind that the spreading of the glorious light of citizenship and the responsibility of casting the vote—not to satisfy personal ambitions orparty aggrandisement, but to send representatives to deal sincerely and determinedly with the problems that surround us,—is not confined to War- rington alone, but is spreading to all thoughtful wealth producers throughout the country. Last year two Independent Labour men, Mr. W. Crooks and Mr. A. Henderson were returned to the Commons, and the Municipal contests in November resulted in over 40 seats being secured to the credit of Labour. Most striking gains were recorded in West- minster, Woolwich, Birmingham .and Manchester, and heavy polls in such Conservative strongholds as Blackburn and Preston. This is surely a clear indication that Independent Labolir in the near future will take a respectable position amongst the orthodox parties in Council Chamber and Legislative Assembly. To my mind a paragraph in Ferdinand Lasal les' "Working Man's Programme" cogently summarises and illustrates the difficulties and ultimate triumph of this new movement. It is an apt and homely comparison. "Have you ever witnessed a sunrise from a lofty mountain? A purple streakcolours the extreme verge of the horizon, announcing the new light; mists of cloud gather, roll themselves into a mass, throw themselves against the glow of the morn- ing, and succeed in covering its rays for a moment. But no power in the world can avail to hinder the slow and majestic rising of the sun itself, which an hour later stands in the firmament, visible to all, and giving light and warmth to all the earth." It is claimed that we are indebted to the Non- Trading Party for arousing the working-man (I refer locally of course) from his past attitude of indifference and apathy, to his present sense of responsibilities and duties as a citizen. Sir, may I be permitted to men- tion that long before the Non-trading agitation was initiated, a handful of men, members of the I.L.P. were preaching and teaching, holding open air meetings time after time, urging and beseeching the working classes to give up a portion of their leisure to the affairs of their town and country. They prayed and laboured for purity and higher ideals in political life. They were denounced as charlatans and quacks, decried as dreamers and visionaries. It was the old old story which has travelled from age to age. Progress has always met with ridicule and persecution. Public opinion always howled "Crucify, crucify," whenever an uncommon mind appeared, or an uncommon idea was promulgated. Life is made almost unendurable to those born ahead of the age in which they live. The Independent Labour Party stands on the same platform to-day with increased numbers, and their principles of humanity and Brother- hood spread like the golden dawn of a summer morn- ing. Imbued with hope and conrage they carry on the fight, consoled in their hearts that in the short span of life allotted they are doing their utmost to make" His will be done on earthas it is in Heaven."—Yours truly, ABRAHAM WARBURTON,

Cairo-street.

Stockton Heath and Urban Powers. Sir,—As the ratepayers at the late parish meeting decided to have an election of Councillors, T take it as a Warning that the public of this district are awaken to a sense of their responsibility. From the tone of the remarks and actions of the Chairman, it looks as if urban powers may be claimed in the near future by Stockton Heath, and doubtless some may be of opinion that as the district is steadily growing and becoming from rural to semi-urban, such powers would be benefi- cial and to the general welfare of the district. This might be so,but in my opinion,before any such steps are taken the whole question will rePTire very careful attention, for I contend that under the present condi- tions of the district, it could not be worked more efficiently and economically under the powers of an Urban District Council than as at the `present time, and in making such an assertion I shall endeavour to prove that my statement is a correct version. Ist, the whole rate of the district at present under the Rural District Council is something like 3s. in the 2, which sum includes poor rate, county rate, general dis- trict and special expenses rates all includel. The benefits for such are these : Two seats upon the Rural Council (and a parish council to wit). A surveyor resident in the place; duties almost exclusively con— fined to Stockton Heath. A supervision by Sanitary Inspector,Medical Officer and all other requisite officials, public lighting, scavaging and maintainance of main . and other roads and streets, the removal of refuse and nightsoil, and the poor rate administered and the rates collected. Now what other than these could be ob- tained by urban powers? Well, Ist, full powers (sub- ject to the will of the L.G.8.) are given to govern and control the whole affairs of the district other than County Council matters and the administration of the poor rates under a Council elected by the ratepayers. 2nd, with power to levy a rate to meet the current ex- penditure. which summarised, would result as follows: Establishment charges, which include offices, fires lighting, printing and other items that total up sur- prisingly £100; elections, £3O; notifications of in-

fectious diseases, £10; hospital charges say £2O; emptying ashpits and removing dustbins and nightsoil, £150; scavaging roads and streets, wages £100; maintenance of same, £150; sewers, £5O; public light- ing, £l2O ; salaries of officials, Surveyor and Sanitary Inspector combined, £170; Clerk and Rate Collector, £7O; Medical Officer, £2O; Law Adviser and expenses, £3O. The foregoing items are something near what might be expected to commence with, and the total is £l,OlO, or 2s. 7d. in the 2 on the present rateable value. The Poor and County Rates and other expenses would be charged extra as separate from district rates. The amount of £470 now received would be more than doubled, and' I am not over estimating the cost of working under urban powers for facts go to prove that the cost of administration is greater than under Rural authorities which cover a much larger area. Stockton Heath in the near future will have to face a new sewage scheme, for if the district increases in the future as of late years, the present sewage system will not be adequate for the increased requirements. The plea of increased income goes for nothing, as it is very often counterbalanced by increased demands, as buildings are erected and new streets made. Now my contentionsare, if it is desired that the place should further develop, keep off urban powers! It cannot be denied that Stockton Heath has been selected by the speculative builder, and as a residential area by its low rates. The school accommodation question was mentioned but the primary stage seems to be completely ignojed Surely it is not from personal motives that the ques'ion of Secondary Education is given the more prominent position, for the powers that be assured us that at the proper time the'necessary steps would be taken to meet the demands of the Education Act,but as yet nothing has been made publicly known of what has or is)being done in the matter' and as Stockton Heath is not one of the selected places for the new schools by the Cheshire County Council it is high time the public were made aware of the true facts of the case, and this election gives the ratepayers an opportunity to assert their rights by de- manding fromlthe candidates some pledges that this question shall not be shelved any longer. The future welfare of the children of the working classes is at stake, and they should demand that proper facilities be made—so that the children may have a fair chance in the race for their future livelihood amongst men. The new Education Act, is meant for the children to be taught under the best possible conditions and it is acknowledged by all, that the existing school accommodation is far from satisfying the requirements of the Act. Now is the ratepayers' opportunity for supporting the men who will endeavour to carry out the requirements of this question and also men who have got the welfare of the district at heart and not personal motives to serve or axes to grind. I remain, yours , DACEISIDAC.

March 17th, 1904.

Chinese Labour in South Africa. Sir—The term slavery which has been applied to the con- ditions which are to regulate the Chinese labour in South Africa has been warmly resented by those who advocate the introduction of Asiatic labour into the Transvaal Colony. We freely admit that these conditions are very different from those with which we have been accus- tomed to associate the word in days gone by. It may be fairer to avoid a term which may suggest an exagger- ated idea, and let the facts of the case suggest their own designation. The imported labourers are to engage for a term of three years. The conditions are to be explained to them by the agents of the mining companies, who are paid in proportion to their success in recruiting labourers. These labourers are to assemble in Hong Kong, when the conditions are to be made clear by a representative of the British crown. Should the conditions as so ex- plained be objectionable to the labour recruits, they are free to decline to go further. But there is no intention of paying their expenses back to their homes, and they may find themselves adrift at Hong Kong, probably penniless, hundreds and possibly a thousand miles away from their native home. These recruits who volun- tarily, or by the pressure of necessity, engage to go forward are shipped free of charge. and are finally landed in the compounds at Kimberley. The limits of these compounds are the limits of their liberty. This con- finement applies not alone to working hours but to their -.thole time, their life, in the Colony. If liberty is granted to go outside the enclosures it must be under signed permission from the employer or owner. Should the employer be unjust, cruel, or arbitrary, they are not free to give notice,and seek employment with another mine owner,but they must continue to serve the same whatever may be the character of the task, or the task- master. They are liable to punishment if they seek to escape. Such punishment might have been by the lash, had it not been that the Chinese Minister in London insisted that corporal punishment should not be allowed. Anyone assisting these bondmen to escape, or harbour- ing them outside the compounds, is liable to a heavy- fine or to imprisonment. These are the facts, the damn- ing facts,which condemn the Ordinance as a retrograde movement, which if it does not actually land us in a system of slavery goes very far in that direction. I may be told this is the only way whereby the mines can be made to pay, that on the prosperity of the mines depends the future of the Colony • and if this is not done, the War contribution will be repudiated on the ground that England has made it impossible for the mines to earn the money to pay it with. I do not wish to make light of this aspect of the case, but the action o f the mineowners from the period of the Jameson Raid to the present, has not been of the character to increase the confidence of the English people in their honour or disinterestedness. Mr. Cresswell, who for a number of years was manager of the Village, Main Reef Mine strongly maintains that white labour can be economically employed in the mines, if only modern labour saving machinery is introduced. Possibly the true explanation of the mineowner's objections to facilitating the introduction of white labour may be found in the letter of one of the mineowners to Mr. Cresswell in which he said that the white workman would want political power, and would form trade unions, and dictate terms of employment. It is Mtite true that judging the question from this dis- tance we are at a considerable disadvantage, and we stand in great need of a disinterested opinion on the point of the possibility of white labour working the mines. The opinion of the mineowners whose interest it is to have the cheapest and most dependent form of labour, carries very little weight with the English people. If it were practicable to get a Commission of level headed Britishand Colonial workmen to examine the conditions existing in the Kimberley mines, and see if it is not possible to work these mines by white or native workmen, such a Com- mission would throw an amount of light on the question which at the preser t time we sadly need. So far we have seen no evidence which carries weight that with proper conditions, and proper wages, white labour might not be got to work the Kimberley mines, and seeing that this coufatry needs an outlet for its people this aspect of the question should be thoroughly thrashed out before the country commits itself to an experiment which is dangerous in itself, at variance with all the traditions of freedom which have long been associated with the English race, repugnant to our Dutch subjects and that of our Colonial children, who did so much in the work of winning South Africa, (as they thought) for a freer and a better civilization. But I do net admit that even if white labour were out of the question for the diamond mines, the alterna- tive of Chinese labour, especially under the conditions of serfdom is wise or right. Consider what class of Chinese are likely to sign these conditions of servitude. The recruits will come from the lowest classes, who respect neither themselves nor their liberty. A class which is the custodian of most of the vices, and fewest of the virtues, of the Chinese character. The gambling and the opium dens will be in each compound. It will be almost impossible to introduce such a class, and keep out diseases such as bubonic plague and leprosy. They are to bring their wives, but we are told no respec- able Chinese women will come, and the women who are likely to be brought are those of the character of camp followers. Let us beware lest Kimberley, for which many an English and Scottish lad gave his life to relieve, does not become tho plague spot of South Africa, deciminating the seeds of moral degradation and physical disease. A further objection to the intro- duction of Chinese labour into the Colony, arises from the fact that the Chinese will not make permanent Colonists. The Government appears to recognize this in the arrangement to send them back to China when their term expires. What is wanted above all other things in South Africa is an increase of men of British blood and habits, who will permanently settle in the country, becomeattached to it, and help to hold it for the Empire, and for the race. As long as the Dutch race predominates (although we all hope they will become loyal Colonists), there is a grave danger of a repetition of the late war, and a bid for absolute inde- pendence with the Dutch people in the ascendant. The only hope at present of being able to reduce with safety the British garrison in the Transvaal, is by the Govern- ment making the conditions of labour such as will secure British emigrants. To introduce Chinese labour is the worst possible method. It will postpone the settlement of the country, and introduce dangers, the outcome of which we cannot fully foresee. We need to be reminded to think "Imperially" on questions affecting freedom, and above all to think righteously. The lines of material prosperity run parallel to the lines of national righteousness, and individual freedom; and although in our shortsighted- ness we may think at times the lines diverge, the time will come when in looking back we shall be able to dis- cern how national righteousness has ever produced national prosperity. Even if such were not the case, a nation or an Empire, if it is to abide, must choose the right path wherever that path may lead. CHARLES E. PARKER, Penketh, March 14th, 1904.

Death In a Warrington Street. About five minutes past nine on Tuesday night, Police-constable Dyson was going on duty,and when passing Messrs. Mackie and Co.'s works in Spring- field-street, he noticed a man in a sitting position on the steps. Having examined him, he sent for Dr. Smith, who pronounced life extinct. The officer, with the assistance of Police-constable Dawson, took the deceased to the police mortuary, and it was sub- sequently ascertained that the body was that of John Welsby, watch maker, 36, Knutsford-road, Warring- ton. He had for some time been suffering from a weak heart.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.] SOCIAL SERM3NETII,

FOR WORKING MEN,

BY JOHN TAYLOR. Author of "Search Lights on Land, Labour,

anti Sociar Problems." THE NATIONAL DE3T SWINDLE.

"I would ask whether any Parliament, however constituted, can have the right to plunder one portion of the community for the profit of the other? If it has not that right,and if it should assume and exercise a power which does not belong to it, "I would then ask your Lordships what are the rights which in such a case should belong to an oppressed and injured people." —EARL STANHOPE.

These are nice questions to ask the House of Lords any way. Earl Stanhope put these conundrums to that august assembly nearly eighty years ago, and they have never been answered yet. It required some courage in those days of venality and corruption, and wholezle bribery and swindling, to protest against the iniquities of the age, and say one word in favour of righteousness and justice, and purity. The man who dared to stand up for God and truth and honesty was branded as an enemy to his country and a traitor to the King. I. PATRIOTISM IS ALWAYS POPULAR WHEN IT PAYS.

Thereare plenty loyal lusty patriots who can shout thenesel yes hoarse in praise of any policy that puts money into their own pockets. They are willing to live by their country's losses and grow rich through her misfortunes.. Thousands of working men, who were getting a pound a week when the war fever btoke out, threw up their situations and joined the Yeomanry, that they might get a free trip to South Africa, at live) shillings a day and their keep. Some of them coal I neither ride nor shoot, and they were of no earthly us on the battle- field, but they were heartily sorry w ion the war was over, and they were shipped hod). 3 a.; tin to join the ranks of the unemployed. Mr. Herbert Chamberlain gave us a pod illustration of this cheap patriotism just before the war in South Africa ended. Everybody knows that Birmingham has long been the hub of the universe for militant Toryism and reckless Jingoism, and these tinpot patriots have their reward. They have made a pile of money out of the nation's misfortunes. At the annual meeting of the Birmingham Small Arms Company, Mr. Chamberlain said that during the last year of the war their output of guns had doubled, and it was still increasing. They could pay practically forty per cent. dividend in cash, and they would be able to make a distribution of fifty shillings per share, which was nominally a dividend of fifty per cent., but as in reality anything between one hundred and fifty and two hundred per cent., according to the value the shareholders chose to put on their new shares.

Who would not be a Jingo patriot, and clamour for war every day, when business yields a profit of two hundred per cent. in one year? Is it liktily that the shareholders in such a company will ever pray for peace, either in South Africa or anywhere else? It is war that keeps their machinery going night and day, and these disinterested patriots will chuck up their caps for joy, and shout themselves hoarse, in favour of any mad enterprise that will yield them two hundred percent.

11.-THE NATIONAL DEBT SWINDLE IS A

CHOICE EXAMPLE OF TORY PATRIOTISM.

• You must remember that in the days when the National Debt was first contracted, the working men of this country had no votes, and if they ventured to speak their minds on any political question, they would get run in gaol, and probably transported or hanged for their pains. We owe an enormous debt of gratitude to men like Cobbett and Richardson, the author of the famous "Black Book," who unearthed the secret rascality of the times, and proclaimed it on the housetops as a warn- ing to others. Richardson shows that in the fifty-five years from 1760 t.o 1815 the/Whole of the gold and silver coined in England onlyamounted to £67,427,952, or an average of only £1,225,944 a year. During that same period our reckless Tory rulers ran up a debt of £700,000,000, or an average of £12,727,272 a year. In other words, for every sovereign we coined we ran more than ten sovereigns into debt. How was the thing done? Well, it never was done honestly. We got the debt laid on our shoulders it is true, but ice never got half of the money that the debt represents. We were swindled, bamboozled, and robbed, by the very men who ought to have protected us. Richardson says: "The debt is well known to have been fostered by deception, to have been increased by fraud, and supported by brute force and cruel injustice to the people. It is a gross fraud and a foul cheat and delusion to thrust this debt upon the shoulders of the people without their consent', I take it for granted that you will never take your wife's silver teaspoons to your uncle's till youare hard up. No man ever trades with a money-lender till he is driven there by dire necessity, and then the usurer is master of the situation, and he can dictate his own terms.

The Tory Governments that ran up our National Debt behaved like reckless gamblers. and they were always " stoney broke "and at their wits' end to raise money. There was not a• large supply of gold and silver in circulation, and banknotes were usually very heavily depreciated. So the Barings, and Rothschilds, and Shylocks of those days would pay £6oo'in gold, or £BOO in Bank of England notes, and they would insist on a Government promissory note for £l,OOO, and they got it. Of course the price varied with the necessities of the times. When the nation was more deeply involved in debt and difficulties than common, the money-mongers insisted on harder terms.

Between 1797 and 1819 the Bank of England suspended payment thirteen times, and refused to pay its just debts. Four times in one year that aristocratic pawn- shop was hard up, and was only saved from its creditors by Act of Parliament. In that disastrous year 1797 the Ministers were so hard up that they actually gave away £219 in National Debt securities for every £lOO in depreciated banknotes. So that for every million of these cheap notes, you and I are - saddled with a National Debt of £2,190,000 by that year's deal. Richardson, in his Black Book, gives a long list of transactions of this kind. In 1804, for every £l,OOO in bank-notes you could buy £1,850 in national securities. In 1815, the year of the Battle of Waterloo, the price was £l9lO in scrip for £l,OOO in notes.

We had a great outcry in the country a few years ago when Isaac Gordon, and a few other moneylenders, kind been making one hundred per cent out of their Unlucky victims. But nobody denounces reckless Kings and incompetent Ministers for carrying on such games. We are the biggest hypocrites on God's earth. In the reign of Edward VI., Parliament declared that the chargin,g of interest is a vice most odious and detestable, and contrary to the Word of God, and moneylenders were sent to the pillory and the jail. Cromwell found he could not stamp out usury, so he fixed the legal rate of interest at six per cent, and declared that any man charging more should forfeit the whole of the debt. But in spite of these Acts of Parliament, when King William 111. was hard up, the aristocratic land grabbers and titled Shylocks of those days actually got eight per cent for loans to the National Debt, and they never paid more than £6OO in gold for £l,OOO in stock. Were these usurers sent to the pillory and the stocks? Were their debts confiscated? How could you expect it when the men who lent the money and plundered the nation were the men who made the nation's laws, and the cause of all our misfortunes.

111.-THE NATIONAL DEBT WAS !GREATLY IN- CREASED BY STOCK-JOBBING SWINDLES.

When our aristocratic Shylocks first began to lend money to the nation,they raised a row about the security. So the Customs and Excise Duties were made to pay the interest on the debt under an Act of Parliament passed in the reign of William 111. Bat the Rothschilds and Barings of those days could not grow rich fast enough by buying Government securities at half price, and getting eight per cent. on the nominal value. They preferred gambling on the Stock Exchange and rigging the market and cornering the Government, and bleeding the nation whenever it was in a tight place. Between 1793 and 1801, the average price of Govern- ment three per cent. Stock was £6l 17s. 6d. per £lOO of scrip. In other words, you could buy £l,OOO worth of Debt Stock for £6lB 155., in notes, or less than £5OO in gold. But the Stock was in few hands, and the Shylocks had a trades union of their own, so. they said to the Government when it was in difficulties: "We will not renew your bills or lend you any money till you have redeemed your floating debt at current prices." When the Government had agreed to these terms, a loan was negotiated for forty millions. The old Stock had to be redeemed at £6l 170 6d per £lOO, and the new stock was issued at £57 73 6d per £lOO. So there was a loss of £4 10s. to the nation on each £lOO of Stock. The money-mongers made £2,234 500 on that deal, and you and I arp paying interest to-day on money that the na- tion never received. Between 1803 and 1815 the average market price of ' Consols was £62 17s 6d per £lOO, and the Government had a big deal in new Stock. The money-lenders in- sisted that debts to the nominal value of £176,173,240 should be wiped oft at that price before they would lend any more. When the Government had signed the contractthese harpies soldjout at £62 17 sad per cent, and bought back again at £6O is 6d per cent. A very simple sum in arithmetic will show you that there was a loss to the nation of £2 ips. on each £6O 7s. 6d. that Government received on that deal. And there was a profit for the money-lender of £4,404,331 in banknotes. If you add these two deals together you will find that the nation lost and the Shy- locks gained £6,G38,831 in currency. How do you think this loss was recovered? By creating a new debt of £11,000,000, and you and I are paying the principal and interest of that debt to-day. We are indebted to men like William Cobbett and JosephHumelforiunearthing these scandalous transactions and denouncing them in the House of Commons. Mr. Alexander Baring, the money-lender, had a seat in nine

LOCAL DISTRESS.

PUBLIC APPEAL NOT CONSIDERED NECESSARY.

While there is undoubtedly keen distress in the Bewsey district, intorm'ation from reliable sources goes to show that the conditions generally are not such as to call for the opening of a public subscription list. The Mayor and Mayoress have given the matter careful consideration and several meetings of ladies and gentlemen, convened by them, have been held at the Town Hall during the week. The replies received from large employers of labour, in response to a letter issued by the Town Clerk, were submitted, and the general opinion was that the distress was confined to the Bewsey district and that there was no necessity for the Mayor to make a public appeal, or for the appointment of a relief com- mittee. It was therefore decided not to take. any steps in that direction.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OPINIONS OF EMPLOYERS.

I We are able to give extracts from some of the replies of employers to the letter of the Town Clerk. One large firm state that they do not see any neces- sity for the opening of a fund, and instead of appealing for subscriptions the contribution should be in the form of rates which should be spent on productive works which would leave permanent beneflt,and thereby spread the cost on close-fisted ratepayers as well as on the charitably-disposed. Another firm state that they are not in favour of a distress fund, that the distress does not appear to be so acute as reported, and that men belonging to unions aresupported by those unions, and therefore should not be classified among unemployed. "We are somewhat surprised to hear there is distress existing over and above the normal state of affairs," is the statement in a third letter. Another firm write :—"Judging from our own men,we should say there is no necessity to open a fund. Towards the end of last year things were rather bad, but it seems to us that that is now over."

In another firm's letter, it is stated :—" So far as our men are concerned no necessity, in our opinion, as yet exists for the opening of a relief fund. Wages consider- ably above the average earned during the last six months."

The information furnished by other employers is that "Our men are employed five days per week. Even at this we cannot get them to attend regularly to work." Replies received from school masters go to showthat the children generally are well fed,but many are poorly clad.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
THEIRONWORKERS' FUND.

On Monday a depUtation of ironworkers from the Bewsey district was received by the Mayor at the Town Hall. The men stated that they had col- lected £l5 to alleviate distress in their trade, but before distributing the money they were anxious to know whether a general fund would be opened by the Mayor. In that case they would be prepared to hand the £l5 over to the fund. The Mayor informed the deputation that it was not his intention to open a fund, as the distress generally was no; so acute as to warrant such a course. At the same time he recognized that there was aconsiderable number of unemployed in the Bewsey district. He left the disposal of the £l5 entirely in the hands of the deputation. The iron workers told his Worship that they proposed to collect funds and distribute them through their own committee. The Mayor said he had no objection to that course, but it must be distinctly understood that any appeal made was unofficial and not in any way connected with a central fund. He handed them a cheque for £5.

===== 0002601_19040319_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
RELIEVING DISTRESS IN THE BEWSEY DISTRICT.

The Rev. W. Bracecamp and several ladies and gentlemen have been making efforts to relieve the dis- tress in the Bewsey district. On Saturday and Monday there were distributions of soup and bread to a large number of poor people at All Saints' Mission, but owing to lack of funds the soup kitchen has not been opened again during the week. Deserving cases are, however, being privately relieved. Mr. Bracecamp has received several donations including one of ten guineas from. Lady Greenall. donations, sum will be expended by a com- mittee in the issue of tickets for provisions, milk &c. Councillor Rogers handed over 10 3s. 9d., which he had collected, and it has already been devoted to the provision of soup and other food. The Corporation have provided work for about fifty of the unemployed.
