THE MUSICAL SOCIETY'S CONCERT.

Gounod's "Redemption."

At the Warrington Musical Society's concert on Wed- nesday next Gounod's great work "The Redemption" will be given for the second time in Warrington under conditions which deserve, if they should not happen to command,success. As will be seen from the announcemer t in our advertising columns, a strong array of principal vocalists have been engaged, and there will be a con- siderably augmented orchestra to deal with the com- poser's somewhat imposing instrumental score. In this connection we may remind our readers that some novel features will be noticeable in both the chorus and the orchestra interpreting "The Redemption." Dealing in that replete manner which an author often does in the case of a favourite work,Gounod introduces with strik- ing effect a complement of six extra trumpets above and beyond the usual contingent of brass instruments, and fine use is also made of the harp and the organ. These extra trumpeters, it is interesting to note, will be taken from our local band of the Ist V.B. Prince of Wales Regiment. The choral novelty consists in the employ- ment by Gounod of what he calls "The Celestial Choir," which for this occasion will consist of a number of boys from People's College, whose fresh young voices may be expected to assert themselves conspicuously above those of the more sonorous chorus.

In "The Redemption" we have undoubtedly the consummation of a lifetime devoted to the creation of some of the finest tone pictures of our day, there having been nothing written which can be considered as yititled to rank with it, since Mendelssohn's " Elijaff ' was given to the world. Certain it is that the completion of the work was felt by Gounod himself to mark the accomplishment of a great ideal, and equally true is it that he regarded the score as embodying the best work of which be was capable. The oratorio,—or sacred trilogy, as it is more exactly termed,—is planned on a most imposing scale,'and deals with the three great facts on which depend the exist- ence of the Christian Church,—the Passion and Death of the Saviour—His glorious life on earth from His Resur- rection to His Ascension—and thespread of Christianity in the world through the mission of the Apostles ; the three parts being preceded bra prologue on the Crea- tion, the Fall of our first parents, and the promise of a Redeemer. The music is essentially modern in conception and treatment, and in the course of its performance will be found many of those passages whose rare melodic beauty 'allied with masses of tone colouring—often almost sensuous in its richness and depth—we at once recognise as strikingly characteristic of the composer.

A .Press Association telegram from Chumbi on Thursday says :—The Mounted Infantry with the Thibet Mission had at Linginathan this morning a most exciting chase after a doe of the Shan Sikkim species of deer,which came down on the plain. The animal finally fell, exhausted, and was clipturf.)d alive. It is probably the rarest zoological specimen in the world.

PASSIVE RESISTANCE.

ELEVEN CASES AT WARRINGTON. Rev. J. F. Matthews "In Forma Pauper's."

Warrington's passive resisters, numbering eleven, ap- peared before the magistrates on Thursday. Not much public interest was taken in the proceedings,but several Free Church ministers occupied seats in the gallery. The magistrates on the bench were Messrs. Charles White (presiding), J. Rymer Young, and E. J. Guest. Mr. W H. Woodcock (borough collector) appeared on behalf of the overseers.

The following is a list of the resisters together with the amount of the Education rate unpaid :—Rev. J. F. Matthews, 9, Wash Lane, Latchford, ss; Rev. W. J. Roberts, 30, Parker street, 5s Gd ; EvanE. Hugheo, 10, Crosfield street, is Gd ; Thomas Dale,76, Priestley street is 3d; William Mayer, 33, Froghall Lane, Gs 10d; Edward Howard, 192, Padgata Lane, 3s 2d; Norman Balmford, 15, Museum street, 9s 7d; Andrew Scriven, 4, Suez street, Gs ; Emma J. Fieldhouse, Bridge street, lOs 7d ; William Clatworthy, 21, Lovely-lane, Gs 8d ; William Gallop, 69, Buttermarket street, 10s. Before the cases were taken the Rev. J. F. Matthews said he would like to apply to the bench for a postpone- ment of the proceedings against the passive resisters until the second week in September. The reason for his request was a double one. It was not improbable that between now and September legislation might be introduced.into the House of Commons which would remove the sectarian grievance under which they laboured, and if that were removed they would be able to pay the first half year's rate as well as the next half year's. His second reason was that Mr. French, the presiding magistrate of the Barnstaple bench, had, in the possibility of such a contingency arising, felt it his duty to do the same. He did not suggest that was any precedent for their worships, but he suggested that it might be interesting to them to know that if they did that, and the overseers would consent to it— Mr. W. H. Woodcock: We shall not consent, Mr. Matthews.

The Rev. J. F. Matthews: I think it would help matters considerably. The Chairman: lam afraid we cannot let it go as long as that. The Rev. J. F. Matthews; It is not because we want to escape any penalty. So far as I am concerned, I stand here this morning, in forma pauperis. You can do as you like.

The Chairman: Mr. Woodcock wants his money. Mr. Woodcock: Yes. I want my money. (Laugh- ter.) There is no season why they should not pay. The Rev. J. F. Matthews: Ido not propose to ad- dress you at any length. The Chairman: We cannot allow you to address us at any length. The Rev. J. F. Matthews: We wish to enter our pro- test very courteously and emphatically. We do not feel any legal or moral obligation to pay a rate for sectarian purposes under the present Education Act. The Chairman : Having said that, will you be satis- fied without a distress warrant being issued? The Rev. J. F. Matthews : lam afraid it will not be very much use in my case. All the goods I had are my wife's now.

Mr. Woodcock: I ask for a distress warrant. The Chairman (to the Rev. J. F. Matthews) : I am very sorry you adopt this course? Why can't you pay? The Rev. J. F. Matthews: I have no alternative. I cannot possibly help it. Being what I am, I should never dream of paying a sectarian rate for any sectarian purpose whatever.

Mr. J. R. Young: You pay it in the long run. Why not content yourself with a protest up to this point? Why go a step further and require a distress warrant? The Rev. J. F. Matthews: I fail to see it would ben protest without a distress warrant.

- Mr. Young: You are-goinii-OPay. Your goods will be seized.

The Chairman: Suppose you pay now and protest in September. We don't want to issue distress warrants but we have no choice.

The Rev. J. F. Matthews: I quite admit you have no choice. I don't know whether you will over-ride the legal gift of my goods to my wife. The Chief Constable: Mr. Matthews thinks he will tempt us to arrest him, but he really won't. If he does I shall have him as my guest. Mr. E. J. Guest (to the Rev. J. F. Matthews) : You have my full sympathy in this matter. lam very sorry we are obliged to enforce the law, but you have my full sympathy in the action you are taking. The distress warrant was issued. Mr. Evan E. Hughes was the next resister. The Chairman: Are you one of the gentlemen called • passive resisters? Mr. Hughes: Yes. The Chairman : Won't you pay the 7s. 6cl. quietly? Mr. Hughes: No, I object. Mr. T. Dale, who owed Is. 3d., was asked by the Chairman if he would have the sum lent to him.

Mr. Dale: No sir. I object on a matter of principle and conscience.

The Rev. W. J. Roberts said that if he paid the Education rate it meant paying for the teaching of the doctrine of regeneration in baptism. The Clerk: Have you a legal objection? Mr. Roberts: I consider this in a way a legal objec- tion.

The Chairman : Was the summons properly served? Mr. Roberts said he considered the summons was illegal because it summoned him fornotpayingthe Poor rate. He had never refused to pay that rate, but only the Education rate.

The Clerk: The amount for education is included in the Poorjrate. The Chairman: If you have any legal objection we will listen, but we cannot listen to sentimental objec- tions.

Mr. Roberts was again proceeding to read over his objections to the teaching of certain doctrines but The Chairman said the magistrates could not allow that for a moment.. _

.. --- Mr. Roberts : Ido not consider this justice, Mr A. Scriven said he had an objection to the rate, as the superintendent of a Sunday School of over 300 children. Owing to his position he was bound to pro- test against a sectarian rate. Miss Fieldhouse had a similar objection to the other resisters, but in answer to a remark of the Chairman's, denied that it was sentimental. Distress warrants were issued in all the cases. The Clerk: Will you allow all the names to be put on one distress warrant as before? The Chairman: Yes. The Rev. J. F. Matthews : We are extremely sorry that we have to come before you. The Chairman: So are we. The Rev. J. 17: Matthews: I promise you that if YOU will help us to get the Act remedied we won't come here. We are very grateful to the assistant over- seer for his urbanity and kindness. They are proverbial in Warrington.

Visit of the Officers to the Rev. J. F. Matthews' House.

After the Police Court proceedings the officers attended at Mr. Matthe rsi house, Wash-lane, Latchford, to seize sufficient goods to pay for the amount of the Education Rate. Mr. Matthews however informed the officers that none of the furniture or any article in the house belonged to him as he had assigned them to his wife. The officers then coutteously asked to see Mrs. Matthews in order to have the statement corrobor Med. On interviewing Mrs. Matthews she informed the officers that it was as Mr. Matthews stated viz., that all the articles in the house now belonged to her. The officers therefore were obliged to leave the house, without being able to seize any of the goods. The other distraints were duly enforced, and in each case articles were handed over to the police officers. The Rev. J. F. Matthews' case was the only exception. The sale of the goods will take place on Thursday next. An extremecourse is opm to the authorities if they cue to enforce it, with reference to the attitude'of the Rev. J. F. Matthews. It is hardly likely, however, that this step will be tAken. By his action, we are given to understand, Mr. Matthews is likely to be disfranchised.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football. By " Ovalist."

Swinton sweetly succumbed at the second time of asking and Warrington now hold a strong position in Round 2 of the cup-ties. It seems an awfully long time since last Saturday somehow, and the drawn game at Swinton appears to the memory like some half-forgotten dream. This is all attributable to the excitement of waiting for Wednesday's replay, and the exhilaration caused by Warrington's big win. The more recent event has almost wiped out one's recollection of the earlier struggle. Yet it was, in my opinion, the finer game of the two, notwithstand- qng the fact that pointless draws are usually considered uninteresting.

I was bold enough to predict in this column last week that Warrington would get at least a point out of the match at Swinton. And so they did. The game was almost an exact repetition of the league match three weeks before, for the " Wirepullers " had all the game but could not penetrate the Lions' defensive armour. Considering the strain it was sub- jected to one marvels at the Swinton defence holding out so well. Every man in the Warrington back division had a try—and a desperate one too—at breaking through the barrier of Blues, but all to no purpose,and the seven or eight thousand people had to be content with long-drawn • suspense which never culminated in anything tangible. Naturally the 1,500 Warrington supporters left the colliery village well-content and with rosy visions of the replay at "home sweet home."

The Swinton directors were evidently far from satisfied with the performance of their selected fifteen on Saturday, for no less than six changes were made for Wednesday's struggle. When the team was pub- lished there was scarcely any difference of opinion in Warrington on the fact that the team had not been improved, and the sequel set the seal on the folly of the Swinton directors in not leaving well alone. Gartrell and Molyneux were both sadly missed, while the experiment of placing Chorley in the centre was a colossal failure. Warrington made ducks and drakes of the defence this time and Swinton were quite out-classed, as the score of 20 points to nil in Warrington's favour conclusively proves. A sorrier exhibition than that put up by the Blues has not been seen at Wilderspool for a long time.

Wednesday's game commenced with Swinton show- ing a very bold front, and for a time the home sup- porters feared that their champions were going to have an off-day. Both forwards and backs were a long time in getting into stride, and when a raid on the Swinton line produced a penalty goal to Fish there was real satisfaction and relief. There was not going to be another pointless draw anyhow. This was really the turning point in the game. Warring- ton commenced to imyrove, and a smart bit of work close in resulted in Dickenson making an effort worthy of Jim Valentine himself and scoring near the posts, Fish adding a goal with ease. By the way, the renowned" Jim " and Brother Bob were both on the stand puffing at big cigars and watching the downfall of Swinton's pride and glory with sportsmanlike forti- tude. No man has seen his side beaten oftener at Wilderspool than Jim Valentine, and the experience was therefore not at all new or surprising. Probably he expected nothing better. Warrington was ever a burying ground for other people's hopes in clip-ties.

The half-time score was only seven points to nil in Warrington's favour, and as they turned round with the wind the Blues might be said to have had just a sporting chance. That chance disappeared in the remote distance two minutes after resuming. The home men rose to the top of their form straight away, and superb combination was capped by Fish running in a brilliant try though twice tackled. Then it was Warrington first and Swinton nowhere. They quite out-classed the Blues in all movements outside the scrum and Dickenson put on another try from a smart move by Davies. Fish, as usual kicked the goal. Later a bout of passing completely lost the visitors and Isherwood ran in between the posts ; Fish again obliging. This brought Warrington's total to 4 goal, 4 tries, a nice round twenty points. Only once had Swinton looked like scoring, and that was when the burly Preston got away with Williamson in attendance. He failed to pass, however, and the chance was lost.

The contrast between the two teams was most marked on Wednesday, and Warrington were every bit 20 points the better side. Swinton's back play was atrocious and a positive disgrace to the famous Blue jersey. Warrington on the other hand were moving in fine style once they settled down and the backs reproduced the form that carried them into the final tie a couple of seasons ago. The forwards were also good, Thomas, Fairhurst, and Boardman being very prominent. I must also give special praise to the centres, both Isherwood and Dickenson playing in great style, and leading the attack perfectly. The halves were also much smarter in the second half and if they will:only be as quick and resourceful henceforth Warrington will have a bright prospect. Fish was a host in himself and his dash was in direct contrast to his play during the past few weeks. Qup-tie training seems to have put him in grand fettle. Hallam was as safe as ever,and Harris is always reliable. Dixon alone did well among the Swinton backs, and his play at full-back was the ore redeeming feature of a miser- able exhibition on the part of the team.

A word of praise is due to Mr. J. H. Smith who re- fereed most capably and to the satisfaction of both sides in each match. It was due to his good manage- ment that the games were fought in such good spirit and with a refreshing freedom from injury to players considering the keen3ess shown.

Warrington have 'done well out of the cup-ties so far, as they share gates amounting to £l7O and £l6O respectively. The latter sum is more than double what Warrington have ever taken before in a mid- week match, and the crowd of 7,000 was a positive eye-opener.

To-day Warrington have the good fortune to be again at home in the cup-ties withWigan as opponents Given fine weather the gate records should go with a bang, for the coal heavers have a huge following this season. Moreover Warrington are expected to win, notwithstanding the " cocky" air of the large con- tingent of Wiganers present on Wednesday. Doubtless the home forwards will get a putting-up but the backs should be equal to winning the match. When Wigan were here on December sth, Warrington won by 11 points to 6, after a scrambling game in the fog. Two tries on each side were scored by the forwards, but it was no day for back-play. All Warrington hope for to-day is fine weather and a dry turf, and they should pass into Round 3.

Warrington will again have the same fifteen doing duty, viz : Hallam, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson and Harris, threequarters ; Bate and Davies, half backs; Thomas, Boardman, Cook, Naylor Fairhurst, Jolley, Swift, and Harmer forwards.

The draw for the sec)nd round of the cup-competi- tion is as follows :

Leigh v. Hunslet ; Pontefract v. Batley; Runcorn v. Broughton Rangers; Warrington V. Wigan; Hull v. Salford ; Barrow v. Halifax; Elolbeck v Bradford; and Leeds v. Keighley. To be played on the grounds of the first-named to-day (Saturday).

Runcorn accomplished a fine performance by winning their re-play at Wakefield, and thus earning their right to entertain Broughton Rangers in Round 2. Now, I learn with astonishment that the Runcorn directors have bartered away their " birthright" and consented to play the game at Broughton. This is by no means fair to the other clubs iin the Competition for t amounts to the Rangers buying their way into the third round— nothing more nor less. The Northern Union authori- ties ought to have a voice in such arrangements as these, which are not for the good of the game. I can, however,sympathise with the Runcorn officials. They could have had £lOO for going to Wakefield on Satur- day. Instead they played at home from loyalty to their supporters and were rewarded with a £47 gate, which had to be shared of course. And after all they had to go to the cathedral town on Wednesday! They might well succumb to the blandishments of the Broughton charmer, but the action i . s to be con- demned in the interests of fair play. Little credit to the Rangers if they win the cup by these means!

===== 0002601_19040319_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
WIGAN OPINIONS,

A Wigan corresp-o-n—deni-sends me the following with reference to the match :

Previous to last Sat-ti-r-cia-Y- the Wigan committee and supporters did not interest themselves to any great extent in this season's competition or the Northern Union Cup, preferring to think more of their position in the League. But after the success at Huddersfield

enthusiasm has been aroused, and the whole topic of • football conversation is the forthcoming engagement with Warrington. The Wiganers feel that they would have had a better chance of success if meeting Swinton, but it must not be inferred that they make the journey to Warrington devoid of hope. They have won at Wilderspool before and they intend to make a great effort to do the same again. The com- mittee were so satisfied with the performance of the players last week that with the exception of one for- ward they have chosen the same team. This means that the following players will take the field :—Full back, Mason; threequarters, Barr, Leytham, Harris, and Sharrock ; half-backs, Davies and Sedgwick ; forwards, Hilton (captain), Brown, Collier, Topping, Barton, Whittaker, Blears and Halliwell.

The Wiganers are laying great store by their forwards who are at the present time playing better than they have done during the season, and in recent matches have invariably worn down the opposing pack. They are very dangerous in the loose, and yet are sufficiently weighty to hold their own against the sturdiest pack. It will be a great game between the forwards, for there is little doubt the Wiganers will try their hardest to prevent the ball coming out on the Warrington side, knowing full well the capabilities of the home backs.

Wigan's Weakness will be at half where a secoad. teamer in Sedgwick takes Anderson's place. Ander- son is a fine player in many respects,. but he has been dropped on account of a lack of judgment at critical moments. Still the action of the committee in not playing him has. been strongly criticised in town. Sedgwick will probably do little else than feed Davies, who gets away quickly. The three-quarter line is stronger than when Wigan last visited Warrington on the memorable foggy afternoon. Sharrock, who is extremely strong, will keep a sharp look-out on slippery Fish, and on the other wino. Barr will face Harris. Barr is one of the most powerful runners in the Northern Union, and requires a great deal of stopping. He is very speedy. If Harris, of Wigan, is well he is a very capable bustling centre, but unfor- tunately he has been in poor health for a month or so, and was really unfit to play against Huddersfield. Leytham is better on the wing than at centre, though he is a player who can fill most positions creditably. His great forte is snapping chances and playing with ready judgment. On several occasions he has single handed pulled a match out of the fire for Wig an. Although the Wigan backs are so good individually they sometimes play with little cohesion, and it is iii this respect that they fail to score. If they are on their best behaviour on Saturday some pretty play ought to be seen. Mason, the full back, is essentially a player of pluck, fearless in stopping forward rushes. It is a pity his kick is not bigger.

The prevailing opinion is that Warrington will find, their task far greater than against Swinton, and that if the visitors can control the scrimmage they will win.—Alreiady there are indications that a tremendous crowd will accompany the team, and probably two or three thousand Wiganers will fill the special trains.

Last night the Warrington men were reported "lit." There has been no protest re Dickenson. Harris, the Wigan centre, was refused a permit to play at last night's professional committee meeting.

With the Cup-ties in full pwino• one cannot expect much excitement about the Work's' Competition just yet, buc it win, nevertheless, soon be under way. There are 19 entries this season and the draw for the first round includes 11 byes aid 4 games. Full parti- culars may be gleaned from the poster on the wall. The opening game will take place on Friday the 25th, between London and North-Western and the Cable Company.

I understand that the Committee also have under consideration the formation of a sahoolboys' competi- tion. The elementary schools in the town ought t be able to raise a goodly band of enthusiasts for the handling code.

While studying the " Examiner " file of ten years ago I dropped across the report of the match in which Warrington compiled their record score.. This was on the last Saturday of 1893 at Wilderspool, when the locals defeated Rugby by 10 goals 3 tries,(s9 points)to nil. Barber made his debut on this occasion, and opened the scoring with a brilliant try behind the posts. Charley Potts, Jack Massey and Paddy Carey completed the three-quarter line on that memorable afternoon. On the New Year's Day following Warrington went to St. Helens and drew with the Rees, while the "A" team at home, assisted by Barber, Broady, and Cross from the Stockton Heath club made rings round the Recs. juniors. Those were great days!

ELECTION OF POOR LAW GUARDIAN. TO THE ELECTORS OF LATCHFORD WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN,— Having been asked to allow myself to be nominated for the forthcoming election of a Poor Law Guardian for Latchford Ward, I have much pleasure in placing my services at your disposal. I disclaim all political feeling and have received mane promises of support from representatives of all shades of opinion. I am fully aware of the responsible and manifold duties attaching to the office of a Guardian of the Poor. I have for ten years visited at the Warrington Work- house, chiefly in the Infirmary, and have been a mem- ber of the Isirabeizon society since its commencement. I have for two years been Brabazon Secretary and have been greatly pleased to see what a cheering effect the work has had on the spirits of the invrlids, some of whom are condemned to pass the remainder of their days within the Hospital walls. I have ample time at my disposal and, if elec'ed, I Should devote myself energetically to all the duties coming within the sphere of a Guardian of the Poor, es- pecially in connection with the care of the women, the girlt, the children, and the aged, sick and infirm of both sexes, who together comprise the vast majority of those who have to be dealt with under the Poor Law. There are also many cases arising in connection with out relief which Women Guardians are specially qualified to deal with ber assisting women and girls who are willing to work, to obtain empicyment of earl us kinds, and by helping them in other ways to avoid the brand of pauper ism. Experience has proved the desirability of having on every board of guardians a fair proportion of women to deal specially with their own sex and with the chil- dren. and to supervise the innumerable details which are necessary in a large Institution, and which they are more fitted by nature and training to look after than men. When it is considered that there are 163 women and a large number of children to be eared for, it will be obvious to any thoughtful person that there is plenty of work for two or three women on the Board. I am deeply sensible of the necessity for the strictest economy in the management of such a vast organization as the Warrington Guardians have to control. and on this point I should endeavour to safeguard the Rate- payers' interests by the most judicious expenditure of their money. My only desire in seeking election is, that if success- ful, I may be able to devote my time and energies on behalf of those whose misfortune and infirmities make them dependent on the help of others, and in whose interests as well as your own, I earnestly solicit your support. I am, Ladies and Gentl-men, Yours faitt fully. CONSTANCE H. BROADBENT. The Hoilles, Latchford, March 15th, 1904.

never forget that night when you flung yourself out of the carriage to meet those ruffians." Geoff brushed, but not a smile was on his counten- ance. Looking into her face he said,— "Maude, I want you to be my confessor, I have something on my mind." "Yes! dear, of course; what is it? I shall be delighted to listen to you, and impoge upon you heavy penance," she said laughingly. "I am the biggest coward on earth," said Geoff. "And now, even now, if I could only claim you on the ground of being a brave man, I should not be able to do so." Maude smiled and still went on stroking his hair. She thought he;was thus modestly/ underestimating his own brave deed. "But it is true" he Went on" Even the verynight you considered me a braveanan,l was only acting the part of a hrocrite. The men who attacked your carriage, only did so that they might do me a favour." "Did you never suspect that when the coachman re- turned and found no trace of the men,it was all a make- believe ? If it had been a real attack they would have left me stunned in the road,—perhaps dead. Instead of that, we were washing the mud from our clothes at the club, and chuckling over the whole affair." Now it was Maude's turn to look serious—which she did. But her anger soon passed off when she perceived the spirit of the thing, and when she remembered that she was enjoying her honeymoon with the man she loved best on earth. "My dear girl," said Geoff," believe me, the thought of losing you drove me to do it, and knowing I had no courage of my own, I had to assume it." "But, Geoff, who were the others who helped you?" "Ah ! my dear, I dare not tell you that. I was pledged to secrecy or they would not have helped me. I should never have been able to carry it out, however, if Stephen Yates had not managed the whole affair. He has often said since that I pinched his throat• rather more than was necessary. "There !" she said. 'Yon have let it out. But was there no one else concerned in the scheming ?" "Maude, must absolution be granted me only on the condition that lf tell you all ?" "Yes !" said Maud, catching hastily at the suggestion and with a merry twinkle in her eye. "I shall only grant you absolution when you have made a clean breast of your wrong-doing." Well, then," said Geoff," if you must know, Lady I Allday's last ball was arranged specially to give the opportunity for the attack. Will that do ?" Yes," said IVlaud assuming the tone of an aggrieved woman, " that will do until I see Lady .Ailday,' tossing her head as if to threaten future war. "But what a mighty crash up for my ideal ! " "Never mind," said Geoff. " Perhapssome day, after all, I shall be able to show you that lam us brave as you need me to be." 0," There is one thing, though," said Maude. "My mother must not know of it, or she will never forgive you." "Very well," said Geoff, relieved, and many a time afterwards, when Lady Stuart would repeat to her guests the story of her son-in-law's heroism, Maud and Geoff would steal from the room to hide their merriment. Final :—Bramley, seven points; Barrow,seven.—Tnis match was played ao-ain;on Thurday ,at Halifax, when Barrow won handsomery by 14 points to three points. WAKEFIELD TRINITY v. RUNCORN. These teams having played a pointless draw at Run- corn on S atarday, met again on Wednesday at Wakefield Considering the position occupied by Trinity in the Second League table—being the leaders—much importance was attached to the match, owing to the winners of the tie having to meet Broughton Rangers in the next round. The home team had the best of the game in the early stages, but Runcorn improved• towards the interval, when the score was a goal and a try (five paints) each. After ends were changed Run- corn soon asserted themselves, and scored a goal, eventually winning, by two goals and a try (seven points) to one goal and a try (five points). BRIGHOUSE V. PONTEFRACT. Tais match was played at Brighouse on Wednesday. The visitors won the toss. Nicholl started for the home team, and play ruled at the centre, several tight smarming's ensaing, one of the visitors' forwards having to be cautioned. Ward kicked a goal. At half- time Pontefract led by two points to nothing. Daring the remainder of the game play was even, nothing more being scored, and Pontefract won by one goal (two paints) to nothing.

Dear Sir,—Sunday night my big toe pained me, on Monday it was much worse, and in the evening I could scarcely walk. At 6 p.m. I sent for a bottle of your Gout Pills, and took two at once. At 9.30 1 was much relieved, and could eat my supper. I took another before going to bed, and although so hot I got a fair night's rest. I have taken two more pills to-day, and now, at 6.30, lam alright again. This is a record cure. Yours truly, PHILIP BRANS. EADE'S GOUT PILLS Are perfectly safe in their action, eliminate all injurious matter, and are highly restorative. Parliaments,and Cobbett told him to his face that so long as his ancestors were chandlers, and tallow merchants, and traders, they were respectable people. What they were when they bought seats for rotten boroughs, and became loan-mongers and stock-jobbers, and voted for their own interests,he would not like to say. Of course poor Cobbett was fined and imprisoned,and persecuted fcr uttering these wholesome truths, but nobody ever had the hardihood to contradict him. The thieves who lived by plundering the nation did not object to wrong-doing, but they had a mortal aversion to criticism and exposure. I suppose it is no use crying over spilt milk. The National Debt has been created, and it has been laid on your shoulders and mine, and our children's children will have to repay both principal and interest. But what right had a gang of everlasting fools to plunge us into this debt at all? Why should we be struggling to-day with a debt of £768,000,000, which is about £lB per head for every man, woman and child in the United Kingdom? And why should we compel generations yet unborn to pay for the wars of the French Revolution and the Spanish Succession, and for our own folly and wickedness in South Africa? It is only just dawninf, on some of us what terrible truth there is in ShakeiPeare's lines : The evil that men do lives after them; The good is oft interred with their bones. The mystery of it is that history repeats itself. Mr. Gladstone established a sinking fund by which the Debt was automatically reduced, and paid off by instalments. Under that scheme more than £200,000,000 melted away. But the Tories are in power again and they have plunged us into more wars and suspended the sinking fund,and increased our debts. The effect of this suicidal policy is seen in the loss of our national credit. The price of Consols has fallen twenty-five per cent since the Tories plunged us into these mad wars. If I had my way I would hang or shoot every states- man who plunges the nation into war. As soon as ever war broke out the war-makers should be tried for their lives byan international jury of freemen. If they were found guilty of provoking any war that mighthave been honestly avoided, I would give them an hour to make their wills and say their prayers, and then I would exe- cute them., And I would make each generation pay its own war debts. If a man is cursed with a drunken father, who ruins himself, the law does not permit him to ruin his children's children. It is about time we learned to manage our own affairs and leave our neighbours to their own devices, and then wars Shall cease. John Bull has not sense enough to go in when it rains. He gets bitten a score of times by the same dog, and his head is so thick you cannot teach him anything. There is no fool like an old fool.
